Networking Mastery Guide

From "packets go in, packets come out" to reading a network the way you read code — eighteen modules, built for an engineer who thinks in systems.

Who this guide is for

You are a working engineer. You can reason about distributed systems, you read packet captures without flinching at hex, and you have been nodding along in design reviews when someone says "the LB terminates TLS and the edge caches it." This guide turns those nods into real understanding: what actually happens to your bytes between send() and the other side's recv(), and why networks are designed the way they are.

No prior networking study is assumed. Python fluency helps — the runnable examples are Python — but every example is small and explained line by line.

What you will be able to do

By the end you will be able to explain encapsulation without hand-waving, subnet a /16 on paper and check it with a script, read a routing table and predict where a packet goes, trace a DNS resolution and an HTTP request end to end, describe the TLS 1.3 handshake from memory, and troubleshoot "the site is down" by splitting the stack instead of guessing. The interview Q&As at the end of each module are the check: if you can answer them out loud, you know the module.

How to use this guide

Read the modules in order the first time. Each builds on the last — Module 5's subnetting assumes Module 4's addressing, Module 12's TLS assumes Module 10's TCP. Every module follows the same arc: why it matters, the mental model, how it works (with a diagram), the exact structure, a realistic example, misconceptions, interview questions with answers, and things to try on your own machine.

The back matter is a reference shelf: glossary, subnetting cheat sheet, common-ports table, and a troubleshooting flowchart. It is meant to be revisited, not read once.

How to run the examples

Every runnable example was executed on CPython 3.13, and the output shown is the real output. The sandbox where this guide was built has no internet access, so every example runs against localhost or pure computation — nothing needs the real network to learn from.

~/.local/bin/python3.13 --version   # confirm Python 3.13
~/.local/bin/python3.13 example.py  # run a saved example

Commands marked "run on your Mac — your output will differ" need your real machine (and sometimes your real network). They are safe to run; where one isn't purely read-only, the module says so.

Conventions used in this guide

  • RFC citations are real. Every RFC number named (791, 8200, 826, 2131, 9110, 8446, and the rest) is a genuine standards document. Where everyday practice drifts from the RFC, the text says so.
  • Standard vs vendor vs convention. "Standard" means an RFC or IEEE spec. "Vendor" means terminology or behavior specific to one maker's gear (Cisco's "trunk port" is the famous one). "Convention" means what practitioners do that no document mandates. The guide labels which is which.
  • Sample outputs from live commands (ping, dig, traceroute) are annotated illustrations, clearly labeled — your output will differ.
  • Diagrams are hand-drawn schematics, not packet captures. Bit widths and field orders are accurate; they are drawn to teach, not to scale.

The eighteen modules

  1. Think Like a Network Engineer — layers, encapsulation, the end-to-end principle
  2. How Data Actually Moves — bits to frames to packets to segments
  3. Ethernet & Switching — MACs, frames, switches, collision vs broadcast domains
  4. IPv4 + IPv6 — addressing and both headers, side by side
  5. Subnetting — CIDR, VLSM, calculations checked by script
  6. ARP & Neighbor Discovery — resolving addresses on the local link
  7. DHCP & SLAAC — how hosts get configured
  8. VLANs & Layer-2 Reliability — 802.1Q tagging, STP
  9. Routing & ICMP — tables, longest-prefix match, ping and traceroute
  10. TCP & UDP — handshakes, reliability, ports
  11. DNS — resolution, records, caching
  12. HTTP & the Web — messages, methods, status codes, TLS 1.3
  13. CDN & Caching — anycast, edge PoPs, cache headers
  14. Wireless Networking — 802.11, channels, WPA2/WPA3
  15. NAT & Firewalls — address translation, stateful filtering
  16. VPNs & Tunnels — concepts, no config tutorials
  17. Network Security Deep Dive — threat models, segmentation, zero trust, DDoS
  18. Troubleshooting Like a Pro — the layered methodology, one worked scenario

Back matter: glossary, subnetting cheat sheet, common-ports table, troubleshooting flowchart.

Module 01 — Think Like a Network Engineer

Networking Mastery Guide · Module 1 of 18

1. Why it matters

Picture a 2 AM page. Checkout is failing for some customers but not all. The app logs look clean. The database is fine. Somewhere between the browser and the backend, something is dropping or mangling traffic — and nobody can say where.

This is the moment that separates engineers who "know some networking" from engineers who think like network engineers. The difference is not a bigger vocabulary. It is a diagnostic grammar: a way of asking which layer is broken and proving the answer in minutes instead of guessing for hours.

Every production network incident is a layer problem. DNS failing to resolve is not a "network outage" — it is a name-resolution failure at the application layer, and you prove it by bypassing DNS with a raw IP. A connection that resets halfway through a large upload is a transport-layer story, and you prove it with retransmission counters. A host that cannot reach its own gateway is a link-layer story. The layers give you a map of the system and a procedure for searching it: start at the bottom, prove each layer works, move up.

As a Solution Architect, you will also design with layers whether you name them or not. A service mesh sidecar, a TLS-terminating load balancer, a NAT gateway, a Kafka cluster — each of these sits at a specific layer and inherits that layer's constraints. Put a component at the wrong layer and you inherit the wrong failure modes. The layered model is how you reason about where a box belongs, what it can see, and what it cannot fix.

So this module builds the one mental model everything else in this guide hangs on. Get it cold, and the remaining seventeen modules are details.

2. The mental model

A network is too complex to hold in your head all at once. The industry's answer, refined over fifty years, is layering: split the problem into stacked layers, where each layer offers a service to the layer above and uses the service of the layer below. A layer knows nothing about the layers beneath its direct neighbor. It only knows the interface.

Think of international shipping. You hand a parcel to a courier. The courier does not care what is inside. The airline that flies the courier's containers does not care about the courier's routing labels. The ship's engine room does not care about the airline. Each layer handles its own addressing, its own errors, its own handoffs — and each layer's payload is the layer above's entire package, untouched and unexamined.

Networking works the same way. Your web browser hands bytes to TCP. TCP does not know or care whether those bytes become an HTTP request or a database replication stream. IP takes TCP's segment and does not know whether the machine below uses Ethernet, Wi-Fi, or a fiber link. Ethernet takes IP's packet and does not know IP exists — it only sees a payload to deliver to a neighboring MAC address. This deliberate ignorance is the whole trick. It is what lets a 1970s protocol (TCP) run unchanged over a 2020s radio (5G). The layers below changed completely; the interface held.

Two consequences fall out of this that you will use constantly:

Each layer communicates with its peer. Your machine's TCP talks to the server's TCP. Your machine's IP talks to the next router's IP. Physically, bits travel down your stack, across the wire, and up the far stack — but logically, each layer has a conversation with the same layer on the other end. That peer conversation is an illusion maintained by encapsulation, and understanding exactly how the illusion is maintained is the subject of Module 2.

Failures localize to layers. When the peer conversation breaks at one layer, the layers above it break too — they cannot function without the service below. But the layers below keep working fine. So "the network is down" is never the diagnosis. The diagnosis is always "layer N is broken, and here is the test that proves it." The rest of this guide is, in large part, a catalog of those tests.

Why two models exist: OSI 7 vs TCP/IP 5

You will meet two stacks, and interviews love asking why. Here is the honest history.

OSI (Open Systems Interconnection), seven layers, was designed by committee at ISO in the late 1970s and published as ISO/IEC 7498. It was designed before it was built: a beautiful, rigorous reference model created by standardizing first and implementing later. Almost nobody ever built a pure OSI network. The protocols it specified lost the marketplace to TCP/IP. What survived is the vocabulary — "Layer 2 switch," "Layer 3 router," "Layer 7 load balancer." When someone says "that's a layer 2 problem," they are speaking OSI.

TCP/IP, five layers (sometimes described as four), is the opposite story: it was built first and documented later. It grew out of ARPANET research in the 1970s. It won because it worked and was free. Standards caught up later — RFC 1122 (1989) describes it layer by layer as requirements for Internet hosts. Nobody sat down and designed the TCP/IP model as a teaching tool. It is a description of what actually runs the Internet.

So which do you use when?

  • Use TCP/IP's five layers to reason about real systems. It matches what is actually on the wire: Application, Transport, Network (Internet), Link, Physical. When you debug, you debug this stack.
  • Use OSI's seven layers as shared vocabulary. Interviews, vendor docs, and certification material all speak OSI. You need to be fluent, and you need to know the mapping cold: OSI's top three layers (Application, Presentation, Session) all collapse into TCP/IP's single Application layer. Everything else lines up one-to-one.

The honest footnote: RFC 1122 actually describes four layers, folding the physical layer into the link layer. The five-layer version — with Physical split out — is the teaching variant everyone uses because "bits on a medium" deserves its own box. Both framings are fine as long as you know why they differ. If an interviewer says "TCP/IP has four layers," they are citing the RFC. If you say five, you are using the classroom split. Neither of you is wrong.

3. How it works: the five-layer stack

Here is the stack, top to bottom, with the name each layer gives to its unit of data and the protocols you will meet at each layer. Read it slowly — you will reference this diagram for the rest of your career.

Two things in that diagram deserve your attention.

First, the narrow waist at the Network layer. Above IP there is glorious diversity — HTTP, gRPC, Kafka, DNS, thousands of application protocols. Below IP there is also diversity — Ethernet, Wi-Fi, fiber, 5G. But in the middle, everything converges on one protocol: IP. This is the single most important architectural fact about the Internet. It is why a browser from 2024 can talk to a server from 2004 over a radio link from 2022. Innovation happens above and below the waist; the waist itself barely changes. When you design systems, look for your own narrow waists — the one stable contract everything else builds on.

Second, the PDU names: message, segment, packet, frame, bits. These are not interchangeable synonyms, and interviewers notice when you mix them. A segment is TCP's unit. A datagram is UDP's (or IP's, loosely). A packet is the network layer's unit. A frame is the link layer's unit. Using the right word signals which layer you are reasoning about. It is a small thing that marks you as someone who thinks in layers.

Now the OSI mapping, so you can translate fluently between the two vocabularies:

The collapse at the top is the part interviewers probe. "Where does TLS live?" TLS does presentation-layer jobs (encryption, compression) and session-layer jobs (session resumption) — but on the real Internet it lives in the Application layer, sitting between TCP and HTTP. "Where does a load balancer live?" A Layer-4 load balancer makes decisions from TCP headers. A Layer-7 load balancer parses HTTP. Same box, different layer, totally different capabilities — and the OSI vocabulary is how you say which one you mean.

4. The exact structure: what each layer owns

Here is the stack as a contract table. For each layer: the question it answers, the unit it handles, what it adds, and what breaks when it fails. This table is worth memorizing — it is the checklist you will run during incidents.

Layer Answers the question PDU Adds Typical failure looks like
5 · Application What are we saying? Message Application headers, encoding, semantics DNS fails, TLS handshake fails, HTTP 5xx, Kafka consumer lag
4 · Transport Which conversation on this host? Segment (TCP) / Datagram (UDP) Port numbers, sequencing, reliability Connection refused, resets mid-stream, retransmit storms
3 · Network Which host on the Internet? Packet IP addresses, TTL, fragmentation No route to host, TTL expired, asymmetric routing
2 · Link Which neighbor on this wire? Frame MAC addresses, FCS checksum Can't reach gateway, ARP fails, interface errors
1 · Physical Can bits move at all? Bits Encoding, signaling, connectors Link down, CRC errors, flapping, no carrier

Two columns in that table do quiet, important work.

"Answers the question" is the addressing story. Each layer has its own address space, and the addresses do not leak across layers. Ports identify conversations within a host. IP addresses identify hosts across the Internet. MAC addresses identify neighbors on one link. When someone asks "what is the address of this server," the correct reply is "at which layer?" A server has a MAC address, an IP address, and a set of ports — and they answer different questions.

"Adds" previews encapsulation. Each layer wraps the payload it receives from above with its own header (and sometimes a trailer). The unit names exist because each wrapped bundle is a different object: TCP wraps the application message into a segment; IP wraps the segment into a packet; Ethernet wraps the packet into a frame. Module 2 walks this process byte by byte.

One more precision point before we move on. You will sometimes hear "Layer 8" jokes — "the user," "politics," "the firewall admin." Funny, and occasionally useful as a reminder that systems include people. But it is not a layer. Do not cite it in an interview unless you are clearly joking.

5. A realistic example: one curl, five layers

Run curl https://example.com on your Mac. Here is what the stack does, bottom-up in time but top-down in logic. The application layer acts first; the physical layer moves first.

Application. curl wants an HTTP GET for /. But first it needs an IP address, so it asks the resolver — itself an application-layer protocol, DNS — for example.com. The resolver may ask your configured DNS server over UDP port 53. Only when the answer comes back does curl build the HTTP request. Notice: DNS is not "below" HTTP. Both are applications using the transport service.

Transport. curl opens a TCP connection to port 443. TCP performs the three-way handshake (SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK — Module 5 covers this in full), then — because this is HTTPS — TLS negotiates encryption inside the TCP stream. Only then does the HTTP request flow. From TCP's view, the TLS handshake and the HTTP request are just bytes to deliver reliably and in order.

Network. Every TCP segment is wrapped in an IP packet with your Mac's source address and the server's destination address. Your Mac consults its routing table: is the destination on the local network? No. So it addresses the packet to the gateway router and lets the routers figure out the rest. IP makes one promise — best-effort delivery — and keeps it simply.

Link. To reach the gateway, your Mac needs the gateway's MAC address, so it sends an ARP request ("who has this IP?") as a broadcast frame. The gateway answers with its MAC. Your Mac wraps the IP packet in an Ethernet frame addressed to that MAC and hands it to the Wi-Fi or Ethernet driver.

Physical. The driver turns the frame into radio symbols (Wi-Fi) or voltage transitions (Ethernet cable). Bits move.

On the server side the whole thing runs in reverse: physical receives bits, link strips the frame, network strips the packet, transport reassembles the stream, application parses HTTP. Each layer on the server has a peer conversation with the matching layer on your Mac — and each conversation is blissfully unaware of the layers below it.

If this request fails, the layer model tells you where to look. DNS resolution failing? Application layer — test with the raw IP to confirm. TCP handshake never completing? Transport — check firewalls and listen queues. "No route to host"? Network — check the routing table. Can't even ARP the gateway? Link — check the interface. No carrier on the port? Physical — check the cable. Bottom-up, one layer at a time. That procedure will serve you for the rest of your career.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"OSI is how the Internet works." No. The Internet runs on TCP/IP. OSI is a reference model that was never widely implemented — but its vocabulary ("L2," "L7") is everywhere. Know both, and know which one describes reality.

"TCP/IP has exactly N layers." RFC 1122 describes four; the classroom split gives five. Arguing the number misses the point. What matters is the ordering and the contracts between layers.

"Encapsulation means encryption." It does not. Encapsulation means wrapping — each layer adds its header around the payload from above. The payload is still readable by anyone on the path unless a layer also encrypts it (TLS at the application layer, IPsec at the network layer, WPA3 at the link layer). Wrapping is not hiding.

"Each layer is a separate piece of software." On a real machine the boundaries blur. Your OS kernel implements TCP, IP, and ARP together. A NIC handles parts of the link and physical layers in hardware. The layers are a model of responsibilities, not a blueprint of code modules. The model holds even when one program does three layers' jobs.

"The physical layer is just cables." It is encoding, signaling, modulation, connectors, and media — the entire problem of turning bits into physics. Wi-Fi's physical layer (OFDM modulation, channel bonding, MIMO) is enormously sophisticated. Never dismiss L1. Some of the nastiest outages — a dirty fiber connector, a marginal cable — live here.

"Higher layers are more important." Reliability flows upward, not importance. When L2 breaks, everything above it breaks. Senior engineers debug bottom-up for exactly this reason: prove the physical link, then the neighbor reachability, then routing, then transport, then the application. Skipping layers is how you spend four hours debugging DNS when the cable was unplugged.

"A firewall is a Layer 3 device." A packet-filtering firewall looks at IP headers (L3) and ports (L4). A "next-gen" firewall parses application protocols (L7). A firewall is not at a layer — it inspects at whatever layers its rules need. Always ask which headers a middlebox reads before deciding where it sits.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Why do we still teach the OSI model if the Internet runs on TCP/IP?

Because OSI won the vocabulary war even though it lost the protocol war. Nobody runs OSI protocols, but everyone says "Layer 2 switch," "Layer 3 routing," "Layer 7 load balancing." The seven layers give us a shared coordinate system for locating problems and components. TCP/IP's five layers describe what is actually on the wire. A working engineer is fluent in both and can translate: OSI layers 5–7 (Session, Presentation, Application) all collapse into TCP/IP's single Application layer, and everything else maps one-to-one.

Q2: What does "peer-to-peer communication" mean between layers, and why call it an illusion?

It means each layer behaves as if it has a direct conversation with the same layer on the other machine — your TCP talks to the server's TCP, your IP talks to each router's IP. It is an illusion because physically, data only travels down your stack, across the medium, and up the far stack. No layer-N header ever travels directly to its peer. The illusion is maintained by encapsulation: each layer wraps the layer above's data, and the peer layer unwraps it. The illusion is useful because it lets each layer's protocol be designed and reasoned about independently.

Q3: Where does TLS live in the stack, and why do people argue about it?

In practice, TLS lives in the Application layer — it runs on top of TCP, and its handshake bytes travel inside the TCP stream. The argument comes from OSI thinking: TLS does presentation-layer work (encryption, which OSI assigns to Layer 6) and session-layer work (session resumption, Layer 5). Since the Internet has no separate presentation or session layer, all of that collapses into the application layer. The practical consequence: TLS is end-to-end. Routers, switches, and Layer-4 load balancers cannot see inside it — which is exactly why Layer-7 load balancers must terminate TLS to make routing decisions.

Q4: A load balancer can be "Layer 4" or "Layer 7." What is the real difference, and when does it matter?

A Layer-4 load balancer makes decisions using only transport-layer headers: source and destination IP and port. It is fast and it never looks at your application data — so it works for any TCP protocol and it cannot do URL-based routing, header-based routing, or TLS inspection. A Layer-7 load balancer parses the application protocol (usually HTTP): it can route by path, header, or cookie, and it must terminate TLS to do so. It matters whenever routing needs application semantics — canary by header, path-based routing to different services, or WAF inspection. The cost is that the LB becomes protocol-aware: it has to understand HTTP/2 or gRPC framing, and it becomes a place where application-layer bugs can hide.

Q5: The end-to-end principle says keep the network dumb and put smarts at the edges. Why, and when do we violate it?

The argument — from Saltzer, Reed, and Clark's 1981 paper — is that functions like reliability and security can only be completely implemented end-to-end, because only the endpoints know what "correct" means. A network that tries to guarantee delivery still needs end-to-end retransmission for the cases it misses, so the network-level mechanism is redundant cost. Hence: IP is best-effort, TCP (at the endpoints) provides reliability. We violate the principle constantly, for good reasons: NAT (address scarcity), firewalls (security policy must be enforced even against compromised endpoints), CDNs and caches (performance), and load balancers. The modern view is not "never violate it" but "know what you lose when you do" — every middlebox that touches payloads becomes a failure domain and an ossification point.

8. Try it — on your Mac

Each command below is labeled run on your Mac — your output will differ. They are read-only and safe.

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# See your interfaces and their link-layer (MAC) addresses: L2 identity
ifconfig | grep -E "^(en|lo)|ether "

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# See your routing table: which L3 gateway handles which destination
netstat -rn | head -20

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# See your DNS resolver configuration: an L5 service your apps depend on
scutil --dns | grep -A2 "resolver #1"

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Watch the layering happen: DNS (L5) first, then TCP (L4) to port 443
# Needs internet. -v shows the handshake; * lines are curl's commentary.
curl -v -o /dev/null https://example.com 2>&1 | grep -E "^* |^> " | head -25

The curl -v output is worth reading slowly. Lines starting with * show DNS resolution, the TCP connect, and the TLS handshake — three application/transport-layer conversations. Lines starting with > show the HTTP request. What you do not see — IP addresses being routed, Ethernet frames, ARP — is everything below L4 doing its job invisibly. That invisibility is the point of layering.

9. Code: encapsulation as nested envelopes

The single idea of this module — each layer wraps the payload from above — fits in thirty lines. This script models each layer as a function that prepends a small header to a payload, then unwraps the whole stack on the receiving side. It runs anywhere Python runs.

code/m01/encapsulation.py:

application message : 15 bytes
on the wire (frame) : 51 bytes
overhead added      : 36 bytes
wire bytes          : b'ETH|IPv4|10.0.0.1>TCP|8080>HTTP/1.1 GET /index.html'

link layer saw      : b'ETH|'
network layer saw   : b'IPv4|10.0.0.1>'
transport layer saw : b'TCP|8080>'
application got     : b'HTTP/1.1 GET /index.html'

Each layer only reads its own header. The payload passes through
every layer below its own untouched -- that is the peer illusion.

Notice the wire bytes: headers nest outside-in in the order the layers added them, and the original message sits intact at the center. Real stacks do exactly this with real binary headers — Module 2 packs one with struct.

Next: Module 2 — How Data Actually Moves. Encapsulation, byte by byte: what each header carries, what changes at every hop, and what stays untouched end to end.

Module 02 — How Data Actually Moves

Networking Mastery Guide · Module 2 of 18

1. Why it matters

Module 1 gave you the map: five layers, each wrapping the payload from above. This module walks the territory. You will see exactly what gets added at each layer, and how many bytes it costs. And — the part that surprises most people — what changes as a packet crosses each router, and what stays untouched from sender to receiver.

This matters because "the packet" is not one thing. The frame your laptop transmits is not the frame the server receives. The MAC addresses are rewritten at every hop. The TTL ticks down. But the TCP segment inside — the ports, the sequence numbers, your HTTP request — arrives byte-identical. Knowing which bytes are hop-local and which are end-to-end is how you read a packet capture without drowning in it. It is also how you reason about what NAT, load balancers, and proxies are actually doing: each one is defined by which bytes it rewrites.

2. The mental model

Think of a postal system with a strict rule: each sorting office may only open the outermost envelope.

You write a letter (the application message) and put it in an envelope addressed with apartment numbers (ports). That goes inside a larger envelope addressed with street addresses (IP addresses). That goes inside a courier pouch addressed to the next sorting office (MAC addresses). Each office opens only its own envelope, reads its own address, puts the inner envelope into a new outer envelope for the next leg, and sends it on. Nobody below the street-address office ever sees the apartment number. Nobody above it ever sees the courier pouch.

Three ideas to hold onto:

Wrapping is adding, not transforming. Each layer prepends a header (and the link layer appends a trailer). The payload passes through untouched. This is why the transport layer's reliability guarantees survive any number of hops: the bytes TCP checksummed at the sender are the bytes TCP verifies at the receiver.

Headers are fixed-format binary, not text. HTTP headers are human-readable text. Everything below the application layer is packed binary: fields at exact bit offsets, multi-byte numbers in network byte order (big-endian). A 20-byte TCP header is twenty bytes on every machine on Earth. Module 4 will dissect the IPv4 header field by field; the struct demo at the end of this module shows how packing works.

Each hop re-wraps the link layer. This is the key motion of the whole Internet. A router receives a frame, strips the link header, looks at the destination IP, decides the next hop, decrements the TTL, and wraps the packet in a brand-new frame for the next link. The packet (L3) persists across hops. The frame (L2) is single-hop. Hold that distinction and half of networking clicks into place.

3. How it works: the encapsulation walk

Here is one HTTP request descending the sender's stack. Each row is what the data looks like after that layer has wrapped it. Widths are illustrative, not to scale — but the byte counts are real minimums.

A few things to notice in that walk.

The link layer is the only one with a trailer. Ethernet appends a 4-byte Frame Check Sequence (FCS) after the payload — a CRC-32 over the whole frame. Every other layer puts everything up front. The reason is historical and practical: the FCS is computed as the frame streams out onto the wire, and the receiver verifies it as the frame streams in. Trailers belong to streaming hardware.

The names change because the object changes. After TCP wraps it, the bundle is a segment. After IP wraps that, it is a packet. After Ethernet wraps that, it is a frame. Same bytes inside, different object at each layer — because each layer's header defines a new unit with its own addressing and its own fate.

On the receiving side the walk runs in reverse. The server's NIC checks the FCS and strips the Ethernet header, handing the packet to IP. IP checks the destination address and header checksum, strips its header, hands the segment to TCP. TCP reorders, acknowledges, and hands the byte stream to the application. Each layer verifies only its own header and never looks deeper — the deliberate ignorance from Module 1, now in motion.

What changes at each hop vs what stays

Now the part that actually surprises people. Follow one packet from your laptop to a server across two routers:

This diagram is the single most useful picture in the first four modules. Three rules to take from it:

MAC addresses are hop-local. The destination MAC on hop 1 names Router A — not the server. The server's MAC is meaningless on the laptop's local network, and the laptop's MAC is meaningless past the first router. Every router strips the old frame and builds a new one. If you ever wonder "why does the packet have the router's MAC as destination," this is why: frames deliver to neighbors; packets deliver to destinations.

TTL is the loop fuse. Each router decrements Time To Live by one. If it hits zero, the packet is discarded and an ICMP "time exceeded" message goes back to the sender. Without TTL, a routing loop would circulate a packet forever. With it, the packet dies after at most 255 hops. traceroute exploits this deliberately: it sends packets with TTL 1, 2, 3… and listens to which router complains at each step. You will use this in the Try-it section.

Everything from the IP addresses inward is end-to-end. Source IP, destination IP, TCP ports, sequence numbers, HTTP bytes — no router touches them. (NAT is the famous exception: it rewrites the source IP and port, which is precisely why NAT breaks the end-to-end principle and why protocols have to work around it. Module 7 covers NAT in full.) Because the TCP segment is untouched in transit, TCP's checksum and sequence numbers give a true end-to-end integrity check. That is exactly what the end-to-end principle from Module 1 asks for.

One subtlety worth naming: the IPv4 header checksum is recomputed at every hop, because the TTL field it covers changes. This is a rare case of a network-layer field that is hop-mutable. IPv6 removed the header checksum entirely — the designers decided the link layer's CRC and the transport layer's checksum already cover integrity, so a third checksum was pure cost. That is the kind of design evolution Module 4 will trace.

4. The exact structure: header sizes and the MTU budget

Here are the minimum header sizes you will see on a normal Ethernet network. "Minimum" because IPv4 and TCP headers can grow with options — but in practice they almost never do, and several designs (notably IPv6) forbid the growth entirely.

Header Minimum size What the bytes buy you
Ethernet II 14 B (+ 4 B FCS trailer) 6 B dst MAC + 6 B src MAC + 2 B EtherType
IPv4 20 B Addresses, TTL, protocol, checksum, fragmentation fields
IPv6 40 B Addresses (32 of the 40), flow label, no checksum, no fragmentation
TCP 20 B Ports, sequence/ack numbers, flags, window, checksum
UDP 8 B Ports, length, checksum — that is the whole header

The number that governs everything is the MTU — Maximum Transmission Unit: the largest frame the link layer will carry. For Ethernet it is 1500 bytes of payload (the classic value; jumbo frames at 9000 exist inside data centers but never cross the public Internet). Every layer below must fit inside it:

  • 1500 B Ethernet payload
  • minus 20 B IPv4 header → 1480 B for TCP
  • minus 20 B TCP header → 1460 B MSS (Maximum Segment Size)

That 1460 is the largest chunk of application data TCP will put in one segment on a standard Ethernet path. When both ends handshake, they announce their MSS and use the smaller of the two. Send more than the MTU and something has to fragment — which Module 4 explains is a misery you want to avoid, and Module 5 explains TCP avoids via MSS negotiation.

Notice the asymmetry this creates for small messages. A 200-byte HTTP request pays 58 bytes of header (29% overhead, as the diagram showed). A full 1460-byte segment pays the same 58 bytes (4%). Protocol efficiency is not a property of the header — it is a property of how full you pack the payload. This is one reason bulk transfer is efficient and chatty request-response protocols pay a "small packet tax." It is also why Nagle's algorithm and delayed ACKs exist: to avoid sending a 41-byte frame to carry 1 byte of telnet keystroke.

5. A realistic example: one GET, annotated hop by hop

Let us trace curl http://203.0.113.10/index.html from a laptop. (203.0.113.10 is from the RFC 5737 documentation range — a stand-in for "some server.") The laptop is 192.168.1.50, its gateway Router A is 192.168.1.1, and the path is Laptop → Router A → Router B → Server.

On the laptop, descending the stack. The application builds GET /index.html HTTP/1.1 (~200 bytes with headers). TCP wraps it: source port 49152 (ephemeral, picked by the OS), destination port 80, sequence number 1000. IPv4 wraps that: source 192.168.1.50, destination 203.0.113.10, TTL 64 (the common initial value on Linux and macOS; Windows uses 128), protocol field 6 (meaning "TCP inside"). Ethernet wraps that: source MAC = laptop's NIC, destination MAC = Router A's LAN interface (learned via ARP moments earlier), EtherType 0x0800 (meaning "IPv4 inside").

Hop 1: laptop → Router A. Router A receives the frame, verifies the FCS, and sees its own MAC as the destination — so it unwraps to the IP packet. The destination IP (203.0.113.10) is not local, so Router A consults its routing table and picks the next hop: Router B. It decrements TTL to 63 and recomputes the IPv4 header checksum. Then it wraps the packet in a new frame — source MAC = Router A's outbound interface, destination MAC = Router B's inbound interface.

Hop 2: Router A → Router B → Server. Same dance. Router B unwraps, routes, decrements TTL to 62, re-wraps with its own MAC as source and the server's MAC as destination. The server's NIC sees its own MAC, unwraps to IP, sees its own IP, unwraps to TCP, sees port 80 with a listening socket, and hands the byte stream to the web server. The HTTP response makes the return trip the same way, with every address mirrored.

What a capture shows. If you ran tcpdump on the laptop, you would see frames with the laptop's and Router A's MACs, TTL starting at 64. On a capture taken at the server, the same TCP segment — same ports, same sequence number 1000, same HTTP bytes — arrives in frames carrying Router B's and the server's MACs, with TTL 62. The segment is identical. The frames are not. That is the whole lesson of this module in one observation.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"The packet travels unchanged from source to destination." Only the L3 payload and above does. The frame is rebuilt at every hop, the TTL ticks down, and the IPv4 checksum is recomputed each time. Saying "the packet" when you mean "the frame" (or vice versa) is the most common vocabulary slip in interviews. Use the PDU names from Module 1.

"TTL is a time." The name is a fossil. TTL is a hop count — decremented once per router, not once per second. (The original RFC 791 did define it as seconds, with a minimum decrement of 1 per hop. In practice every implementation treats it as pure hop count. IPv6 renamed the field Hop Limit to end the confusion.)

"Bigger headers are always worse." Header size is a fixed cost per unit, not a tax on throughput. IPv6's 40-byte header looks wasteful next to IPv4's 20 — until you notice it eliminated the per-hop checksum recomputation and router fragmentation, which cost far more in router CPU than 20 bytes cost in bandwidth. Judge headers by what they do, not just their size.

"Encapsulation order is arbitrary." It is not. Headers nest in layer order because each layer's demultiplexing field tells the receiver what is inside. EtherType says "IPv4," "IPv6," or "ARP." The IP protocol field says "TCP" (6), "UDP" (17), or "ICMP" (1). The TCP/UDP destination port says which application socket gets the bytes. Each header is the map to the next one. Strip them out of order and you cannot parse the packet.

"localhost traffic goes through all the layers." Mostly yes, usefully no. Traffic to 127.0.0.1 descends the stack to IP and is looped back internally — it never touches a NIC, never gets a real Ethernet header, never sees the wire. The layering logic still applies (sockets, ports, TCP semantics all work), but there is no link-layer framing and no MTU of 1500 in the usual sense (loopback MTUs are typically 16384 or larger). Do not benchmark network code over localhost and extrapolate to the wire.

"The 5-layer model means 5 headers." Not every layer adds bytes on every packet. Tunneling (VPNs, VXLAN) adds extra IP or Ethernet headers — a packet can carry two IP headers. MPLS inserts a label between L2 and L3. And some layers add nothing: a UDP datagram on the loopback interface may skip L2 framing entirely. Count headers on the wire, not layers in the model.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: A packet arrives at a router. Describe exactly what the router does with it, header by header.

The NIC receives the frame and verifies the Ethernet FCS; a bad FCS means silent discard — the link layer does not notify anyone. The router checks the destination MAC: if it is not the router's own (or broadcast), the frame is dropped. It strips the Ethernet header and looks at the EtherType to find IPv4 or IPv6. At the network layer it checks the destination IP against its own addresses. If the packet is addressed to the router itself, it goes up the local stack. Otherwise the router does a routing-table lookup for the next hop. It decrements TTL — dropping the packet with an ICMP time-exceeded if it hits zero — and recomputes the IPv4 header checksum. Then it wraps the packet in a brand-new frame: source MAC = the outbound interface, destination MAC = the next hop's MAC (via ARP if not cached). The TCP segment and everything above it is never examined or modified.

Q2: Why does the TTL exist, and what would break without it?

TTL is a fuse against routing loops. If routers disagree about the topology — during convergence, or from a misconfiguration — a packet can circulate forever, consuming bandwidth on every lap. TTL guarantees termination: each router decrements it, and at zero the packet is discarded. Without it, one routing loop could saturate links indefinitely. The elegant hack built on top of this: traceroute sends probes with TTL 1, 2, 3…. Each router that expires a probe sends back ICMP time-exceeded, revealing its address. TTL doubles as a topology-discovery tool.

Q3: What is the difference between MTU and MSS, and why do both exist?

MTU is a link-layer property: the largest payload a single frame can carry (1500 bytes on standard Ethernet). MSS is a transport-layer agreement: the largest TCP payload the two endpoints will put in one segment (typically 1460 = 1500 − 20 IP − 20 TCP). Both exist because the layers have different jobs. The link layer defines what fits on the wire. TCP needs to know, before sending, how much application data to hand down so the result fits — otherwise IP must fragment, which is expensive and fragile (Module 4). So during the handshake each side announces its MSS, they take the minimum, and TCP sizes its segments to fit. MSS is TCP adapting to the MTU without IP ever having to fragment.

Q4: NAT rewrites the source IP and port. Which layers' bytes change, and what breaks because of it?

NAT modifies the network layer (source IP) and the transport layer (source port) — it has to rewrite the port because multiple internal hosts share one public IP, and the port is the demultiplexing key for return traffic. It must then recompute both the IP header checksum and the TCP/UDP checksum, since both cover the changed fields. What breaks: anything that embeds addresses in the payload (old FTP active mode, SIP), end-to-end IPsec (AH authenticates the IP header, so NAT invalidates it), and inbound connections (there is no NAT mapping until an outbound packet creates one — the reason peer-to-peer apps need hole punching). NAT is the clearest everyday example of violating the end-to-end principle: a middlebox rewriting end-to-end bytes.

Q5: You capture the same HTTP request at the sender and the receiver. The TCP bytes are identical but the capture looks different. Explain.

The captures differ at the link layer and in TTL. At the sender, frames carry the sender's MAC and the first router's MAC, with TTL at its initial value (64 or 128). At the receiver, frames carry the last router's MAC and the receiver's MAC, with TTL decremented by the hop count. Everything from the IP addresses inward — source/destination IP, ports, sequence numbers, HTTP payload — is byte-identical, because no router rewrites end-to-end bytes. If the TCP bytes differed, that would indicate a middlebox (proxy, NAT, or something adversarial) rewriting the stream.

8. Try it — on your Mac

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Trace the routers between you and a destination. Needs internet.
# Each line is a router that expired a probe: TTL 1, 2, 3... at work.
traceroute -m 8 example.com

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Watch real frames: link-layer view of your own traffic. Needs sudo + internet.
# -e shows MAC addresses, -v decodes headers. Ctrl-C to stop.
sudo tcpdump -e -v -c 5 -i en0 host example.com

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Your interface MTU: the link-layer budget everything must fit inside.
ifconfig en0 | grep mtu

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Localhost-only: no network needed. Shows the loopback path skipping the NIC.
ping -c 3 127.0.0.1

The traceroute output is the TTL mechanism made visible. If you see three asterisks (* * *) on a hop, that router chose not to answer — common with firewalled infrastructure. The path still continued past it. Missing answers are normal; they are not failures.

9. Code: packing a binary header with struct

Below the application layer, headers are packed binary — fields at exact offsets, multi-byte numbers in big-endian "network byte order." Python's struct module is how you build and dissect them. This demo packs a toy 12-byte transport header and parses it back.

code/m02/pack_header.py — output below is pasted from a real run:

header format : !HHII -> 12 bytes
wire bytes    : c0 00 00 50 00 00 03 e8 00 00 00 0f 47 45 54 20 2f 69 6e 64 65 78 2e 68 74 6d 6c
total on wire : 27 bytes (12 header + 15 payload)

parsed back:
  src_port   = 49152
  dst_port   = 80
  seq        = 1000
  length     = 15

port 80 as big-endian (network order): 00 50
port 80 as little-endian (x86 native) : 50 00
Every header on the Internet uses network order so a Mac,
a Linux server, and a router ASIC all read the same bytes.

Read the wire bytes left to right: c0 00 is 49152 in big-endian, 00 50 is port 80, 00 00 03 e8 is sequence 1000, 00 00 00 0f is length 15 — then the ASCII payload 47 45 54... ("GET /index.html"). Every real header decodes the same way: fixed offsets, fixed widths, big-endian. Module 4 dissects the IPv4 header field by field using exactly this technique.

Next: Module 3 — Ethernet & Switching. MAC addresses, the frame layout, how switches learn, and why a loop at Layer 2 melts a network in seconds.

Module 03 — Ethernet & Switching

Networking Mastery Guide · Module 3 of 18

1. Why it matters

Everything above the link layer assumes the link layer works. IP assumes it can hand a packet to a neighbor. TCP assumes segments arrive uncorrupted. When the link layer misbehaves, the symptoms show up everywhere else — as retransmissions, as "no route to host," as a gateway that answers ARP sometimes and not others.

Ethernet is also where most engineers' mental models are weakest, because it is invisible. You never type a MAC address. DHCP hides the addressing, switches hide the forwarding, and Wi-Fi hides the medium. But the moment you debug "host A can ping the gateway but not host B," or "the network melts every time someone plugs in a cable," you are debugging Ethernet and switching. This module makes the invisible visible: what a MAC address really is, what a frame looks like on the wire, and how a switch decides where each frame goes.

There is a second reason this module matters for a backend engineer. Your containers, your Kubernetes pods, your cloud VPCs — all of them are Ethernet networks under the hood. A veth pair is a virtual Ethernet cable. A Linux bridge is a software switch that learns MAC addresses exactly the way this module describes. VXLAN wraps Ethernet frames inside UDP so they can cross an IP network. When you understand physical switching, virtual networking stops being magic.

2. The mental model

A local network is a neighborhood. Every device has two names. The MAC address is the semi-permanent name of the network interface — burned in by the vendor, overridable by software. The IP address is the routable name of the host, and it changes when the device moves networks. The MAC answers "which interface on this wire." The IP answers "which host on the Internet." Ethernet delivers frames between MACs on one link. IP delivers packets between IPs across links. ARP is the directory that maps one to the other on a local network.

Now, the medium. The original Ethernet (1970s–80s) was literally a shared cable: one wire, many stations, and only one could talk at a time. If two talked at once, the signals collided and both had to retry. The set of stations that can collide with each other is a collision domain. The set of stations that receive each other's broadcasts is a broadcast domain. On the original shared cable, every station was in one big collision domain and one broadcast domain.

A hub is just an electrical repeater: it takes bits arriving on one port and repeats them out of every other port. It does not read addresses. So a hub preserves the shared-medium behavior — all ports are one collision domain.

A switch is the device that broke the shared medium apart. It reads the destination MAC of each frame, looks it up in a table it learns by watching traffic, and forwards the frame out of only the right port. Each port becomes its own collision domain. The broadcast domain stays whole — broadcasts and unknown destinations still go everywhere — because the switch has no way to route them more precisely.

That one paragraph is 80% of what interviews ask about switching. The rest is detail: how the table is learned, what happens when the destination is unknown, and why loops are fatal.

3. How it works: hubs, switches, and the two domains

The hub panel shows why hubs died. When A sends to B, the hub repeats the frame to C as well — C's NIC has to receive and discard it. Worse, if C transmits at the same moment, the signals collide and both frames are destroyed. The old CSMA/CD protocol (Carrier Sense, Multiple Access with Collision Detection) managed this with "listen before talking, back off randomly after a collision" — clever for 1983, hopeless past 100 Mbps. Modern networks are switched and full-duplex: each port is its own collision domain of exactly two stations (the host and the switch port), and with full-duplex there are effectively no collisions at all. CSMA/CD is a museum piece — but interviewers still ask what it was, because it explains why the collision domain concept exists.

The switch panel shows the modern reality. The switch has learned that B's MAC lives on a particular port, so A's frame goes only there. C never sees it. A and C can now transmit simultaneously without interfering — the switch buffers and forwards each independently. Aggregate throughput multiplies.

How the switch learns: the CAM table

The switch's forwarding table is called the CAM table (Content-Addressable Memory — named for the fast lookup hardware, though modern switches often implement it in other silicon). Some vendors call it the MAC address table. Same thing.

Learning is beautifully simple, and it happens from the source address of every frame:

  1. A frame arrives on port 3 with source MAC 3c:22:fb:8a:1d:44.
  2. The switch records: "3c:22:fb:8a:1d:44 lives on port 3." It does not matter who the frame was for. The source address is trusted as a statement of location.
  3. The switch looks at the destination MAC. If it is in the table, the frame is forwarded out of only that port. If it is not — or if it is broadcast/multicast — the frame is flooded: sent out of every port except the one it arrived on.

That is the entire algorithm. Entries age out after a few minutes (typically 300 seconds) so stale locations disappear when devices move. The table has finite size — thousands to hundreds of thousands of entries, depending on the switch. That limit is why MAC flooding attacks work. Stuff the table with fake source MACs until it overflows, and the switch degrades to hub behavior, flooding everything. A real attack with a real name: CAM table overflow.

Note what the switch never does: it never modifies the frame. No TTL decrement (that is IP's job), no checksum recomputation, no address rewriting. A switch is transparent to everything above the link layer — which is why you can put a switch anywhere in a path and TCP never notices.

Why loops are fatal at Layer 2

Here is the nightmare scenario, and it happens in real offices with real frequency: someone plugs both ends of a patch cable into the same switch, or connects two switches with two cables for "redundancy."

At Layer 3, a loop is an annoyance — TTL kills the circling packet after 255 hops. At Layer 2, there is no TTL. Ethernet frames have no hop count, no loop protection, nothing. So a broadcast frame in a looped topology circulates forever, and worse — every switch that sees it floods it out of every port, multiplying it. One broadcast becomes two, two become four. Within seconds the loop saturates every link with endlessly circulating copies. The network melts. Engineers call it a broadcast storm, and the only fix is to physically break the loop.

This is why the Spanning Tree Protocol (STP, IEEE 802.1D) exists: switches exchange messages (BPDUs) to discover the topology, elect a root, and logically block redundant ports — keeping them as standby. If the active path fails, a blocked port unblocks. The VLAN/STP module later in this guide covers the protocol mechanics. That includes the 30–50 second convergence pain of classic STP, its modern replacement RSTP, and — at larger scale — routed fabrics that avoid L2 loops by design. For now, carry this one fact: redundancy at Layer 2 requires a loop-prevention protocol; at Layer 3 it does not, because TTL exists.

4. The exact structure: the Ethernet II frame

Ethernet II (a.k.a. DIX Ethernet, after DEC/Intel/Xerox) is the frame format that won. It is what "Ethernet frame" means today. The older IEEE 802.3 variant — with its length field and LLC header — is something you will essentially never see on a modern network.

Two fields do the quiet heavy lifting, so they get the accent color:

EtherType is how the receiver knows what it just got. An Ethernet payload could be IPv4, IPv6, ARP, or a VLAN-tagged frame. The two bytes after the source MAC say which. This is the same demultiplexing pattern from Module 2 — every header carries the key to the next one. Common values to recognize: 0x0800 IPv4, 0x86DD IPv6, 0x0806 ARP, 0x8100 VLAN-tagged (802.1Q, which inserts 4 bytes after the source MAC — the VLAN module covers this).

FCS is the link layer's integrity check: CRC-32 computed over everything from the destination MAC through the end of the payload. The sending NIC computes it in hardware as the frame streams out. The receiving NIC recomputes it as the frame streams in. If it mismatches, the frame is dropped silently — Ethernet has no negative acknowledgment. Corruption becomes loss, and loss is someone else's problem (TCP's, usually). This "detect, don't correct" philosophy runs through the whole stack: the network reports errors by dropping, and the endpoints recover.

The 64-byte minimum is a fossil with teeth. It dates to the CSMA/CD era: a frame had to be long enough that a collision could be detected before transmission finished. The minimum survived into the switched full-duplex era because changing it would break hardware everywhere. Practical consequence: tiny payloads get padded. A 28-byte ICMP echo request becomes a 64-byte frame. At high packet rates, this padding is real overhead — it is one reason packet-per-second (pps) is often the binding constraint on routers and firewalls, not bits per second.

5. A realistic example: the first frame between two hosts

Host A (3c:22:fb:8a:1d:44) wants to ping Host B (192.168.1.20, MAC a4:5e:60:11:22:33). Both are plugged into the same switch, which has just been powered on — its CAM table is empty. Here is what actually happens, step by step.

Step 0: ARP — learning the destination MAC. A knows B's IP but not its MAC. So A builds an ARP request: "who has 192.168.1.20?" This is a broadcast frame — destination MAC ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff — with EtherType 0x0806. The switch receives it on port 1. Two things happen: the switch learns "A's MAC lives on port 1" (from the source address), and because the destination is broadcast, it floods the frame out of every other port. B receives it, recognizes its own IP, and replies with a unicast ARP reply: "192.168.1.20 is at a4:5e:60:11:22:33." The switch learns "B's MAC lives on port 2" and forwards the reply only to port 1. A caches the mapping. ARP is a whole protocol worth its own study (Module 6), but notice its shape: it is the directory lookup that makes everything below possible.

Step 1: the first ICMP echo. A builds the ping packet and wraps it in a frame: source MAC = A's, destination MAC = B's, EtherType 0x0800. The switch looks up B's MAC — it is in the table now (learned from the ARP reply) — and forwards the frame out of port 2 only. One frame, one port, no waste.

The counterfactual. Suppose the switch had not seen B's ARP reply (B was silent, or the entry aged out). Then the destination MAC is unknown unicast, and the switch floods the echo request out of every port except port 1 — exactly like a broadcast. B answers, the switch learns B's location from the reply's source address, and subsequent frames go direct. Unknown-unicast flooding is the graceful fallback that makes learning switches work without any configuration.

The address itself: anatomy of a MAC

A MAC address is 48 bits, usually written as six hex octets: 3c:22:fb:8a:1d:44. The structure carries real information:

  • OUI — the first 24 bits (3c:22:fb). The Organizationally Unique Identifier, assigned by the IEEE to a vendor (that one is Apple's). The remaining 24 bits are the vendor's serial number for the interface. "Globally unique" holds only if vendors assign them honestly — most do, but the address is trivially spoofable in software, so treat uniqueness as convention, not guarantee.
  • Bit 0 of the first octet — unicast vs multicast. If the least significant bit is 1, the address names a group, not a station. 01:00:5e:xx:xx:xx is IPv4 multicast, 33:33:xx:xx:xx:xx is IPv6 multicast. A switch floods multicast like broadcast unless IGMP/MLD snooping (Module 6) tells it which ports actually want the group.
  • Bit 1 of the first octet — global vs local. If set, the address is locally administered — assigned by software, not the vendor. Virtual machines, containers, and some privacy features (like MAC randomization on phones) use locally administered addresses. This is why "the MAC is burned into the hardware" is only half true: the default comes from hardware, but the OS can override it freely.
  • Broadcast — ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff. All 48 bits set. Every NIC on the LAN receives and processes it. Broadcasts are necessary (ARP, DHCP discovery) and dangerous (storms) — the most expensive single address on the network.

The mac_bits.py demo at the end of this module parses these bits for five real-world MAC addresses.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"Switches route." They do not. A switch forwards by MAC address within one broadcast domain; it never looks at IP addresses, never decrements TTL, never makes a routing decision. A "Layer 3 switch" is really a router with many ports and marketing — the moment it routes, it is doing router work. If a device forwards by MAC, it is switching. If it forwards by IP, it is routing.

"MAC addresses are permanent and globally unique." The burned-in default usually is unique, but the locally-administered bit exists precisely so software can override it. VMs, containers, and phone privacy features do this routinely. And nothing stops two devices from claiming the same MAC — the result is flapping CAM entries and intermittent connectivity, a genuinely confusing failure mode. Treat MACs as conventionally unique identifiers, not cryptographic ones.

"A switch eliminates broadcasts." No — a switch forwards broadcasts (it must; ARP and DHCP depend on it). Switches split collision domains, not broadcast domains. Only routers and VLANs split broadcast domains. This is the single most-tested distinction in networking interviews.

"Full-duplex means no collisions, so CSMA/CD still runs just in case." On a modern full-duplex switched link there is no carrier sensing and no collision detection. The NIC transmits and receives simultaneously on separate pairs. CSMA/CD is disabled. It survives only in half-duplex relics and certification exams.

"Bigger CAM tables are always better." A bigger table holds more MACs, but every entry must be looked up at line rate, and flooding behavior on overflow is a security cliff, not a graceful slope. Data-center switches size tables for their role (tens of thousands for top-of-rack, more for aggregation). The design question is always "what happens at capacity," not just "how much capacity."

"The FCS protects against malicious tampering." CRC-32 detects accidental corruption — bit flips from noise. It is trivially forgeable by anyone who can recompute it, which is anyone who can send frames. Integrity against adversaries comes from cryptographic MACs (as in WPA3 or IPsec), not from the Ethernet FCS. Different "MAC," different universe — do not confuse them in an interview.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Walk me through exactly what a switch does when it receives a frame.

Three steps, in order. First, learn: read the source MAC and record (or refresh) the mapping of that MAC to the ingress port, with a fresh aging timer. Second, decide: look up the destination MAC in the CAM table. If found, forward out of only that port. If not found — or if the destination is broadcast or multicast — flood out of all ports except the ingress port. Third, verify: the NIC hardware checks the FCS before any of this; a corrupted frame is discarded silently and never reaches the learning logic. Note what is absent: no TTL decrement, no checksum recomputation, no modification of any kind. The frame leaves exactly as it arrived.

Q2: What is the difference between a collision domain and a broadcast domain, and which devices split which?

A collision domain is the set of stations whose transmissions can collide with each other — a physical-layer concept from the shared-medium era. A broadcast domain is the set of stations that receive each other's broadcast frames — a link-layer concept. Hubs split neither: all ports share one collision domain and one broadcast domain. Switches split collision domains (one per port) but not broadcast domains. Routers split both — a broadcast never crosses a router. VLANs let one switch behave as multiple broadcast domains. The interview one-liner: "switches break collisions, routers break broadcasts."

Q3: Why is a Layer 2 loop catastrophic while a Layer 3 loop is merely annoying?

Because Ethernet has no TTL. At Layer 3, a looping packet's TTL decrements each hop and it dies after at most 255 circuits — wasteful but self-limiting. At Layer 2, a broadcast frame in a loop circulates forever, and every switch that sees it floods copies out of every port, so the copies multiply exponentially. The result is a broadcast storm that saturates all links within seconds. There is no in-protocol recovery; someone has to physically break the loop or rely on Spanning Tree Protocol, which exists precisely to detect loops and block the redundant ports.

Q4: Host A can ping the gateway but not Host B on the same subnet. Where do you look?

This is a classic layer-by-layer isolation question, and the symptom already narrows it: reaching the gateway proves A's NIC, cable, IP config, and the switch path to the gateway all work. So the fault is specific to the A↔︎B path. Check in order: (1) ARP — does A have B's MAC (arp -a)? A stale or missing ARP entry is the most common cause. (2) The switch's CAM table — is B's MAC learned on the expected port, or flapping between ports (duplicate MAC or a loop)? (3) B itself. Is it actually up? Is its subnet mask correct — a wrong mask makes B think A is remote, so B sends replies to the gateway instead. Is a host firewall dropping the pings? Notice the method: each check tests one layer's contract before moving on.

Q5: If switches learn from source addresses, what stops a host from lying about its source MAC?

Nothing, at the basic switching level — and that is a real attack surface. An attacker can spoof source MACs to poison the CAM table, redirecting traffic meant for someone else. Or the attacker can flood thousands of random source MACs to overflow the table. The switch then degrades to hub-like flooding — the CAM overflow attack — and the attacker can sniff traffic meant for others. Defenses are port-level policies, not part of basic switching. Port security limits how many MACs a port may learn. Dynamic ARP inspection and DHCP snooping cross-check bindings. 802.1X authenticates the device before the port forwards at all. The lesson generalizes: learning from unauthenticated input is convenient and exploitable, at every layer.

8. Try it — on your Mac

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Your MAC (L2 identity) and MTU per interface. No network needed.
ifconfig en0 | grep -E "ether |mtu"

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Your ARP table: the IP-to-MAC directory your machine has learned.
# Entries expire; ping something first to populate it.
arp -a | head -10

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Per-interface packet/error counters: CRC errors and drops show here.
# Needs no network, but counters move only with traffic.
netstat -i | head -8

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Watch ARP in real time: who is asking for whom. Needs sudo.
# Generate traffic in another window: ping -c 2 <gateway-ip>
sudo tcpdump -e -n -c 6 -i en0 arp

The arp -a output is worth a careful look. Each line maps an IP to a MAC with an expiry — that is a tiny learned table, the host-side cousin of the switch's CAM table. Entries marked incomplete mean an ARP request went out and no reply came back: the host is asking "who has this IP?" and hearing silence. That single word, incomplete, is one of the most diagnostic strings in networking.

9. Code: reading the policy bits in a MAC address

Two bits in the first octet decide how a switch treats the address — and the first three octets name the vendor. code/m03/mac_bits.py parses five real-world MACs. Output pasted from a real run:

3c:22:fb:8a:1d:44  -- a normal NIC (Apple OUI 3c:22:fb)
    OUI=3c:22:fb  kind=unicast (one station)  locally_administered=False
01:00:5e:00:00:fb  -- IPv4 multicast (mDNS 224.0.0.251)
    OUI=01:00:5e  kind=multicast (a group of stations)  locally_administered=False
33:33:00:00:00:01  -- IPv6 multicast (all-nodes ff02::1)
    OUI=33:33:00  kind=multicast (a group of stations)  locally_administered=True
ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff  -- broadcast
    OUI=ff:ff:ff  kind=broadcast (every station on the LAN)  locally_administered=True
02:00:5e:10:00:00  -- locally administered (VM / container vNIC)
    OUI=02:00:5e  kind=unicast (one station)  locally_administered=True

Note the last three rows: the IPv6 multicast address, the broadcast address, and the VM address all show locally_administered=True. For the VM address that bit is meaningful — software assigned it. For 33:33:... and ff:ff:... it is structural noise. Multicast OUIs are reserved by the IEEE rather than assigned to vendors. The broadcast address is all-ones by definition. So the local/global bit carries no vendor meaning for either. The bit only speaks about vendor assignment for unicast addresses. Details like this are what "know the address format" actually means.

Next: Module 4 — IPv4 + IPv6. Addressing, CIDR, the IPv4 header field by field, fragmentation, and the 128-bit redesign that fixed IPv4's regrets.

Module 04 — IPv4 + IPv6

Networking Mastery Guide · Module 4 of 18

1. Why it matters

IP is the narrow waist of the Internet — the one protocol everything converges on, from Module 1's diagram. Every routing decision, every firewall rule, every load-balancer target, every Kubernetes Service is ultimately an IP address and a prefix. If you are fuzzy on addressing, you are fuzzy on all of them.

There is also a historical weight to this module. IPv4 (RFC 791, 1981) was designed when the Internet had hundreds of hosts and its designers worried about everything except running out of addresses. IPv6 (RFC 8200, 2017 — obsoleting the original RFC 2460 from 1998) is the redesign that had forty years of hindsight: it kept what worked, deleted what hurt, and made addresses effectively infinite. Understanding what changed and why teaches you more about protocol design than either header alone.

2. The mental model

An IP address is a locator, not an identity. It says where a host is attached to the network topology, the way a street address says where a house is. And like street addresses, IP addresses are hierarchical: the prefix names the neighborhood (the network), the suffix names the house (the host). Routers do not memorize every house on Earth. They memorize neighborhoods — "everything starting with these bits goes that way" — and the hierarchy keeps the global routing table small enough to fit in a router's memory.

That hierarchy is expressed in CIDR notation: 192.168.1.0/24. The /24 is the prefix length — the number of leading bits that name the network. The remaining 8 bits name hosts: 2⁸ = 256 addresses, minus the network and broadcast addresses, leaving 254 usable. Longer prefix, smaller network, more specific route. A router with overlapping routes (10.0.0.0/8 via path A, 10.1.0.0/16 via path B) picks the longest matching prefix — the most specific neighborhood wins. That one rule, longest-prefix match, is the core of IP forwarding.

Two honest caveats before the details. First, "locator, not identity" is the design intent, but practice leaks: DHCP reassigns addresses, NAT rewrites them, and mobile devices roam between them. Anything that treats an IP as a stable identity — allowlists, sessions, rate limiters — is building on a locator and should know it. Second, hierarchy assumes addresses are assigned topologically. When they are not (anycast, or a VM migrated across the world keeping its IP), routing still works, but the mental model of "address = location" bends. The exceptions prove the rule's usefulness.

IPv4 addressing: from classes to CIDR

The original IPv4 design (RFC 791) split the 32-bit address into classes by leading bits: Class A (0xxxxxxx, /8, 16 million hosts), Class B (10xxxxxx, /16, 65k hosts), Class C (110xxxxx, /24, 254 hosts). It was simple and it was wasteful. An organization needing 300 hosts got a Class B and burned 65,000 addresses. By the early 1990s the waste was visibly exhausting the space — the first "address crisis."

CIDR (Classless Inter-Domain Routing, RFC 1519, 1993) killed the classes. Any prefix length became legal: /23, /27, /13. Allocation matched need, and — just as important — providers could aggregate: advertise one /16 instead of 256 separate /24s, shrinking the global routing table. Every address you will ever configure is classless, even if old-timers still say "that's a Class C" when they mean "/24." The classful terms survive only as slang for the old default mask sizes.

The private ranges: RFC 1918

Because 4 billion addresses were not enough, RFC 1918 (1996) reserved three blocks for private use — addresses that are never routed on the public Internet and can be reused inside every organization:

Block CIDR Addresses Typical use
10.0.0.0 – 10.255.255.255 10.0.0.0/8 16,777,216 Large enterprises, cloud VPCs
172.16.0.0 – 172.31.255.255 172.16.0.0/12 1,048,576 Mid-size networks, Docker's default
192.168.0.0 – 192.168.255.255 192.168.0.0/16 65,536 Home routers, small offices

Note the middle block carefully: it is 172.16.0.0/12, covering 172.16.x.x through 172.31.x.x — not "172.168.x.x," a mistake that appears in more whiteboard sessions than anyone admits. Private addresses reach the public Internet only through NAT, which rewrites them to a public address at the border (Module 7). The entire cloud runs on RFC 1918 space behind NAT and load balancers; the public IPv4 addresses are just the front doors.

Two more special ranges worth knowing now: 127.0.0.0/8 is loopback (Module 2), and the RFC 5737 documentation ranges (192.0.2.0/24, 198.51.100.0/24, 203.0.113.0/24) exist so examples like this guide's never collide with real addresses.

3. How it works: the IPv4 header, field by field

Here is the 20-byte fixed header from RFC 791, drawn to bit scale. Every field's width below is exact.

The fields fall into four jobs:

Sizing and parsing. Version (always 4) lets a receiver distinguish this from other protocols. IHL tells the receiver where the header ends and the payload begins — necessary because Options make the header variable-length. Total Length bounds the whole datagram at 65,535 bytes. In practice, almost nothing sends datagrams anywhere near that large; the path MTU is what actually limits size.

Forwarding. TTL is the hop fuse from Module 2. Protocol is the demultiplexing key to the transport layer. The two address fields are the whole routing story — every router decision is a longest-prefix match on the destination address.

Quality of service, relabeled twice. The second byte started life as "Type of Service" in RFC 791 — a priority scheme nobody used. RFC 2474 (1998) redefined 6 bits as DSCP (Differentiated Services Code Point) for traffic classification. RFC 3168 (2001) claimed 2 bits for ECN (Explicit Congestion Notification), letting routers signal congestion by marking packets instead of dropping them. The byte's history is a small lesson: header fields get repurposed when the original idea fails.

The checksum that IPv6 deleted. The 16-bit header checksum covers only the header, and every router recomputes it because TTL changes each hop. That per-hop recomputation was pure cost for a check that the link layer's CRC already performed better. IPv6 dropped it without remorse.

Fragmentation: why it happens, why it is hated

A router receives a 1500-byte packet but the next link's MTU is 1400. It cannot forward the packet whole — so it fragments: splits the payload into pieces, each with its own IP header, and the destination reassembles them. The highlighted row in the diagram is the machinery:

  • Identification (16 b): all fragments of one datagram share an ID, so the receiver can group them.
  • Flags (3 b): bit 0 is reserved; DF (Don't Fragment) forbids fragmentation; MF (More Fragments) marks every fragment except the last.
  • Fragment Offset (13 b): where this fragment's payload sits in the original datagram, in units of 8 bytes.

Reassembly happens only at the final destination — never at intermediate routers. If any fragment is lost, the whole datagram is discarded after a reassembly timeout. Now the reasons fragmentation is hated:

  1. It amplifies loss. Lose one fragment of ten and you lose the entire datagram — but the network still carried the other nine. Effective goodput collapses under even modest loss.
  2. It is a security hole. Overlapping fragments, tiny fragments, and fragment floods have fueled evasion and DoS attacks for decades. Firewalls must reassemble to inspect — or guess. Many networks simply drop fragments at the border.
  3. It breaks the transport layer's assumptions. TCP sets DF and relies on Path MTU Discovery (RFC 1191): send with DF set, and if a router cannot forward without fragmenting, it returns ICMP "fragmentation needed," revealing the bottleneck MTU. The sender then shrinks its segments. When that ICMP is filtered — common behind misconfigured firewalls — you get the classic PMTUD black hole. Large packets vanish while small ones pass. The connection mysteriously stalls on big transfers but works fine for chat.

The industry's verdict: fragmentation is a failed feature. IPv6 moved it to an extension header that only the source may use — routers never fragment. Modern practice is "discover the path MTU, size segments to fit, never fragment." The DF bit, once an optimization, is now effectively mandatory.

4. IPv6: the 128-bit redesign

IPv6's headline feature is address space: 128 bits, or roughly 3.4 × 10³⁸ addresses — enough to give every atom on Earth's surface its own /64, with room to spare. But the deeper story is the redesign. The IPv6 authors had twenty years of IPv4 operational pain to learn from, and the header shows it.

Notation and compression rules

An IPv6 address is 8 groups of 4 hex digits: 2001:0db8:0000:0000:0000:ff00:0042:8329. Writing all 32 hex digits every time would be cruel, so RFC 4291 defines two compression rules:

  1. Drop leading zeros in each group: 0db8 → db8, 0042 → 42.
  2. Replace the longest run of all-zero groups with :: — once per address: 2001:db8::ff00:42:8329.

The "once" matters. :: can appear only once because each use is ambiguous about how many zero groups it stands for — with two of them, you cannot reconstruct the length. 2001:db8::1 is unambiguous (seven groups are written or implied... actually one group written plus ::, so :: must stand for six zero groups). Two :: sequences would leave two unknowns and one equation. Preferred form also lowercases the hex and never compresses a single zero group (write 2001:db8:0:1:1:1:1:1, not 2001:db8::1:1:1:1:1) — a convention from RFC 5952 that keeps addresses canonical for comparison and logging.

Address types

IPv6 abolished broadcast entirely and generalized the rest:

Type Prefix Purpose
Global unicast 2000::/3 Publicly routable addresses — the equivalent of public IPv4
Link-local fe80::/10 Every interface autoconfigures one; valid only on its link; never routed
Unique local (ULA) fc00::/7 Private addressing, RFC 4193 — the IPv6 answer to RFC 1918
Multicast ff00::/8 Replaces broadcast and IPv4 multicast; ff02::1 = all nodes on the link
Anycast (from unicast space) Same address on multiple hosts; routing delivers to the nearest

Three of these deserve a closer look.

Link-local (fe80::/10) is IPv6's quiet superpower. Every IPv6 interface has one, automatically, with no DHCP and no configuration. It makes neighbor discovery, router advertisements, and "talk to your gateway" work out of the box. When you see fe80::1 as a default gateway, that is the router's link-local address — and it works because gateways are always on-link. The catch: link-local addresses are ambiguous across interfaces (every link has an fe80::1), so they need a zone identifier: fe80::1%en0. That %en0 is not part of the address — it tells your OS which link you mean.

Anycast is not a special prefix — it is the same address configured on multiple machines, with routing delivering each packet to the nearest one. DNS roots, CDNs, and cloud load balancers all lean on anycast. The subtlety: "nearest" is per-packet from the routing table's view, so consecutive packets in one TCP connection can theoretically land on different hosts. In practice, stable routing keeps them together — but anycast plus long-lived stateful connections is a combination to design carefully.

Multicast replacing broadcast is a philosophical fix. IPv4 broadcast interrupts every host on the LAN, even the uninterested ones. IPv6 multicast groups (ff02::1 all nodes, ff02::2 all routers) let hosts subscribe to only what they need. Neighbor discovery — IPv6's replacement for ARP — uses solicited-node multicast (ff02::1:ffxx:xxxx), which interrupts only hosts whose address shares the last 24 bits. Less noise, same function.

The header, side by side: what got dropped and why

The story of the redesign, field by field:

Dropped: the header checksum. Per-hop recomputation was pure cost. The Ethernet FCS catches link corruption; TCP/UDP checksums catch end-to-end corruption. A third checksum in the middle added nothing.

Dropped: router fragmentation. Moved to an extension header that only the source may add. A router that receives an oversize packet drops it and sends ICMPv6 "Packet Too Big" — which is Path MTU Discovery made mandatory rather than optional. The protocol no longer offers a feature everyone agreed was a failure.

Dropped: variable-length options. IPv4's IHL and Options made every header parse a variable-length operation in the router fast path. IPv6's header is a fixed 40 bytes — always. Optional features moved to extension headers, chained via the Next Header field: each extension says what follows it, like a linked list. Routers that do not need an extension skip it without parsing it. Fixed cost for the common case, extensibility without disturbing the fast path.

Renamed, honestly: TTL → Hop Limit (it was always a hop count), Protocol → Next Header (it now chains to extensions, not just TCP/UDP), Total Length → Payload Length (it excludes the fixed header now).

Kept and enlarged: the addresses, from 4 bytes to 16. Thirty-two of the forty bytes are addresses. Everything else in the header is 8 bytes. That ratio tells you what the protocol is for.

Added: the Flow Label (20 bits). A new idea: the source can label all packets of a flow with the same value, letting routers identify the flow — for ECMP load balancing, for QoS — without parsing transport headers.
