Whether the Internet widely uses it is still debated (RFC 8200 defines the semantics; deployment varies). It is the one genuinely new forwarding feature, and its lukewarm adoption is itself a lesson about ossification.

5. A realistic example: a dual-stack host talks to the world

Your Mac is almost certainly dual-stack: it has both an IPv4 address (likely RFC 1918, behind NAT) and one or more IPv6 addresses (often a global unicast and a link-local). When you connect to a hostname, the resolver returns both A (IPv4) and AAAA (IPv6) records. Happy Eyeballs (RFC 8305) then races the two connection attempts — usually preferring IPv6, falling back to IPv4 within ~250 ms if IPv6 stalls. You get the better protocol without noticing.

Watch what differs at each layer for an IPv6 connection. The application is identical — HTTP does not care. TCP is identical. At the network layer, several things differ. The header is the fixed 40 bytes. The source might be your global unicast or your link-local (for on-link destinations). There is no checksum to compute. And if the packet is too big for the path, no router will fragment it — you will get an ICMPv6 Packet Too Big, and your stack will shrink its packets. At the link layer, one visible change: no ARP. IPv6 replaces ARP with Neighbor Discovery (ICMPv6-based, Module 6), using solicited-node multicast instead of broadcast. The frame still carries MAC addresses — Ethernet does not care which IP version rides inside; EtherType 0x86DD says "IPv6 in here."

The practical upshot for your work: most cloud VPCs, Kubernetes clusters, and CDNs now run dual-stack or IPv6-first internally. When you write firewall rules, security groups, or network policies, you are writing them twice — once per address family — and the IPv6 rules are the ones people forget. "It works on IPv4 but not IPv6" is the new "it works on my machine."

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"IPv6 is just IPv4 with bigger addresses." The addresses are the least interesting change. The redesign removed the header checksum, router fragmentation, variable-length headers, and broadcast — four features that caused decades of operational pain. IPv6 is what IPv4 would look like if you could delete your regrets.

"We don't need IPv6 because NAT solved the address shortage." NAT conserved addresses at the cost of breaking the end-to-end principle (Module 2). It rewrites addresses and ports in flight — which breaks inbound connections, complicates protocols, and forces applications through hole-punching hacks. NAT was a survival tactic, not a solution. IPv6 restores globally routable addresses — every device can have a public address again, with firewalls (not address translation) providing the security boundary.

"The middle 172.16.0.0 block is 172.16.0.0/16." It is 172.16.0.0/12 — 172.16.0.0 through 172.31.255.255. This is the most commonly misremembered RFC 1918 range, and it matters: Docker's default bridge uses 172.17.0.0/16, which sits inside the /12. If your corporate network also uses 172.17.x.x somewhere, containers and the corporate LAN collide.

":: can be used twice if the address is long enough." No — once per address, always. Two :: sequences make the address unparseable, because you cannot tell how many zero groups each one stands for. 2001:db8::1::1 is not an address. It is a syntax error.

"Link-local addresses are useless without a router." They are the opposite of useless: two hosts on the same link can communicate over link-local with zero configuration — no DHCP, no router, no global addresses. This is how a fresh machine finds its router in the first place (router advertisements arrive via link-local). The limitation is scope, not utility: link-local never crosses a router, so you need the %interface zone ID to disambiguate.

"IPv6 has no fragmentation, so big packets just work." IPv6 has no router fragmentation. The source can still fragment via the Fragment extension header — and Path MTU Discovery (via ICMPv6 Packet Too Big) is how the source learns the right size. But the same black-hole failure from the IPv4 section applies: filter those ICMPv6 messages and large transfers stall. "No fragmentation" really means "no router fragmentation." The endpoint still has to do the work.

"Private addresses are secure because they're unroutable." Unroutable from the Internet is not the same as secure. RFC 1918 space is reachable from anywhere inside your network — including from a compromised laptop, a malicious container, or a misconfigured peering. Private addressing is a routing convenience. Security comes from firewall rules and segmentation, not from the address range.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Why did IPv6 drop the header checksum? Isn't that less safe?

The checksum was protecting the wrong thing at the wrong cost. It covered only the IP header, and because TTL changes at every hop, every router had to verify and recompute it — per-packet CPU work in the forwarding fast path. Meanwhile, corruption on the wire is already caught — and caught better — by the Ethernet FCS, a CRC-32 over the whole frame. End-to-end corruption is caught by the TCP/UDP checksum, which covers the payload the application actually cares about. The IP checksum was a middle check that duplicated both neighbors' work while taxing every router. Dropping it made forwarding cheaper without losing any real protection. The design lesson: a check is only worth its cost if it catches something no other check catches.

Q2: Explain fragmentation — the fields involved, and why modern networks avoid it.

When a router must forward a packet larger than the next link's MTU, IPv4 lets it split the payload into fragments, each with its own IP header. The Identification field groups fragments of one datagram; the MF (More Fragments) flag marks all but the last; the Fragment Offset says where each piece sits in the original, in 8-byte units; the receiver reassembles. Modern networks avoid it for three reasons: loss amplification (one lost fragment kills the whole datagram while the network carried the rest), security (overlapping/tiny fragments evade inspection; many borders drop fragments outright), and the PMTUD black hole (filtered ICMP breaks size discovery, stalling large transfers). The fix is Path MTU Discovery — TCP sets Don't Fragment, learns the bottleneck MTU from ICMP feedback, and sizes segments to fit. IPv6 made this the only option: routers never fragment.

Q3: What is the difference between unicast, multicast, broadcast, and anycast?

Unicast is one-to-one: one source, one destination address, one recipient. Broadcast is one-to-all: one frame delivered to every host on the LAN (ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff at L2; IPv4's 255.255.255.255 at L3) — simple, but it interrupts even uninterested hosts, which is why IPv6 abolished it. Multicast is one-to-many: one address names a group, and only subscribed hosts receive it — efficient for things like routing-protocol chatter and streaming. Anycast is one-to-nearest: the same unicast address is configured on multiple hosts, and routing delivers each packet to the closest one — how DNS roots and CDNs absorb global traffic. The interview signal: broadcast wastes everyone's time, multicast requires subscriptions, anycast is a routing trick rather than an address type.

Q4: A host has the address 10.20.30.40/24. What is its network, its broadcast address, and how many usable hosts? Where can this address be routed?

The /24 fixes the first 24 bits: network 10.20.30.0, broadcast 10.20.30.255 (all host bits set), 2⁸ − 2 = 254 usable hosts (.1–.254). It is in 10.0.0.0/8, one of the RFC 1918 private ranges, so it is routable only inside the private network — never on the public Internet. To reach the Internet it must pass through NAT. Bonus detail: the .0 and .255 reservations are convention from the classful era; on modern point-to-point /31 links (RFC 3021) both addresses are usable, since there is no broadcast to reserve.

Q5: IPv6 gives every device a globally routable address. Doesn't that expose every device to the Internet?

Routable is not the same as reachable. IPv6 restores the ability to address every device globally, but whether packets arrive is decided by firewall policy — same as IPv4. The default posture on IPv6 home routers and cloud security groups is "allow outbound, deny unsolicited inbound," exactly like NAT's effective behavior minus the address rewriting. The real shift is conceptual: with NAT, people confused "unreachable because translated" with "secure." IPv6 forces the honest version: your security boundary is your firewall rules, stated explicitly, not a side effect of address sharing. Audit the rules; do not trust the address family.

8. Try it — on your Mac

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Both address families on your interfaces: look for "inet" (v4) and "inet6" (v6).
# Your link-local fe80:: address is there with zero configuration.
ifconfig en0 | grep -E "inet6? "

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Your IPv6 default route: likely via a fe80:: link-local gateway with %en0 zone ID.
netstat -rn -f inet6 | grep default

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Does the world resolve over v6? Compare A vs AAAA answers. Needs internet.
dig +short example.com A; dig +short example.com AAAA

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Ping the IPv6 loopback and your link-local gateway. No internet needed for ::1.
ping6 -c 2 ::1

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
# Python one-liner: classify any address. No network needed.
python3 -c "import ipaddress; a=ipaddress.ip_address('fe80::1'); print('link_local:', a.is_link_local, '| private:', a.is_private)"

The dig output is the interesting one. If example.com returns an AAAA record and your network has IPv6, your browser is already using IPv6 for most of the web — Happy Eyeballs just never told you.

9. Code: addressing with the stdlib ipaddress module

Python's ipaddress module handles CIDR math, RFC 1918 detection, and IPv6 compression with no network access. code/m04/ip_addrs.py — output pasted from a real run:

network   : 192.168.1.0/24
netmask   : 255.255.255.0
broadcast : 192.168.1.255
hosts     : 254 (usable; network+broadcast reserved)
192.168.1.50 in 192.168.1.0/24: True
192.168.1.50 is_private       : True

RFC 1918 private ranges (never routed on the public Internet):
  10.0.0.0/8          16,777,216 addresses
  172.16.0.0/12        1,048,576 addresses
  192.168.0.0/16          65,536 addresses

written out : 2001:0db8:0000:0000:0000:ff00:0042:8329
compressed  : 2001:db8::ff00:42:8329 (:: used once, leading zeros dropped)
as integer  : 42540766411282592856904265327123268393 -> 128 bits = 126 bits used

2001:db8::1    -- global unicast (documentation range)
  is_private=True  is_link_local=False  is_multicast=False  is_loopback=False

fe80::1        -- link-local (every interface has one)
  is_private=True  is_link_local=True  is_multicast=False  is_loopback=False

ff02::1        -- multicast (all nodes on the link)
  is_private=False  is_link_local=False  is_multicast=True  is_loopback=False

::1            -- loopback
  is_private=True  is_link_local=False  is_multicast=False  is_loopback=True

Two details worth noticing. First, is_private returns True for the documentation range 2001:db8::/32 and for link-local — the module treats "not globally routable" as private, which is the operationally useful definition. Second, the "126 bits used" line. The address 2001:0db8:... needs only 126 of its 128 bits because its top two bits are zero. It is a reminder that the space is 128 bits wide even when individual addresses do not use all of them.

Next: Module 5 — TCP: Reliability. Sequence numbers, the handshake, retransmission, flow control, and congestion control — the protocol that makes the unreliable network feel reliable.

Module 05 — Subnetting: Cutting Address Space Into Useful Pieces

Every worked number in this module was cross-checked by a Python script (code/m05/subnet_check.py) against the stdlib ipaddress module. Where hand math and the script disagreed, the script won and the prose was fixed. One disagreement happened: the VLSM total is 936 addresses, not 1024.

1. Why it matters

Every IP address you have ever seen belongs to a network. The address alone is not enough to use it. You also need to know where the network ends and the host part begins.

That boundary decides three things that show up constantly in backend work. First, which destinations are "local" — reachable by shouting on the LAN — and which need a router. Second, how many devices fit on one network before you must carve it up. Third, how routers compress thousands of networks into a handful of routing-table entries.

Interviewers probe subnetting because it is the rare topic that is both mechanical and judgment-based. The math is exact: get the numbers right or wrong. The design is judgment: given a /16 and six departments, how do you slice it so nothing overlaps, nothing wastes, and next year's growth has somewhere to go.

This module builds both. The mechanics first — CIDR notation, network and broadcast addresses, usable ranges, host-count math. Then the judgment — variable-length subnet masking (VLSM) with a full worked enterprise example.

2. The mental model

Think of an IPv4 address as a 32-bit street address with a sliding divider.

The first N bits name the street. The remaining 32−N bits name the house. The number N — the prefix length — is the position of the divider. Change N and you change what "the network" means, even though the 32 bits stay the same.

192.168.1.10/24 says: the first 24 bits are the street (192.168.1), the last 8 bits are the house (10). 192.168.1.10/16 says: the first 16 bits are the street (192.168), the last 16 are the house (1.10). Same address, two different networks, because the divider moved.

Everything in subnetting is arithmetic on that divider. The network address is the address with all house bits set to zero. The broadcast address is the address with all house bits set to one. The usable hosts are everything in between.

One more piece of the model: routers think in prefixes, not addresses. A router's table says "to reach 10.0.0.0/8, send it out eth0" — one line covering 16 million addresses. Subnetting is how you choose the prefixes that make routing tables small and networks sane. The longest-prefix match rule (covered in Module 04) picks the most specific matching prefix, so well-chosen subnets route cleanly.

3. How it works

CIDR notation

Classless Inter-Domain Routing (CIDR) replaced the old class system (A, B, C) in the early 1990s. The old classes forced network sizes into three buckets: 16 million hosts, 65 thousand hosts, or 254 hosts. Nothing in between. CIDR replaced the buckets with the divider: any prefix length from /0 to /32 is legal.

The notation is address/prefix-length. 10.0.0.0/8 means the first 8 bits are fixed. 172.16.0.0/12 means the first 12 bits are fixed. The prefix length also has a dotted-decimal twin called the subnet mask: a /24 mask is 255.255.255.0 — 24 one-bits followed by 8 zero-bits.

Handy equivalences worth memorizing:

Prefix Mask Addresses Usable hosts
/32 255.255.255.255 1 1 (host route)
/31 255.255.255.254 2 2 (point-to-point, RFC 3021)
/30 255.255.255.252 4 2
/29 255.255.255.248 8 6
/28 255.255.255.240 16 14
/27 255.255.255.224 32 30
/26 255.255.255.192 64 62
/25 255.255.255.128 128 126
/24 255.255.255.0 256 254
/23 255.255.254.0 512 510
/22 255.255.252.0 1024 1022
/20 255.255.240.0 4096 4094
/16 255.255.0.0 65,536 65,534
/8 255.0.0.0 16,777,216 16,777,214
/0 0.0.0.0 4,294,967,296 4,294,967,294

Usable hosts = 2^(32−N) − 2. The −2 reserves the network address (all host bits zero) and the broadcast address (all host bits one). Two exceptions interviewers love. A /31 skips the reservation — RFC 3021, point-to-point links only, 2 usable. A /32 is a single host address with no network or broadcast at all, used for loopbacks and host routes.

The four addresses of any subnet

Every subnet calculation answers the same four questions:

  1. Network address — the subnet's name. Host bits all zero.
  2. Broadcast address — the "everyone on this subnet" address. Host bits all one.
  3. First usable — network address + 1. Usually the gateway's address.
  4. Last usable — broadcast address − 1.

Convention, not law: the gateway takes the first usable address (.1), and DHCP pools start just above it. Nothing in the protocol requires this. It is a habit the whole industry shares, which makes it nearly as binding.

The binary AND: how a host finds its own network

A host learns its subnet by AND-ing its address with its mask, bit by bit. Take 192.168.10.77 with a /26 mask. Only the last octet is interesting:

 77 =  0100 1101
mask =  1100 0000   (/26 → last octet keeps 2 network bits)
AND =   0100 0000 = 64

Network address: 192.168.10.64. Broadcast: host bits all one — 01 + 111111 = 127 → 192.168.10.127. Usable: .65 through .126.

The fast mental method skips binary. Find the "interesting octet" — the one where the mask is neither 255 nor 0. The block size is 256 minus the mask octet. For /26 the mask is 255.255.255.192, so the block size is 256 − 192 = 64. Subnets start at multiples of 64: 0, 64, 128, 192. The host .77 falls in the 64-block: network .64, broadcast .127. This multiple-of-block-size trick answers subnetting questions in seconds.

Two rules keep the trick honest. Network addresses always land on a multiple of the block size — if yours doesn't, you made an arithmetic error. And the broadcast of one subnet is always one less than the next subnet's network address.

Reading any mask at a glance

Mask octets only take nine values, and each maps to a fixed bit count. Memorize the column, and you can read any mask without binary:

Octet 0 128 192 224 240 248 252 254 255
Bits 0 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8

So 255.255.240.0 is 8 + 8 + 4 + 0 = /20. 255.255.255.224 is /27. 255.255.254.0 is /23. The octet values climb by halves of the remaining gap: 128, then +64 = 192, then +32 = 224, then +16 = 240, and so on. When a mask octet isn't in this table, the mask is invalid — a quick sanity check that catches typos like 255.255.255.100.

4. The exact structure: worked calculations

Every number below is asserted by code/m05/subnet_check.py.

Worked 1 — the canonical /24. 192.168.1.0/24: network 192.168.1.0, broadcast 192.168.1.255, usable 192.168.1.1 – 192.168.1.254, 254 hosts. This is the shape of a small office LAN or a home network.

Worked 2 — a /26 sliced from a /24. 10.0.0.128/26: block size 256 − 192 = 64; 128 is a multiple of 64, so the network is 10.0.0.128. Broadcast 10.0.0.191. Usable 10.0.0.129 – 10.0.0.190, 62 hosts. A /24 yields four /26s: .0, .64, .128, .192.

Worked 3 — a /30 point-to-point link. 203.0.113.4/30: block size 4; network 203.0.113.4, broadcast 203.0.113.7, usable .5 and .6 — exactly two addresses, one per router interface. WAN links between routers are /30s (or /31s, see below) because a link needs exactly two endpoints and nothing else.

Worked 4 — a /20. 172.16.16.0/20: the interesting octet is the third: mask 255.255.240.0, block size 256 − 240 = 16. Multiples of 16: 0, 16, 32… Network 172.16.16.0, broadcast 172.16.31.255, usable 172.16.16.1 – 172.16.31.254, 4094 hosts. Notice the broadcast spills into the fourth octet at .31.255 — a common place to slip.

Worked 5 — "which subnet is this host in?" Given 192.168.10.77/26: block size 64, the 64-block starts at .64. Network 192.168.10.64, broadcast 192.168.10.127, usable .65 – .126. The host .77 sits inside that range. This is the single most-asked subnetting interview question, and the block-size trick answers it without touching binary.

Sizing: the power-of-two ladder

Picking a prefix for N hosts: find the smallest power of two that covers N + 2 (network + broadcast), then the prefix is 32 minus that exponent. Need 400 hosts? 400 + 2 = 402 → next power of two is 512 = 2^9 → prefix /23 → 510 usable. Need 90? 92 → 128 = 2^7 → /25 → 126 usable. Need 25? 27 → 32 = 2^5 → /27 → 30 usable.

This ladder is the engine of VLSM. You climb it once per slice.

5. A realistic example: carving 10.10.0.0/16 with VLSM

Variable-Length Subnet Masking means what it says: different subnets of the same parent get different prefix lengths, sized to their actual need. Fixed-length subnetting (every slice a /24) wastes addresses on small departments and starves big ones. VLSM fits each slice.

The scenario: a regional office gets 10.10.0.0/16 (65,536 addresses). Requirements:

Slice Hosts needed Power of two Prefix Block
Engineering VLAN 400 512 = 2^9 /23 10.10.0.0/23
Sales VLAN 220 256 = 2^8 /24 10.10.2.0/24
Server farm 90 128 = 2^7 /25 10.10.3.0/25
Management 25 32 = 2^5 /27 10.10.3.128/27
WAN link A 2 4 = 2^2 /30 10.10.3.160/30
WAN link B 2 4 = 2^2 /30 10.10.3.164/30

The method, step by step:

  1. Sort largest first. Allocate the /23 before anything else. Big blocks need alignment (a /23 must start on a multiple of 2 in the third octet), and allocating small slices first fragments the space into pieces no big block can fit into. Same logic as memory allocation: largest-fit first.
  2. Engineering: 10.10.0.0/23. /23 = block size 2 in the third octet. Network 10.10.0.0, broadcast 10.10.1.255, usable 10.10.0.1 – 10.10.1.254 (510 hosts). Mask 255.255.254.0.
  3. Sales: 10.10.2.0/24. Next free address is 10.10.2.0. Broadcast 10.10.2.255, usable .1 – .254 (254 hosts).
  4. Servers: 10.10.3.0/25. Next free: 10.10.3.0. Block size 128. Broadcast 10.10.3.127, usable .1 – .126 (126 hosts).
  5. Management: 10.10.3.128/27. Next free: 10.10.3.128. Block size 32. Broadcast 10.10.3.159, usable .129 – .158 (30 hosts).
  6. WAN A: 10.10.3.160/30. Block size 4. Network .160, broadcast .163, usable .161, .162.
  7. WAN B: 10.10.3.164/30. Network .164, broadcast .167, usable .165, .166.

Total consumed: 512 + 256 + 128 + 32 + 4 + 4 = 936 addresses out of 65,536. The verifier confirms all six slices are disjoint, all sit inside 10.10.0.0/16, and 64,600 addresses remain — 10.10.3.168 through 10.10.255.255 — for growth. A growth plan matters: the next likely request is "engineering needs 600 hosts," and you can point at the free space instead of renumbering.

A note on the /31 versus /30 choice. RFC 3021 lets a /31 carry two usable addresses with no network or broadcast reservation, which is exactly what a point-to-point link needs. It works on modern gear. But not all gear — older or quirky implementations still assume a broadcast address exists. In an interview, "I'd use /30 unless the platform guarantees /31 support" shows you know both the standard and the real world.

Worked 7 — the membership test. Given an address, which slice owns it? This is what routers do on every packet (longest-prefix match), and it's a sharp interview question because the /23 spans two third-octet values:

  • 10.10.1.200 → Engineering (10.10.0.0/23 covers .0.0–.1.255; the second half of the /23 is easy to forget)
  • 10.10.3.130 → Management (10.10.3.128/27)
  • 10.10.3.166 → WAN B (10.10.3.164/30)
  • 10.10.3.200 → no slice — free space, available for growth
  • 10.10.4.1 → no slice — free space

The method: walk the slices largest-first and test membership. The verifier asserts all five. The trap answer is "10.10.1.200 is unallocated" — it isn't; the /23's broadcast is 10.10.1.255, so .1.x is Engineering's second half.

Worked 8 — sizing the parent. Four offices, each needing the full six-slice carve (936 addresses). Give each office a /16 for headroom — 936 used, 64,600 free per office, same as the worked carve. Four /16s aggregate into one /12: 10.0.0.0/12, covering 10.0.0.0–10.15.255.255. The first four /16s go to the offices (10.0, 10.1, 10.2, 10.3); twelve /16s remain for future sites. Upstream, the whole company is one line: 10.0.0.0/12. This is hierarchical addressing — the same idea as the VLSM carve, one level up. Design the hierarchy before you carve the leaves, and aggregation takes care of itself.

Supernetting: aggregation in the other direction

Subnetting splits; supernetting (aggregation, summarization) combines. Take the VLSM carve above. Six prefixes live inside 10.10.0.0/16, and they are contiguous: 10.10.0.0/23, 10.10.2.0/24, 10.10.3.0/25, 10.10.3.128/27, 10.10.3.160/30, 10.10.3.164/30. The upstream router needs exactly one line — 10.10.0.0/16 — to reach all of them. That is why the carve kept everything inside one /16. Contiguous allocation aggregates; scattered allocation doesn't.

The worked check: can 10.10.0.0/23 and 10.10.2.0/24 aggregate into 10.10.0.0/22? A /22 covers 10.10.0.0–10.10.3.255 — yes, both fit, and so do the remaining four slices. One /22 line covers the whole office. But advertise only the /22 upstream, never the /30s: the internet doesn't need to know about your WAN links, and every extra prefix is routing-table bloat someone else pays for.

Aggregation breaks when allocation is discontiguous. If Engineering had taken 10.10.0.0/23 and Sales had taken 10.99.0.0/24 "because it was free," no single prefix covers both without also covering everything between them. This is the real cost of sloppy address planning: not wasted addresses, but a routing table that grows with every exception.

The private ranges you'll actually carve (RFC 1918)

Real enterprise carves start from private space. Three ranges, and the choice between them is a design decision:

Range Prefix Addresses Typical use
10.0.0.0 – 10.255.255.255 /8 16,777,216 Large enterprises, one /16 per site
172.16.0.0 – 172.31.255.255 /12 1,048,576 Mid-size; one /16 per site still fits (16 sites)
192.168.0.0 – 192.168.255.255 /16 65,536 Small sites, labs, home networks

The planning rule: pick one range for the whole organization and never mix. Two sites both using 192.168.1.0/24 can't be merged later without renumbering — and mergers happen. A /8 gives every site its own /16 with room for 256 sites; that headroom is why 10.0.0.0/8 dominates enterprise design. The worked VLSM example used 10.10.0.0/16 — site "10" of a 10.0.0.0/8 plan, with sites 11, 12, … untouched.

IPv6 subnetting in sixty seconds

The bit math is identical; the conventions differ. A site typically receives a /48. Each LAN gets a /64 — not sized by host count, because 2^64 hosts is never the constraint. The /64 boundary is architectural: SLAAC (Module 07) requires a 64-bit interface identifier.

Carving is done on nibble boundaries (multiples of 4 bits) so the hex stays readable: 2001:db8:0::/48 → 2001:db8:0:1::/64, 2001:db8:0:2::/64, … 2001:db8:0:ffff::/64 gives 65,536 LANs per site. Nobody computes block sizes in v6 — with 16 bits of subnet space per site, the discipline is naming and documentation, not arithmetic. The interview point: "how many hosts per IPv6 subnet?" is a trick question. The answer is "we don't size v6 subnets by hosts; every LAN is a /64."

Classful addressing: the history interviewers assume you know

Before CIDR (1993), the divider couldn't slide. The first bits of the address fixed the network size — the class system:

Class Leading bits Range Networks Hosts each
A 0 1.0.0.0 – 126.255.255.255 126 16.7M
B 10 128.0.0.0 – 191.255.255.255 16,384 65,534
C 110 192.0.0.0 – 223.255.255.255 2M 254

An organization needing 300 hosts got a Class B and wasted 65,000 addresses; one needing 300,000 couldn't use a Class C. Address exhaustion was really allocation exhaustion. CIDR fixed it by letting the divider sit anywhere. Interviewers ask about classes to check whether you know why CIDR exists — the answer is waste, not elegance. You'll still see the fossils: "Class C" used to mean /24, and old documentation says "a Class B" when it means 172.16.0.0/12.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"The network and broadcast addresses are wasted." They are reserved, not wasted. The network address names the subnet in routing tables and ACLs. The broadcast address is the delivery mechanism for "everyone here" traffic — ARP requests, DHCP discovers. A protocol needs both names to exist.

"Usable hosts = 2^hostbits, I just won't use the top and bottom." Close, but the formula is 2^hostbits − 2 for a reason. Some device, some day, will send to the broadcast address. Some routing table will match the network address. Reserve them formally.

"A bigger subnet is always simpler." A /16 for 200 hosts is simpler until a broadcast storm or a compromised laptop takes down 65,000 addresses' worth of broadcast domain. Subnetting is also a blast-radius decision. Small broadcast domains fail small.

Off-by-one on the interesting octet. The classic error: /20 on 172.16.16.0 computed as broadcast 172.16.16.255. The mask's third octet is 240, block size 16, so the third octet runs 16–31, not 16–16. Always ask "does my broadcast reach into the next octet?" when the mask octet isn't 255 or 0.

Forgetting alignment. A /26 must start on a multiple of 64. 10.0.0.100 is not a valid /26 network address, no matter how convenient it looks. The verifier's alignment assert (int(network_address) % block == 0) catches this; make it a reflex.

Overlapping slices in VLSM. Allocating 10.10.3.0/25 and then 10.10.3.64/26 "because there's room" — there isn't; .64–.127 is inside the /25. Allocate sequentially from the next free address and the overlap can't happen.

"Subnetting is obsolete because of IPv6." IPv6 subnets constantly — every LAN gets a /64, and site allocations are carved with the same largest-first discipline. The bit math is identical; only the address length changes. Enterprises will run dual-stack for another decade at least, so v4 subnetting stays an interview staple.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: How many usable hosts in 10.20.0.0/22, and what are the network and broadcast addresses?

/22 → 2^(10) − 2 = 1022 usable. Interesting octet: third, mask 252, block size 4. Multiples of 4: 0, 4, 8… 0 is a multiple, so network 10.20.0.0, broadcast 10.20.3.255 (the next block starts at 10.20.4.0), usable 10.20.0.1 – 10.20.3.254.

Q2: I give you 192.168.5.0/24 and four departments needing 60, 60, 30, and 12 hosts. Carve it.

Size each: 60 → 64 = 2^6 → /26 (62 usable); 30 → 32 = 2^5 → /27 (30 usable); 12 → 16 = 2^4 → /28 (14 usable). Largest first: 192.168.5.0/26, 192.168.5.64/26, 192.168.5.128/27, 192.168.5.160/28. Used: 64+64+32+16 = 176 of 256 addresses; .176–.255 free. The trap is sizing 60 hosts as /27 (30 usable) — always add the +2 before climbing the power-of-two ladder.

Q3: Why can't two hosts with addresses 10.1.1.5/24 and 10.1.2.5/24 talk directly?

Each host ANDs the destination with its own mask. 10.1.1.5/24 sees 10.1.2.5 as off-network (different /24), so it sends to its gateway instead of ARPing locally. The mask, not the address, defines "local." Change both to /16 and they ARP directly.

Q4: What is a /31, and when would you use it?

A /31 has 2 addresses and, per RFC 3021, no reserved network or broadcast — both addresses are usable. It fits point-to-point links, which need exactly two endpoints. Use it where the platform supports it; default to /30 on unfamiliar gear.

Q5: How does subnetting relate to route aggregation?

They are inverse operations. Subnetting splits a large prefix into smaller ones for assignment; aggregation (summarization) combines small prefixes into a larger one for advertisement — e.g., advertising 10.10.0.0/16 upstream instead of six longer prefixes. Clean VLSM carves aggregate cleanly because the slices are contiguous. This is why the worked example kept everything inside one /16: the whole office is one routing-table line to the rest of the world.

8. Try it

Run on your Mac — your output will differ.

# What subnet is this Mac on? (look for "inet" under en0/en1)
ifconfig | grep -B2 'inet ' | head -12
# Compute network/broadcast/usable for your own address with Python
python3 -c "
import ipaddress
n = ipaddress.ip_network('192.168.1.10/24', strict=False)
print('network:  ', n.network_address)
print('broadcast:', n.broadcast_address)
print('usable:   ', n.network_address + 1, '-', n.broadcast_address - 1)
print('hosts:    ', n.num_addresses - 2)
"
# Replace '192.168.1.10/24' with the inet address from the ifconfig output.
# Run the module's verifier — every worked example, asserted
~/.local/bin/python3.13 ~/workspace/build/networking-guide/code/m05/subnet_check.py
# See how your Mac's routing table uses prefixes (longest match in action)
netstat -rn | head -15

Module 06 — ARP & Neighbor Discovery: From IP Address to MAC Address

Packet formats in this module are exercised by code/m06/arp_frame.py, which builds real Ethernet+ARP frames with struct, decodes them field by field, and asserts the round trip. All runs below are pasted from its actual output.

1. Why it matters

IP routing gets a packet to the right network. It does not get the packet onto the wire. The final hop — from a router or a sender to the actual next device — needs the destination's link-layer address: the MAC address on Ethernet.

Something has to answer the question "I know the IP, what is the MAC?" That something is the Address Resolution Protocol (ARP) on IPv4, and Neighbor Discovery (NDP) on IPv6. They are the glue between Layer 3 and Layer 2, and they run before almost every conversation on a LAN begins.

For interviews, ARP is a favorite because it exposes how well you understand layering. "Why does the ping work the second time but not the first?" is an ARP question. "Why can't ARP cross a router?" is a broadcast-domain question. "What is gratuitous ARP for?" separates people who memorized the acronym from people who have debugged a failover.

2. The mental model

Think of ARP as shouting a question in a crowded room.

You walk into a LAN knowing one thing: the IP address you want to reach. You do not know which physical machine holds it. So you shout to everyone — a broadcast — "Who has 192.168.1.20? Tell 192.168.1.10!" Every machine hears it. All but one ignore it. The owner of 192.168.1.20 answers you directly, a unicast: "192.168.1.20 is at aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff." You write that down in a little notebook, the ARP table, so you never have to shout again for a while.

Three consequences fall out of this picture. First, the question must be a broadcast, so it cannot leave the room — broadcasts do not cross routers. Second, the notebook has an expiry, because machines leave and NICs get replaced. Third, anyone in the room can answer, truthfully or not — which is the entire basis of ARP spoofing attacks.

IPv6 replaces the shout with a polite multicast. Instead of interrupting everyone, Neighbor Solicitation goes to a solicited-node multicast group that only the target (and a few neighbors) listens to. Same question, smaller audience. The notebook, the expiry, and the trust problem all carry over.

3. How it works

The ARP exchange

Alice (192.168.1.10, MAC 00:11:22:33:44:55) wants to send to Bob (192.168.1.20). Her ARP table has no entry for him.

  1. Request. Alice builds an ARP request: "who has 192.168.1.20? Tell 192.168.1.10." She wraps it in an Ethernet frame addressed to ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff — the broadcast address — with EtherType 0x0806. The switch floods it out every port in the VLAN.
  2. Everyone checks. Each host compares the target IP against its own. Non-matches drop the frame silently.
  3. Reply. Bob recognizes his IP. He builds an ARP reply — "192.168.1.20 is at aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff" — and unicasts it to Alice's MAC. Note the asymmetry: the reply is unicast, because Bob learned Alice's MAC from the request.
  4. Cache. Both sides record the other's mapping. Alice caches Bob; Bob opportunistically caches Alice from the sender fields of the request. The entry ages out in minutes (typical defaults: 2–20 minutes depending on OS).

From the verifier script, the actual bytes:

ARP REQUEST   eth_dst=ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff <- oper=request <- sender_mac=00:11:22:33:44:55 <- sender_ip=192.168.1.10 <- target_ip=192.168.1.20
ARP REPLY     eth_dst=00:11:22:33:44:55 <- oper=reply   <- sender_mac=aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff <- sender_ip=192.168.1.20 <- target_ip=192.168.1.10

The ARP table

The ARP table is a cache, not a configuration. Each entry maps an IP to a MAC with a timer. When the timer expires, the next packet to that IP triggers a fresh request. This is why the first ping to a new host sometimes drops: the ICMP echo request is waiting while ARP resolves. The second ping sails through on the cached entry.

On every OS the table is inspectable. arp -a prints it; entries marked incomplete mean a request went out and no reply came back — the signature of a host that is down, firewalled at Layer 2, or on the wrong VLAN.

The ARP table up close

Here is what the table actually looks like on a Mac (run on your Mac — your output will differ):

$ arp -a | head -5
gateway (192.168.1.1) at 3a:22:fb:11:22:33 on en0 ifscope [ethernet]
printer (192.168.1.40) at 8c:3b:ad:11:22:33 on en0 ifscope [ethernet]
? (192.168.1.99) at (incomplete) on en0 ifscope [ethernet]

Three entries, three stories. The gateway resolves normally. The printer has a hostname in parentheses — reverse DNS worked for it. And 192.168.1.99 is incomplete: something ARPed for it and nobody answered. Maybe the device is asleep. Maybe it's a stale DHCP lease pointing at a host that left. Maybe the VLAN is wrong. The entry sits there, retrying, until the OS gives up — and every packet queued for .99 waits behind it. When "I can't reach that one host," this is the first place to look: a healthy table has no incomplete entries that persist.

Entry lifetime varies by OS. On macOS and BSD, dynamic entries expire after about 20 minutes of disuse. On Linux, the neighbor subsystem moves entries through reachable → stale → probe states on a scale of tens of seconds, re-verifying on next use — the same state machine IPv6's NDP formalized. The operational takeaway is the same everywhere: ARP entries are soft state. They heal themselves. If flushing the table (sudo arp -d -a on macOS) fixes the problem permanently, the problem was a stale entry. If the bad entry comes back, something is actively lying — duplicate IP or spoofing — and the flush just bought one timer cycle.

Gratuitous ARP

A gratuitous ARP is a request where the sender and target IP are the same: "who has 192.168.1.10? Tell 192.168.1.10." Nobody needs to answer — the sender already knows its own MAC. The point is the announcement.

It serves two purposes. First, duplicate address detection: if someone else answers, two machines claim the same IP, and the OS can flag or defend it. Second, cache refresh: everyone who hears it updates their ARP table with the sender's MAC. This is the mechanism behind IP failover. When a standby server takes over a virtual IP, it sends a gratuitous ARP. Every switch and host on the LAN relearns which MAC now owns that IP. Without it, traffic keeps flowing to the dead primary until entries expire.

The verifier confirms the shape: sender_ip == target_ip, destination is broadcast, target MAC zeroed — a request frame announcing rather than asking.

GRATUITOUS   eth_dst=ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff <- oper=request <- sender_ip=192.168.1.10 <- target_ip=192.168.1.10

Why ARP never crosses a router

The request is a Layer-2 broadcast: Ethernet destination ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff. A router's job is to terminate broadcasts. It receives the frame, sees the broadcast destination, and does not forward it. Forwarding it would merge every LAN's broadcast traffic into one giant noise domain. Preventing exactly that is why routing was invented.

This is why every routed hop needs its own ARP exchange. When Alice sends to an off-network address, she ARPs for her gateway's IP, not the final destination's. The gateway ARPs for the next hop on the far side. Each broadcast domain resolves its own next hop. Traceroute works because each router decrements TTL and answers locally — no router ever needs the MAC of a host two networks away.

4. The exact structure: the ARP packet (RFC 826)

ARP was defined in RFC 826 (Plummer, November 1982) — one of the oldest RFCs still in daily use, and essentially unchanged. It rides directly on Ethernet (EtherType 0x0806), not inside IP. The 28-byte payload for Ethernet/IPv4:

Field by field:

Field Size Value on Ethernet/IPv4
Hardware type 16 bits 1 (Ethernet)
Protocol type 16 bits 0x0800 (IPv4)
Hardware addr len 8 bits 6
Protocol addr len 8 bits 4
Operation 16 bits 1 = request, 2 = reply
Sender hardware addr 48 bits Requester's MAC
Sender protocol addr 32 bits Requester's IP
Target hardware addr 48 bits Zeroed in requests; filled in replies
Target protocol addr 32 bits The IP being resolved

The generic HTYPE/PTYPE design is why ARP survived: the same format resolves other pairings in theory. In practice, on modern networks, it is always Ethernet and IPv4.

5. IPv6: Neighbor Discovery (RFC 4861)

IPv6 abolished ARP entirely. Its replacement, Neighbor Discovery, is defined in RFC 4861 (Narten et al., September 2007) and runs on top of ICMPv6 — not raw Ethernet frames. Five message types cover what ARP, ICMP router discovery, and ICMP redirects did separately in IPv4:

  • Neighbor Solicitation (NS, type 135) — the ARP request equivalent. "Who has this IPv6 address?" Sent to the solicited-node multicast address, not broadcast: ff02::1:ffXX:XXXX, derived from the target's last 24 bits. Only hosts in that multicast group process it, so a 500-host LAN doesn't interrupt all 500 hosts for one lookup.
  • Neighbor Advertisement (NA, type 136) — the ARP reply. Carries the target's link-layer address, plus flags: S (solicited — this answers a solicitation), O (override — update your cache even if you have an entry), R (router).
  • Router Solicitation (RS, type 133) — a host joining the network asks "are there any routers here?" Sent to the all-routers multicast ff02::2.
  • Router Advertisement (RA, type 134) — routers answer (and also announce periodically) with the network prefix, default-router information, and flags that drive SLAAC and DHCPv6 (Module 07). Sent to all-nodes ff02::1.
  • Redirect (type 137) — a router tells a host about a better first-hop for a destination. Same job as ICMP redirect in IPv4.

Two design upgrades matter. First, Duplicate Address Detection: before using an address, a node sends an NS for it with the unspecified source address (::). If an NA comes back, the address is taken. This is gratuitous ARP's first job, built into the base protocol. Second, Neighbor Unreachability Detection: NDP actively probes whether a cached neighbor still answers, rather than waiting for a timer to expire. Stale entries get detected instead of merely aging out.

The security story is unchanged in kind: NDP trusts whoever answers, same as ARP. SEND (Secure Neighbor Discovery, RFC 3971) adds cryptographic address ownership, but deployment is rare — in practice, networks defend with RA Guard and dynamic ARP inspection on the switch, not with protocol cryptography.

6. A realistic example: the first ping

You plug a laptop into the office LAN and ping the printer at 192.168.7.40 for the first time. Here is every step, in order:

  1. Your laptop ANDs 192.168.7.40 with its /24 mask → on-network.
  2. ARP table lookup for 192.168.7.40 → miss.
  3. ARP request broadcast: "who has 192.168.7.40? Tell 192.168.7.21." Your ping packet waits in a queue.
  4. The printer replies, unicast: "192.168.7.40 is at 8c:3b:ad:11:22:33."
  5. Your laptop caches the entry, then finally sends the ICMP echo.
  6. The printer's reply path: it ARPs for your address only if it lacks the entry — but it learned your MAC from your request in step 3, so usually no second broadcast happens.

Now ping a host across the VPN, say 10.99.0.8. Step 1 gives off-network. Your laptop ARPs for the gateway (192.168.7.1), not for 10.99.0.8. The gateway's MAC goes in the Ethernet header; 10.99.0.8 stays in the IP header. This split — L2 address of the next hop, L3 address of the destination — is the single most important idea in this module.

Reading ARP on the wire

Theory sticks better after you've seen the bytes. On your Mac, this capture filter shows ARP as it happens (run on your Mac — your output will differ):

sudo tcpdump -e -n arp -c 6

Typical output when you ping a new host:

00:11:22:33:44:55 > ff:ff:ff:ff:ff:ff, ethertype ARP (0x0806), length 42:
    Request who-has 192.168.1.20 tell 192.168.1.10, length 28
aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff > 00:11:22:33:44:55, ethertype ARP (0x0806), length 42:
    Reply 192.168.1.20 is-at aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff, length 28

Read it the way the switch does. The first line's destination is the broadcast address — that's why every host on the LAN processes it. The second line's destination is Alice's MAC — unicast, because Bob learned it from the request. length 28 is the ARP payload; length 42 is the full frame, matching the verifier script's 14 + 28 exactly. When debugging, the two failure signatures are: requests with no replies (target down or L2-isolated), and replies from a MAC you don't recognize (duplicate IP or spoofing).

NDP options: the details that matter

RFC 4861 messages carry options as TLVs, and two of them do the heavy lifting:

  • Source/Target link-layer address option. The NS carries the sender's MAC so the target can reply and cache without a second exchange — the same free learning ARP gets from its sender fields. The NA carries the target's MAC: the actual answer.
  • MTU option (in Router Advertisements). Tells hosts the link MTU. IPv6 routers never fragment (Module 04's fragmentation story), so a wrong MTU means blackholed large packets — the v6 analog of a classic PMTU failure.

The RA also carries prefix information options: each announced prefix comes with its valid and preferred lifetimes. A prefix can be deprecated (preferred lifetime zero — stop using it for new connections) while remaining valid (existing connections continue). This is how networks renumber gracefully: announce the new prefix, deprecate the old, wait for connections to drain. DHCPv4 has no equivalent — renumbering v4 means lease expiry and a flag day.

ARP in virtualized environments

Virtualization made gratuitous ARP load-bearing infrastructure. When a VM migrates between hypervisors (vMotion and its equivalents), its IP stays the same but the physical switch port changes. The hypervisor sends a gratuitous ARP (historically a RARP — same idea, older protocol) on behalf of the VM the moment it resumes. Every physical switch relearns the MAC-to-port binding instantly. Without it, the VM would be unreachable until ARP entries expired — a multi-minute outage on every migration.

Containers complicate the picture further. Each container gets a veth pair and often its own network namespace; ARP happens per-namespace, and overlay networks (VXLAN, Geneve) replace broadcast with head-end replication or multicast underlay. The mental model survives unchanged — "who has this IP?" still gets asked — but the room being shouted in is now virtual, and the shouting is tunneled.

Joining an IPv6 network, step by step

The Module 06 IPv6 story deserves the same first-ping treatment as ARP. A laptop joins a v6-enabled LAN:

  1. It multicasts a Router Solicitation to ff02::2 (all routers): "I'm new — who's the router?"
  2. The router answers with a Router Advertisement to ff02::1: "your prefix is 2001:db8:7::/64, I'm your default router, A=1 so use SLAAC, O=1 so fetch DNS from stateless DHCPv6."
  3. The laptop forms a tentative address. It combines the prefix with a random interface ID (privacy extensions, Module 07). Then it runs Duplicate Address Detection: it sends a Neighbor Solicitation for its own tentative address with source :: (the unspecified address — it can't use the address until it's proven unique).
  4. Silence means success. No NA arrives, so the address is unique. The laptop assigns it and starts working.
  5. Later, when it needs the router's MAC, a normal NS/NA exchange resolves it — cached in the neighbor table (ndp -a on your Mac), with unreachability detection probing it if traffic stalls.

If step 3 gets an answer — an NA for the tentative address — the address is taken. The laptop picks a new random ID and tries again. This loop is why privacy-extension addresses are effectively collision-free in practice.

IPv4's quiet DAD (RFC 5227)

IPv4 has duplicate detection too, though nobody calls it that. RFC 5227 defines the ARP-probe ritual: before using an address — at boot, at DHCP bind, at link-up — a host sends ARP probes with the sender IP set to 0.0.0.0 ("is anyone using 192.168.1.77?"). The zeroed sender IP is the tell: a normal ARP announces ownership, a probe pointedly claims nothing. If a reply or a conflicting probe arrives, the address is taken — the DHCP client sends a DECLINE (Module 07) and asks for another. macOS and Windows both do this on every DHCP bind, which is why a duplicate IP usually surfaces as a pop-up warning rather than silent corruption.

7. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"ARP resolves hostnames." No. DNS resolves names to IPs; ARP resolves IPs to MACs. They are different lookups at different layers, and confusing them produces confused debugging.

"The ARP reply is broadcast too." It is unicast. The requester put its MAC in the sender fields, so the responder knows exactly where to send the answer. (Gratuitous ARP is the exception that proves the rule — it is a request nobody needs to answer.)

"Clearing the ARP table fixes connectivity." Sometimes — stale entries after a NIC replacement or an IP move do cause blackholes, and flushing forces fresh resolution. But if the entry re-learns wrong, you have a duplicate IP or a spoofing problem, and the flush just hid it for one timer cycle.

"IPv6 doesn't need neighbor discovery because addresses are plentiful." Address abundance has nothing to do with it. The L3-to-L2 mapping question exists at any address length. IPv6 still asks "who has this address" — it just asks by multicast instead of broadcast.

"ARP spoofing requires the attacker to be a router." It requires the attacker to be on the LAN. Any host can send forged ARP replies — "192.168.1.1 is at attacker-MAC" — and poison victims' caches. Defense is switch-side (dynamic ARP inspection) or cryptographic (rarely deployed). This is why "the LAN is trusted" is an assumption, not a fact.

"Proxy ARP means ARP crosses routers." Proxy ARP is a router answering ARP on behalf of distant hosts — typically to support hosts with no default gateway configured. The ARP exchange itself still stays inside the broadcast domain; the router just lies in its answer. It is a compatibility hack, and modern guidance is to turn it off: it masks misconfiguration and breaks cleanly-designed addressing.

8. Interview Q&A

Q1: Why does the first ping to a host sometimes time out while the second succeeds?

The first echo request can't leave until the sender resolves the destination MAC. The ARP request goes out, the reply comes back, the entry is cached — and by then the first ping's timeout expired. The second ping finds a warm cache and completes. (On some stacks the first ping is merely slow rather than lost; the mechanism is the same.)

Q2: A server was re-IP'd from .50 to .60, and half the LAN still can't reach it 10 minutes later. What's going on?

Stale ARP entries. Hosts that recently talked to .50 cached its old MAC with a fresh timer. Fix: wait for expiry, flush caches (arp -d on the affected hosts), or — the correct operational answer — the server should have sent a gratuitous ARP on re-IP so everyone relearned immediately.

Q3: Why can't a host ARP for an address on another subnet?

It never tries. The host ANDs the destination with its mask, sees off-network, and ARPs for its gateway instead. Even if it did broadcast the request, the router wouldn't forward it — broadcasts terminate at the router. Address resolution is per-broadcast-domain by construction.

Q4: What's the difference between ARP and Neighbor Discovery, beyond "one is v4 and one is v6"?

Three substantive differences. NDP uses solicited-node multicast instead of broadcast, so uninvolved hosts aren't interrupted. NDP folds in router discovery, prefix learning (via RA), redirects, and duplicate address detection — ARP needed separate protocols for those. And NDP runs over ICMPv6 with unreachability detection, actively probing stale neighbors instead of relying purely on timers.

Q5: Walk me through what happens, at L2 and L3, when host A sends to host B on a different subnet.

A ANDs B's IP with its mask → off-network. A looks up its gateway's IP in the ARP table (ARPs if missing) and builds a frame: destination MAC = gateway's MAC, destination IP = B's IP. The gateway receives it, routes by destination IP, ARPs for the next hop on the egress interface, and re-frames with the next hop's MAC. The IP header's source and destination never change; the Ethernet header is rewritten at every hop.

9. Try it

Run on your Mac — your output will differ.

# Your ARP table right now — the "notebook" from the mental model
arp -a | head -10
# Watch it work: clear one entry, then ping, then look again
# (shows the entry go from "incomplete" to resolved)
ping -c1 192.168.1.1 >/dev/null 2>&1; arp -a | grep '192.168.1.1'
# Your own MAC and IP — the sender fields of every ARP you emit
ifconfig en0 | grep -E 'ether|inet '
# IPv6 neighbors — the NDP equivalent of the ARP table
ndp -a | head -10
# Build and decode real ARP frames locally (no network needed)
~/.local/bin/python3.13 ~/workspace/build/networking-guide/code/m06/arp_frame.py

Module 07 — DHCP & SLAAC: How Hosts Get Their Addresses

Packet formats in this module are exercised by code/m07/dhcp_packet.py, which builds a real DHCP DISCOVER and OFFER with struct, decodes every option, and asserts the round trip. All runs below are pasted from its actual output.

1. Why it matters

Nobody types an IP address into a laptop anymore. Somewhere between "joined the Wi-Fi" and "loaded the page," the machine acquired an address, a subnet mask, a gateway, and a DNS server — in under a second, with no human involved. That is DHCP on IPv4, and SLAAC (plus DHCPv6) on IPv6.

For a backend engineer, this matters in three places. First, debugging. "The service can't reach the database" is sometimes simpler than it looks: the container got no DHCP lease and sits on a 169.254.x.x address. Second, design: lease times, reservations, and exclusion ranges are operational decisions with real failure modes. Third, interviews: DORA is one of the most asked-about exchanges in networking because it is small enough to draw from memory and rich enough to reveal depth — broadcast vs unicast, timers, options, relay agents.

2. The mental model

Think of DHCP as a hotel check-in desk.

You arrive with no room (no address). You shout into the lobby — a broadcast — "is there a front desk here?" (DISCOVER). A clerk answers, "yes, I can offer you room 77" (OFFER). You say "I'll take room 77" (REQUEST). The clerk confirms, "room 77 is yours until noon tomorrow, here's your key card" (ACK). The key card lists your room number, the floor's subnet mask, the elevator location (gateway), and the concierge number (DNS).

The lease has a checkout time. At half the lease (T1), you call the same clerk and ask to extend (renew). If the clerk doesn't answer, at seven-eighths of the lease (T2) you shout into the lobby again — any clerk will do (rebind). If nobody answers by checkout, you vacate the room: the address goes back to the pool, and you start over with a DISCOVER.

SLAAC is the hostel version: no front desk at all. The building announces "this floor is 2001:db8:1::/64" over the PA (a Router Advertisement). You pick your own room number — derived from your MAC or randomized — and move in. No lease, no checkout, no clerk. The tradeoff: you get an address and a prefix, but no DNS server and no gateway beyond the announcing router, which is why networks often pair SLAAC with a lightweight DHCPv6 for the extras.

3. How it works

The DORA exchange (RFC 2131)

DHCP is defined in RFC 2131 (Droms, March 1997). The four-message exchange runs over UDP: the client speaks from port 68, the server from port 67. Every step matters, including the ones that look redundant.

  1. DISCOVER — client to server, broadcast. The client has no address, so it can't receive unicast. Source 0.0.0.0:68 → destination 255.255.255.255:67. It includes the client's MAC and a transaction ID (xid) — a random 32-bit number that matches the four messages of one conversation.
  2. OFFER — server to client, broadcast (or unicast if the client set the broadcast flag off and the server knows better — in practice, usually broadcast on the first exchange). "You can have 192.168.1.77." The offered address sits in the yiaddr ("your IP") field. Multiple servers may offer; the client picks one, typically the first.
  3. REQUEST — client to server, broadcast. The client announces which offer it accepts — broadcast deliberately, so the losing servers hear it and withdraw their offers. This is the step people forget, and it's the reason the exchange needs four messages instead of two.
  4. ACK — server to client. The lease is committed. The ACK carries the full option set: subnet mask, router, DNS, lease time, and the server's identifier.

From the verifier script — the actual packets:

DISCOVER  xid=0x12345678  msg_type=1 (1=DISCOVER)  options=[12, 53, 55, 61]
OFFER     xid=0x12345678  msg_type=2 (2=OFFER)  yiaddr=192.168.1.77
          mask=255.255.255.0  router=192.168.1.1  dns=8.8.8.8  lease=86400s

The lease lifecycle: T1 and T2 in plain language

A lease is a timer, not a permanent grant. For a lease of length L:

  • T1 = 0.5 × L (renew). The client unicasts a REQUEST to the same server that granted the lease: "extend my lease." If the server ACKs, the clock resets to a fresh L. This is the normal, quiet path — a healthy client renews at T1 forever and keeps its address across days and reboots.
  • T2 = 0.875 × L (rebind). If T1 got no answer, the client escalates. The server may be down, or the network moved. The client broadcasts a REQUEST to any server: "whoever you are, extend this lease." Any server with authority over the subnet can ACK.
  • Expiry = L. If T2 also fails, the client must stop using the address. It unconfigures the interface and restarts at DISCOVER.

The practical consequence: lease length is a tradeoff. Short leases (hours) recycle addresses fast — good for guest Wi-Fi with high churn. Long leases (days) keep addresses stable and cut broadcast traffic — good for offices. But a long lease also means a misconfigured scope poisons clients for days. When someone asks "how long should the lease be," the answer is "it depends on churn," and now you know what churn means here.

Key options

Options are the TLV (type-length-value) list after the fixed header. The interview-relevant ones:

Option Name What it carries
1 Subnet mask e.g. 255.255.255.0
3 Router Default gateway (can be a list)
6 DNS servers Resolver list — without this, names don't resolve
15 Domain name Search suffix, e.g. corp.example.com
51 Lease time Seconds; drives T1/T2
53 Message type 1=DISCOVER … 5=ACK; every packet identifies itself
54 Server identifier Which server sent this — the client renews to this IP
55 Parameter request list What the client is asking for
61 Client identifier Usually the MAC; the stable identity for reservations

Option 53 deserves emphasis: every DHCP packet carries it. A sniffer identifying DHCP traffic reads option 53 first. Option 54 is the quiet hero of renewals — without it, the client wouldn't know whom to unicast the T1 REQUEST to.

Reservations vs exclusions

Both keep an address out of the dynamic pool, for opposite reasons.

  • Reservation: "always give this MAC this IP." The address stays in the pool's range but is pinned to one client identity (option 61 / MAC). Printers, servers, and anything with firewall rules pointing at it get reservations. The client still goes through DORA — it just always wins the same address.
  • Exclusion: "never hand out this range to anyone." Used for statically-assigned infrastructure: gateway .1, switches, access points. The DHCP server pretends those addresses don't exist.

Mixing them up is a classic operational error: excluding the printer's address and forgetting the static assignment creates a duplicate IP the moment someone's laptop grabs it from the pool.

Relay agents: DHCP across routers

DHCP DISCOVER is a broadcast, and broadcasts don't cross routers — the same wall ARP hits. The escape hatch is the relay agent (usually the router itself, via ip helper-address in Cisco parlance). It listens for DHCP broadcasts, wraps them as unicast to the real DHCP server (possibly subnets away), and relays the reply back. The giaddr field ("gateway IP") tells the server which subnet the request came from, so it picks an address from the right scope. One DHCP server can serve fifty VLANs this way. In interviews: "how does DHCP work across subnets?" → relay agent + giaddr.

INFORM: the shortcut for hosts that already have an address

Not every DHCP conversation is DORA. A host with a statically assigned address still needs the options — DNS servers, domain suffix, NTP. It sends a DHCPINFORM: "I have 10.20.7.50, don't offer me anything, just send the options." The server replies with an ACK carrying options and no yiaddr. Stateless DHCPv6 is the same idea in v6 (the O flag's whole purpose). INFORM matters operationally: it lets you centralize DNS and domain configuration even for devices that can't or shouldn't take dynamic addresses — and it generates no lease state on the server, so it scales trivially.

4. The exact structure: the DHCP packet

DHCP reuses the BOOTP format (RFC 2131 kept wire compatibility). The fixed header is 236 bytes, then the 4-byte magic cookie 99.130.83.99, then options:

Field Size Notes
op 1 byte 1 = request (client→server), 2 = reply
htype / hlen 1 + 1 Hardware type/length: 1 / 6 for Ethernet
hops 1 Incremented by each relay agent
xid 4 Transaction ID — random per conversation
secs 2 Seconds since client began acquiring
flags 2 High bit = "please broadcast your reply"
ciaddr 4 Client IP — filled only when renewing
yiaddr 4 "Your" IP — the offered address
siaddr 4 Server IP (next bootstrap server)
giaddr 4 Relay agent IP — zero if no relay
chaddr 16 Client hardware address (MAC in first 6)
sname 64 Server hostname (often zeroed)
file 128 Boot filename (legacy BOOTP)
magic cookie 4 99.130.83.99 — marks the start of options
options variable TLV list, ends with 255; padded to ≥300 bytes total

The script's output confirms the minimum size: both packets came out at exactly 300 bytes — the RFC's floor, padded with zeros. Two details worth noticing. First, ciaddr is zero in DISCOVER (the client has no address — that's the whole point) and filled in renewals. Second, the broadcast flag (0x8000) in the script's DISCOVER: early clients couldn't receive unicast before they had an IP, so they asked servers to broadcast replies; modern stacks handle unicast fine, but the flag persists.

5. A realistic example: laptop joins the office Wi-Fi

Monday, 9:02 AM. The laptop associates with the WPA3 network and the DHCP client wakes up:

  1. DISCOVER broadcast, xid=0x9f3a… The two DHCP servers (primary and failover peer) both hear it.
  2. OFFER × 2, milliseconds apart. Primary offers 10.20.7.144, mask /23, gateway 10.20.6.1, DNS 10.20.0.53, lease 8 hours. The failover offers 10.20.7.145 with identical options.
  3. REQUEST broadcast for 10.20.7.144. The failover server sees the choice and releases .145 back to its pool.
  4. ACK from primary. Lease committed until 5:02 PM.

At 1:02 PM (T1, half of 8h) the laptop unicasts a REQUEST to the primary: "extend 10.20.7.144?" ACK — new checkout 9:02 PM. The user never notices. At 5:02 PM the laptop sleeps; renewal stops mattering. Tuesday 9:02 AM it wakes, finds the lease expired, and DISCOVERs again — probably getting .144 back, since the server remembers recent grants.

Now the failure case: the primary DHCP server dies at noon. At 1:02 PM the T1 unicast goes unanswered. At 4:02 PM (T2 = 7/8 × 8h) the laptop broadcasts a rebind; the failover peer — authoritative for the same scope — ACKs. The user kept their address through a server outage. This is why DHCP failover pairs exist, and why T2 broadcasts instead of unicasting.

6. SLAAC: address without a server

Stateless Address Autoconfiguration (RFC 4862; the RA mechanics come from RFC 4861) lets an IPv6 host configure itself from a single Router Advertisement:

  1. The host sends a Router Solicitation (or just listens — routers multicast RAs periodically to ff02::1).
  2. The RA carries the prefix (e.g. 2001:db8:7::/64), the prefix lifetime, and two flags that matter enormously: A (autonomous — use this prefix for SLAAC) and M/O (managed/other — "also ask DHCPv6 for address / other config").
  3. The host forms its address: prefix + 64-bit interface identifier. It then runs Duplicate Address Detection (an NS for its own tentative address — Module 06's NS/NA in action) before using it.

EUI-64 vs privacy extensions

The interface identifier has two schools:

  • EUI-64 (classic): derived from the MAC — split the 48-bit MAC, insert ff:fe in the middle, flip one bit. The address permanently encodes the hardware identity. Great for servers (stable, derivable). Terrible for laptops and phones: the same identifier appears on every network, so the device is trackable across Wi-Fi networks by its lower 64 bits. This was a genuine privacy problem, not a theoretical one.
  • Privacy extensions (RFC 4941, Narten et al., September 2007): the host generates a random interface identifier, rotates it periodically, and uses it for outgoing connections. The EUI-64 address (or a stable random one, per RFC 7217) stays for incoming. Every modern OS enables this by default.

In interviews: "why do my IPv6 addresses keep changing?" → privacy extensions rotating the interface ID. "Why does the server's stay fixed?" → EUI-64 or static assignment.

The RA flags decide the DHCPv6 question: M=1 means "get your address from DHCPv6 too" (stateful), O=1 means "use SLAAC for the address but ask DHCPv6 for DNS and friends" (stateless DHCPv6). Most enterprise networks run SLAAC + stateless DHCPv6: self-configured addresses, centrally managed DNS.

DHCPv6 in one paragraph

DHCPv6 (RFC 8415, 2018 — it obsoleted RFC 3315) is a separate protocol, not a port of DHCPv4. It runs over UDP ports 546 (client) and 547 (server), uses multicast (ff02::1:2, all-DHCP-agents) instead of broadcast, and identifies clients by DUID rather than MAC. The message names changed too: SOLICIT / ADVERTISE / REQUEST / REPLY mirror DORA's shape. You need to know it exists, when the M/O flags invoke it, and that it's the only way to hand out DNS in a pure-DHCPv6 network — SLAAC alone can't.

When DORA goes wrong: NAK, DECLINE, and exhaustion

DORA has two more messages for the unhappy paths, and interviewers ask about both:

  • DHCPNAK. The server refuses the REQUEST — the client asked for an address that's wrong for this subnet (it moved networks with a stale lease) or no longer valid. The client must drop the address and restart at DISCOVER. A NAK storm after a subnet renumber is the signature of clients holding pre-renumber leases.
  • DHCPDECLINE. The client refuses the offer — it ARPed the offered address (a courtesy check before accepting) and someone answered. The address is in use, so the client declines and the server marks it as conflicted. DECLINE is the protocol's immune response to duplicate addresses.

Scope exhaustion math. A /24 holds 254 usable addresses. Subtract the gateway, subtract exclusions for static infrastructure, subtract reservations — a "254-address" scope often offers ~230. With an 8-hour lease, a lobby with 300 daily visitors and 200 devices present at once fits. The same lobby with a 7-day lease accumulates every visitor's phone for a week and exhausts the scope by Wednesday. The fix is rarely "a bigger subnet" — it's matching lease length to churn, exactly the tradeoff from the T1/T2 section.

Option 82: the relay agent signs its work

When a relay agent forwards a DISCOVER, it can append option 82 (relay agent information). It records the circuit ID — which switch port the request arrived on — and the remote ID, which identifies the relay. The server can then make policy decisions from that data. "Ports in building B get the guest scope." "This circuit gets a 1-hour lease." The server echoes option 82 back, and the relay strips it before delivering to the client. The client never sees it. This is how ISPs and campuses tie DHCP policy to physical location without per-port server configuration.

The RA flag table

Module 06 introduced Router Advertisements; here is the complete flag picture, because "what do the M and O flags do" is a genuine interview question:

Flag Bit Meaning
A (Autonomous) per-prefix Use this prefix for SLAAC
M (Managed) RA header Get your address from DHCPv6 (stateful)
O (Other) RA header Use SLAAC for the address, DHCPv6 for the rest (stateless)

Common combinations: A=1, M=0, O=1 — the enterprise default. Hosts self-address via SLAAC and fetch DNS from stateless DHCPv6. M=1 means full stateful DHCPv6 (address from the server, like v4). A=0 with M=0, O=0 means "here's a default router and nothing else" — a stub announcement. When IPv6 "works but DNS doesn't," check the O flag and whether a stateless DHCPv6 server is actually answering.

7. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"DHCP assigns the gateway." DHCP tells the client the gateway's address (option 3). The gateway exists independently. If option 3 is wrong or missing, the client has a valid IP and can ARP its neighbors — but nothing leaves the subnet.

"A reservation means the device skips DORA." No — it still does the full exchange every time. The reservation only guarantees the outcome. This surprises people debugging slow boots: the delay is DORA itself, not address selection.

"Long leases are always better." Long leases reduce broadcast churn and keep addresses stable, but they also pin addresses to devices that left weeks ago. A conference room with a 7-day lease and 200 weekly visitors exhausts a /24 fast. Match lease length to churn.

"169.254.x.x means DHCP is broken." It means DHCP didn't answer — APIPA (Automatic Private IP Addressing) is the client's fallback, a self-assigned link-local address. The DHCP server might be fine; the relay agent, the VLAN trunk, or the switch port could be the actual fault. Diagnose the path, not just the server.

"SLAAC gives you everything DHCP does." SLAAC gives an address, a prefix length, and a default router (from the RA). It does not give DNS — that needs the O flag + stateless DHCPv6, or RDNSS in the RA (RFC 8106), which many networks don't send. "IPv6 works but names don't resolve" is usually this.

"DHCPv6 is just DHCP for v6." Different packet format, different ports, different client identity, multicast instead of broadcast. Treat it as a cousin, not a translation.

8. Interview Q&A

Q1: Why does DHCP need four messages? Why not just DISCOVER → ACK?

Because multiple servers can hear the DISCOVER. The OFFER is tentative — the server hasn't committed. The REQUEST lets the client pick one offer and tells the losers to stand down (it's broadcast for exactly this reason). Collapse it to two messages and two servers would both believe they leased to the client.

Q2: A client keeps its IP for weeks despite an 8-hour lease. How?

T1 renewal. Every 4 hours it unicasts a REQUEST to the original server and gets a fresh 8-hour lease. The address only changes if renewals fail past T2 and expiry. Stable IPs on DHCP are normal — that's the design working, not a stuck lease.

Q3: How does one DHCP server serve fifty VLANs?

Relay agents. Each VLAN's router interface catches the broadcast DISCOVER, unicasts it to the server with giaddr set to the interface's address. The server uses giaddr to select the right scope and replies to the relay, which delivers it to the client. The server never sees a broadcast.

Q4: Your laptop gets 169.254.10.5. Walk me through the diagnosis.

That's APIPA — DORA produced no ACK. Check in order: is the link actually up (cable/VLAN)? Did the DISCOVER leave (packet capture)? Is a relay agent configured on this subnet's gateway? Is the DHCP server's scope exhausted (check the pool)? Is there a rogue server answering with NAKs? The address tells you that DHCP failed; the path tells you where.

Q5: When would you choose SLAAC over DHCPv6, and what do you lose?

SLAAC for simple, high-churn, or serverless networks — no state to manage, no server to fail, hosts self-configure from one RA. You lose central control: no reservations, no audit trail of who had which address, and no DNS unless you add stateless DHCPv6 or RDNSS. Regulated environments usually pick stateful DHCPv6 for the logs alone.

9. Try it

Run on your Mac — your output will differ.

# The DHCP deal your Mac actually accepted — packet by packet
ipconfig getpacket en0 | head -30
# Just the DNS servers DHCP handed you (option 6)
ipconfig getoption en0 domain_name_server
# Force a fresh DORA exchange (drops and re-acquires the lease)
sudo ipconfig set en0 DHCP
# then re-run: ipconfig getpacket en0
# IPv6: the Router Advertisements your Mac is hearing (SLAAC inputs)
ndp -r | head -10
# Build and decode real DHCP packets locally (no network needed)
~/.local/bin/python3.13 ~/workspace/build/networking-guide/code/m07/dhcp_packet.py

Module 08 — VLANs & Layer-2 Reliability: Dividing the Switch, Taming the Loop

Frame formats and protocol logic in this module are exercised by two scripts: code/m08/dot1q.py (inserts, parses, and strips real 802.1Q tags with struct) and code/m08/stp_election.py (models the root bridge election and port roles in pure logic). All runs below are pasted from their actual output.

1. Why it matters

A 48-port switch is one broadcast domain. Every ARP request, every DHCP discover, every multicast shout reaches all 48 ports. That was fine in 1995. It is not fine when those 48 ports hold the finance team, the guest Wi-Fi, the VoIP phones, and the server iDRAC interfaces. A misbehaving device in one group can eavesdrop on or flood the others.

VLANs fix this without buying more switches. One physical switch becomes several logical switches, each its own broadcast domain, each isolated at Layer 2. Traffic between VLANs must go up to a router (or a Layer-3 switch), which means it passes ACLs and policy. Segmentation without new hardware — that is the entire value proposition.

But switches introduce a second problem: redundancy creates loops, and loops at Layer 2 are catastrophic in a way loops at Layer 3 are not. IP has TTL; a routed loop dies after 255 hops. Ethernet has no TTL. A switched loop circulates frames forever, multiplying broadcasts until the network melts. The Spanning Tree Protocol exists to make redundant links safe. Every enterprise network runs it, usually without anyone thinking about it — until a misconfigured port takes down a building.

2. The mental model

Think of a VLAN as painting ports different colors.

Take a 24-port switch. Paint ports 1–8 blue (Engineering), 9–16 green (Sales), 17–24 red (Guest). A frame entering a blue port may leave through blue ports only. The switch enforces the color rule in hardware, at wire speed. Green never sees blue's broadcasts. Red never sees anyone's.

Now stretch the colors across two switches in different closets. A single cable connects them — the trunk. Frames crossing it carry a little colored sticker: the 802.1Q tag. The far switch reads the sticker and delivers the frame to the matching color. The sticker is 4 bytes, added on ingress to the trunk and stripped on egress. End hosts never see it.

Spanning Tree is the fire marshal for this building. Redundant links between switches are good — a cable cut shouldn't isolate a floor. But two paths between the same switches form a loop. STP walks the building and picks one switch as the reference point: the root bridge. Then it orders every other switch to block the ports that would complete a loop, keeping them ready as hot standbys. If the active path fails, the blocked port unblocks. Loops never form; redundancy still works.

3. How it works

The 802.1Q tag

IEEE 802.1Q (the standard is "IEEE 802.1Q — Bridges and Bridged Networks") defines a 4-byte tag inserted between the source MAC address and the EtherType/length field of an Ethernet frame. Three sub-fields:

  • TPID — Tag Protocol Identifier, 16 bits. Always 0x8100. This is how a switch recognizes "a tag follows" instead of an EtherType. (A related value, 0x88a8, marks provider-bridging / QinQ outer tags — same idea, one more layer.)
  • PCP — Priority Code Point, 3 bits. 8 priority levels (0–7) for Quality of Service at Layer 2. Voice traffic is typically marked 5 or 6 so a congested switch queues it first. This is the only QoS signal Ethernet itself carries.
  • DEI — Drop Eligible Indicator, 1 bit. It marks the frame as expendable: "under congestion, drop this one first." (Called CFI, Canonical Format Indicator, in the original Ethernet-only version of the standard.)
  • VID — VLAN Identifier, 12 bits. The color: which VLAN this frame belongs to. 0 means "no VLAN, tag carries priority only"; 4095 is reserved; the rest — 1 through 4094 — are usable. 4094 VLANs per trunk, from 12 bits.

From the verifier script:

802.1Q tag: TPID=0x8100  PCP=5 (3 bits)  DEI=0 (1 bit)  VID=100 (12 bits)
frame: 74 bytes untagged -> 78 bytes tagged -> 74 bytes after strip (identical)
reserved VIDs 0 (priority-tagged) and 4095 parse back intact
usable VLAN IDs per trunk: 4094 (2^12 - 0 - 4095)

The tag costs exactly 4 bytes and stripping restores the frame bit-for-bit — the script asserts both.

Access vs trunk ports

Every switch port is one of two kinds:

  • Access port: belongs to exactly one VLAN. Hosts plug in here — laptops, printers, servers. Frames enter untagged and leave untagged. The switch knows the port's VLAN from configuration and applies it internally. The host has no idea VLANs exist.
  • Trunk port: carries many VLANs. Switch-to-switch (and switch-to-router, switch-to-hypervisor) links live here. Frames cross with their 802.1Q tags intact, so the far side can sort them by color.

The terms "access" and "trunk" are Cisco's vocabulary, but every vendor has the equivalent (some say "untagged/tagged port"). The concept is universal; the word is convention. In an interview, either term is fine as long as the behavior is right.

The native VLAN and its mismatch pitfall

A trunk can designate one VLAN as native: frames of that VLAN cross the trunk untagged. This exists for backward compatibility with devices that don't understand tags (old IP phones, some hypervisors).

The pitfall: the native VLAN is a per-port configuration, and the two ends of a trunk must agree. If switch A's trunk says native VLAN 1 and switch B's says native VLAN 10, then untagged frames from A's VLAN 1 land in B's VLAN 10 — two broadcast domains silently merged. Traffic leaks across a boundary the designer believed was sealed. Worse, it often mostly works, because return traffic may take a different path — the classic "intermittent weirdness" ticket.

Two rules prevent it. First, make the native VLAN match on both ends. Better still, set the native VLAN to an unused, dedicated ID — often called a "black hole" or dummy VLAN. Then a mismatch fails closed instead of merging real traffic. Second, never carry user data on the native VLAN. management traffic on native VLAN 1 with default settings everywhere is how VLAN-hopping attacks start.

Why broadcast domains matter

Each VLAN is a separate broadcast domain. This is not just tidiness — it is a scaling and safety property:
