The browser checks its own cache, then asks the OS stub resolver, which forwards to the configured recursive resolver. Cold cache: the resolver queries a root server (gets a referral to .com), queries the .com TLD servers (referral to example.com's nameservers), queries the authoritative server (gets the A/AAAA record plus TTL), caches everything at each level, and returns the address. The browser then opens a TCP connection to that IP — DNS's job is done before the handshake starts. Strong answers mention: each layer caches (second visit skips to step one), and the browser may try IPv6 (AAAA) and IPv4 (A) in parallel via Happy Eyeballs.

Q2: Why does DNS use UDP, and when does it fall back to TCP?

UDP because queries are small, idempotent, and latency-sensitive — a handshake would triple the packets for one question and one answer, and a lost query is just re-asked. TCP when the answer doesn't fit in a UDP datagram: the server sets the TC (truncated) bit and the client retries over TCP. Also always TCP for zone transfers between nameservers, and for the encrypted transports (DNS over TLS/HTTPS). The classic UDP size ceiling was 512 bytes; EDNS0 negotiates larger.

Q3: You changed an A record to point at a new server, but half your users still reach the old one an hour later. Why, and how do you prevent it next time?

Cached answers with the old TTL haven't expired in resolvers, OS caches, and browsers worldwide. DNS has no invalidation — only expiry. Prevention: lower the TTL (e.g., to 300s) at least one old-TTL in advance of the change, then make the change, then raise the TTL back. Bonus: mention negative caching — if the name briefly didn't resolve during the change, NXDOMAIN answers are cached too.

Q4: What's the difference between a recursive resolver and an authoritative nameserver?

The authoritative server owns a zone's records and answers definitively (the aa flag in dig). The recursive resolver owns nothing — it walks the tree on the client's behalf (root → TLD → authoritative), caches aggressively, and returns the final answer. Your laptop talks to a recursive resolver; the resolver talks to authoritative servers. Confusing them leads to debugging the wrong machine.

Q5: What does a CNAME do, and where can't you use one?

A CNAME makes one name an alias for another: lookups for the alias restart at the target name. It's how shop.example.com can point at a CDN hostname without tracking the CDN's IPs. You can't use one at the zone apex (example.com itself) because a CNAME can't coexist with the NS and SOA records the apex requires — that's a protocol rule, not a vendor quirk. (Providers' ALIAS/ANAME records work around it by resolving the target server-side; that's vendor-specific behavior.)

8. Try it

Each command is labeled run on your Mac — your output will differ. The sandbox has no internet, so live DNS is a Mac exercise throughout.

Trace a full resolution chain:

dig +trace www.example.com          # run on your Mac — your output will differ

This walks the tree manually: root → TLD → authoritative, exactly §3.1's diagram. Watch the referrals turn into answers.

See the raw answer with TTLs and flags:

dig www.example.com                 # run on your Mac — your output will differ

Read the ANSWER SECTION: the number before IN A is the TTL as the resolver sees it — query twice in a row and watch it count down. The flags line shows aa only when talking to an authoritative server directly.

Ask an authoritative server directly:

dig @ns1.example.com www.example.com   # run on your Mac — substitute a real NS; your output will differ

Compare with the resolver's answer. The aa (authoritative answer) flag appears here.

Look up the less-famous record types:

dig MX example.com +short            # run on your Mac — your output will differ
dig TXT example.com +short           # run on your Mac — your output will differ
dig -x 93.184.216.34 +short         # run on your Mac — reverse lookup (PTR); your output will differ

Run the offline Python examples:

python3 code/m11/dns_resolve.py      # runs anywhere — uses localhost only
python3 code/m11/dns_ttl_cache.py    # runs anywhere — simulates TTL expiry

Then, on your Mac with internet, try the real thing and compare:

python3 -c "import socket; print(socket.getaddrinfo('example.com', 443)[:2])"   # run on your Mac — your output will differ

Standards cited: RFC 1034 (concepts) and RFC 1035 (message format) — the original DNS specs; EDNS0: RFC 6891; DNS over TLS: RFC 7858; DNS over HTTPS: RFC 8484. CNAME-at-apex and MX-to-CNAME restrictions are protocol rules from the base specs; ALIAS/ANAME records are vendor-specific.

Module 12 — HTTP & the Web

How the web's application protocol actually speaks: message anatomy, methods, status codes, persistent connections, and the TLS 1.3 handshake that wraps it all.

1. Why it matters

Every backend you will ever build sits behind HTTP. Load balancers health-check it. CDNs cache it. API gateways route it. Browsers, CLIs, and microservices all speak it. You can know distributed systems cold and still get bitten by HTTP semantics — a cache that ignores your POST, a retry that double-charges a customer, a "secure" cookie that leaks over a downgraded connection.

Interviewers probe HTTP for a specific reason. It is the layer where protocol theory meets production consequences. "Is PUT idempotent?" is not trivia. It decides whether your client can safely retry a timed-out request. "What does 429 mean?" decides whether your backoff logic even triggers. This module builds the mental model first, then the exact bytes, then the judgment calls.

What this module covers: the anatomy of a request and a response, what each method means (semantics, not just syntax), the status codes that actually matter, persistent connections, and the TLS 1.3 handshake — the encrypted tunnel almost every HTTP conversation now runs inside. Citations are to the real standards: RFC 9110 (HTTP semantics) and RFC 8446 (TLS 1.3).

2. The mental model

HTTP is a request/response protocol over a reliable byte stream. That is the whole idea, and everything else is detail.

A client opens a connection — usually a TCP connection, nowadays often wrapped in TLS — and sends a message: "do this thing." The server reads the message, does the thing (or refuses), and sends a message back: "here is what happened." Then, usually, they do it again on the same connection.

Three properties of this model explain most of HTTP's design:

It is stateless. Each request stands alone. The server is not required to remember anything between requests. State exists anyway — cookies, tokens, sessions — but it is carried in the messages, not in the connection. This is what lets a load balancer spray your requests across a fleet of servers. Any server can handle any request, because the request carries its own context.

It is text-framed but byte-exact. The start line and headers are human-readable text. The body is opaque bytes whose meaning comes from the headers (Content-Type, Content-Length). The boundary between headers and body is a single blank line. There is no envelope, no length prefix on the message, no binary framing in HTTP/1.1. The parser reads headers line by line until the blank line, then counts exactly Content-Length bytes. Everything after that is the next message.

Methods have semantics, not just names. GET does not merely "fetch." It promises to be safe (no side effects) and idempotent (repeatable without additional effect). The protocol's guarantees — caching, retrying, prefetching — all rest on these promises. Break the promise (a GET that deletes a row) and the infrastructure built on it will eventually hurt you.

3. How it works

The anatomy of a message

Every HTTP/1 message, request or response, has the same three-part shape: a start line, a block of headers, and an optional body. The diagram shows both sides of one exchange — a client creating an order, a server confirming it.

A few things in that diagram deserve emphasis because they are the source of real bugs.

The request line has three tokens: method, target, version. The target is usually just the path (/orders), not the full URL — the host comes from the Host header. (An absolute URL in the request line is legal; proxies use it. Servers behind proxies must be careful which one they trust.)

Header names are case-insensitive (content-type ≡ Content-Type), but values are usually case-sensitive. A surprising amount of hand-rolled parsing code gets this wrong.

The reason phrase (Created, Not Found) is cosmetic. Only the numeric code has meaning. Clients must never switch on the phrase.

And the body is optional on both sides. A GET response usually has one; a 204 response must not. A GET request with a body is technically allowed by the spec but widely ignored or rejected — proxies and servers routinely drop it. Do not design an API that needs one.

Methods: what each one means

The method is the verb of the request, and each verb carries a contract. Two properties matter most:

  • Safe — the method must not change server state. GET and HEAD are safe. Safe methods can be prefetched, crawled, and replayed without consequence.
  • Idempotent — repeating the request has the same effect as doing it once. PUT and DELETE are idempotent; POST and PATCH are not. Idempotent methods can be retried after a network failure without fear of double effects.
Method Meaning (RFC 9110 semantics) Safe Idempotent Notes
GET Retrieve a representation of the target resource yes yes The workhorse. Cacheable by default. Must not have side effects — a GET that sends email is a protocol violation, and crawlers will trigger it.
HEAD Same as GET, but the server sends headers only, no body yes yes "Does this exist, and how big is it?" Used for existence checks and cache validation.
POST Ask the server to process the enclosed representation no no The general-purpose "do something" verb: create subordinate resources, trigger actions, submit forms. Not idempotent — retrying a POST can double-create. (Real-world practice: APIs return 201 with a Location header on create; idempotency keys, sent as a header like Idempotency-Key, are the industry fix for safe retries.)
PUT Replace the target resource with the enclosed representation no yes "Store exactly this at this URL." Repeating it converges — the tenth identical PUT leaves the same state as the first. The classic interview answer for "which method do retries use."
PATCH Apply a partial modification to the resource no no Carries a diff (JSON Merge Patch, JSON Patch), not a full replacement. Not idempotent in general — applying "increment by 1" twice increments twice.
DELETE Remove the target resource (or schedule its removal) no yes Repeating it converges: after the first delete, the resource is gone; later deletes are no-ops. (Servers often return 404 on the second delete — that is still idempotent in effect.)
OPTIONS Ask what the server supports for this resource yes yes Powers CORS preflight: the browser asks "may I send this cross-origin request?" before sending it. Also used for capability discovery.

A note on reality vs. the RFC: the spec defines these semantics, but nothing enforces them. Frameworks will happily let you delete a database row in a GET handler. The enforcement comes from the ecosystem — caches, crawlers, browsers, and retry logic all assume you kept the contract. The interview insight is exactly this: method semantics are a coordination mechanism between parties that never meet.

Status codes: the ones that matter

Codes are grouped by class. The first digit tells you who should act next.

1xx — Informational. Rarely seen directly. 100 Continue lets a client ask "may I send this large body?" before uploading it; the server answers 100 (go ahead) or an error. 101 Switching Protocols is how WebSocket upgrades begin.

2xx — Success. 200 OK (here is your representation), 201 Created (a new resource exists; look at the Location header), 202 Accepted (I took your request; it is queued — common for async work), 204 No Content (success, and deliberately no body — the correct answer for "deleted" or "nothing to return"). Note: 204 must not include a body, and many clients break if you add one.

3xx — Redirection. The client must do something else to finish. 301 Moved Permanently and 308 Permanent Redirect are cacheable and mean "update your bookmarks" — the difference: 308 preserves the method, 301 historically allowed changing POST to GET (and browsers do). 302 Found / 303 See Other / 307 Temporary Redirect are the temporary equivalents, with the same method-preservation split (307 preserves, 302/303 may rewrite to GET). 304 Not Modified is special: it is not really a redirect — it is a cache revalidation answer meaning "your cached copy is still good, use it." (Module 13 lives here.)

4xx — Client error. The request was bad; retrying it unchanged will fail the same way. 400 Bad Request (malformed), 401 Unauthorized (no/invalid credentials — unauthenticated, despite the name), 403 Forbidden (authenticated but not allowed — unauthorized, despite the name; the naming is famously backwards), 404 Not Found, 405 Method Not Allowed (the resource exists but not for this verb), 409 Conflict (state conflict — e.g., version mismatch on update), 410 Gone (deliberately removed; stronger than 404), 412 Precondition Failed (your If-Match didn't hold — the optimistic-locking status), 415 Unsupported Media Type, 422 Unprocessable Content (syntactically fine, semantically wrong — the validation-error status; it lived in WebDAV's RFC 4918 before RFC 9110 adopted it), 429 Too Many Requests (slow down; check Retry-After).

5xx — Server error. The server failed; the client may retry. 500 Internal Server Error (something broke; details belong in logs, not the response), 502 Bad Gateway (an upstream — your app server, a proxy target — gave an invalid response), 503 Service Unavailable (temporarily overloaded or down; Retry-After tells the client when to come back — this is the status load balancers watch), 504 Gateway Timeout (upstream too slow).

The interview-grade distinctions: 401 vs 403 (authentication vs authorization — say this confidently), 301 vs 302 vs 307/308 (permanent vs temporary, method preservation), 404 vs 410 (unknown vs deliberately gone), and 503 with Retry-After (the "back off politely" contract).

Persistent connections and keep-alive

HTTP/1.0 opened a new TCP connection per request. That was ruinous: every request paid a TCP handshake, and under TLS, a TLS handshake too. HTTP/1.1 made persistent connections the default — after one response, the connection stays open and the next request reuses it. The Connection: keep-alive header (a 1.0-ism) and Connection: close (opt out) still exist, but in 1.1 you assume reuse unless told otherwise.

Why this matters beyond latency: connection reuse changes server capacity planning. A server holding 10,000 idle keep-alive connections is spending file descriptors and memory, not CPU. That is why servers cap idle time (KeepAliveTimeout in Apache, keepalive_timeout in nginx, typically 5–75 seconds) and why load balancers multiplex. It is also why a slow client can be a denial-of-service vector — Slowloris dribbles headers to hold connections open, which is exactly the attack keep-alive timeouts defend against.

HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 go further — multiplexing many streams over one connection — but the principle is the same: the expensive thing is establishing the channel, so amortize it.

4. The exact structure: TLS 1.3 handshake

Almost every HTTP conversation you care about runs inside TLS. The handshake is what turns "anyone on the path can read this" into "only the two endpoints can." TLS 1.3 (RFC 8446, 2018) cut the handshake to one round trip and removed the legacy cipher suites and renegotiation tricks that caused a decade of vulnerabilities.

Here is the message flow, exactly as the RFC defines it:

Walk through it slowly, because the elegance is in what got removed.

ClientHello. The client announces what it supports: TLS versions, cipher suites, and — the TLS 1.3 innovation — a key_share: its half of a Diffie-Hellman key exchange, sent speculatively, before knowing which group the server will pick. It also sends server_name (SNI), which tells the server which certificate to present. SNI is sent in the clear, which is why censors and corporate middleboxes can see which site you are visiting even over TLS. (Encrypted ClientHello exists — RFC 9258 — but deployment is still early.)

ServerHello. The server picks the parameters and sends its own key_share. At this point both sides can compute the same shared secret. Everything after this is encrypted. Note what is missing compared to TLS 1.2: no separate ServerKeyExchange round trip, no cipher-suite negotiation ping-pong. One flight each way.

The encrypted flight. Four messages, all encrypted with keys derived from the shared secret: EncryptedExtensions (negotiated extensions), Certificate (the server's certificate chain), CertificateVerify (a signature proving the server holds the private key matching the certificate), and the server's Finished (a MAC over the whole transcript, proving no tampering). This is also where the handshake authenticates the server. Client authentication (mutual TLS) is possible here too, but rare on the public web — it is common inside service meshes.

Client Finished. The client sends its own MAC over the transcript. Now both sides have authenticated the handshake integrity, and application data flows.

Why 1-RTT matters. TLS 1.2 needed two round trips. On a 100 ms path, that is 200 ms of handshake before the first HTTP byte — plus the TCP handshake's own RTT. TLS 1.3's single round trip roughly halves connection-setup latency. For short-lived connections (APIs, mobile apps on flaky networks), this is a large fraction of total request time.

0-RTT and what it trades away. If the client has talked to the server before, it holds a pre-shared key (PSK) from a previous session. It can send application data in the first flight, alongside the ClientHello — zero round trips. The trade is replayability. The server has not yet proven freshness when it receives early data, so an attacker who captures the flight can replay it. The server will process it twice. That is safe for an idempotent GET of a logo. It is catastrophic for "transfer $100." The RFC is explicit: 0-RTT must only carry idempotent operations, and servers should (and commonly do) disable it or restrict it. Many CDNs and servers turn 0-RTT off by default. The honest summary: 0-RTT trades replay safety for latency, and the industry mostly decided the trade is not worth it except in narrow cases.

Certificates in plain language

A certificate answers one question: is this public key really owned by this name? When your browser connects to bank.example, the server presents a certificate saying "the holder of this public key is bank.example," signed by a certificate authority (CA) your system already trusts.

The chain of trust works like references on a résumé. Your OS or browser ships with a list of ~100 root CAs it trusts. The server's certificate is typically signed not by a root directly but by an intermediate CA, which is signed by the root. Your client verifies the chain link by link: server cert → intermediate → root in the trust store. If any link breaks — expired, wrong name, unknown issuer — the connection fails closed.

Three practical things architects should know:

  1. The CA does not make the connection secure. It makes the identity trustworthy. Encryption without authentication is trivially man-in-the-middled; the certificate binds the encryption to the name you asked for.
  2. Hostname matching is the check that matters. The certificate must name the host you connected to (via Subject Alternative Names today). A valid cert for the wrong name is correctly rejected — this is what stops most phishing-adjacent attacks at the TLS layer.
  3. Short lifetimes are the trend. Public certs now max out at 398 days, and the industry is moving toward 90 days and below. Automation (ACME/Let's Encrypt) is not optional anymore; manual renewal is how outages happen.

5. A realistic example

The best way to internalize the message anatomy is to watch real bytes. This example runs entirely on localhost — no internet needed. It starts http.server in a thread, fetches with http.client (two requests over one persistent connection), prints what the server saw, then drops to a raw socket to show the exact wire bytes.

Run it: ~/.local/bin/python3.13 code/m12/m12_http_localhost.py

Real output (your port numbers will differ):

== client request #1 ==
HTTP/1.1  200 OK
Server: BaseHTTP/0.6 Python/3.13.16
Date: Fri, 16 Oct 2026 17:02:31 GMT
Content-Type: application/json
Content-Length: 19
ETag: "abc123"
Cache-Control: public, max-age=60
body: {"hello": "world"}

== server-side view of the two requests ==
-- request #1 from 127.0.0.1:49864 --
request line: 'GET /doc HTTP/1.1'
  Host: 127.0.0.1:40879
  Accept-Encoding: identity
  Accept: application/json

-- request #2 from 127.0.0.1:49864 --
request line: 'GET /doc HTTP/1.1'
  ...

Three things to notice. First, the message shape from the diagram is exactly what you see: status line, headers, blank line, body. Second, both requests arrived from the same client port (49864) — that is the persistent connection at work. One TCP connection, two request/response cycles. (Python's http.server defaults to HTTP/1.0, which closes after each response; the example sets protocol_version = "HTTP/1.1" so the connection stays open — a detail worth knowing when you read framework code.) Third, the raw-socket section shows there is no magic: GET /doc HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: ...\r\n\r\n is the entire request. You could type it into nc by hand.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"HTTPS is a different protocol." No. HTTPS is HTTP inside a TLS connection. The HTTP semantics — methods, headers, status codes — are identical. The s buys confidentiality, integrity, and server authentication, not new application behavior.

"The status code text matters." The reason phrase (Not Found, Created) is cosmetic and not standardized. A server may legally send 404 Enjoy Your Day. Code that branches on the phrase instead of the number is broken by design.

"POST is for create, PUT is for update." This is the folk version. The real distinction is semantics: PUT means replace the resource at this URL (idempotent), POST means process this (not idempotent). You can create with PUT (client-chosen URL, e.g. PUT /users/123) and you can update with POST. Interviewers asking "design an idempotent create endpoint" want PUT with a client-generated ID, or POST with an idempotency key — either is defensible if you can explain the trade-off.

"Idempotent means safe to retry in all cases." Idempotent means repeating the request converges on the same server state. It does not mean the retry is free: a retried PUT still costs a full request, and a DELETE that returns 404 on the second call can confuse clients that treat 404 as failure. Retry logic should treat 404-after-DELETE as success.

"Keep-alive means the connection never closes." It means the connection may be reused. Either side can close it at any time (Connection: close, idle timeouts, server restarts). Robust clients handle a closed persistent connection by transparently opening a new one — this is the "retry once on a fresh connection" logic buried in every HTTP library.

"TLS 1.3's 0-RTT is free performance." It replays. Unless you have audited every endpoint that could receive early data for idempotency, leave it off. Most production TLS terminators do.

"A valid certificate means the site is trustworthy." It means the site proved control of the name. Phishing sites get valid certificates routinely (the CA verifies domain control, not legitimacy). The padlock means "encrypted to the named party," not "this party is honest."

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Your client POSTs a payment, the connection drops before the response arrives. What do you do?

A: You cannot know whether the server processed it — the classic Two Generals situation at the application layer. The correct design avoids the dilemma instead of solving it: send an Idempotency-Key header with the POST. The server stores the key with the result; a retried request with the same key returns the original result instead of double-charging. If the API does not support idempotency keys, the fallback is read-before-write: GET the order/payment state first, and only POST if it is absent. Never blind-retry a non-idempotent POST.

Q2: Why is PUT idempotent but PATCH not?

A: PUT carries the complete replacement state — applying "the resource is now exactly X" ten times leaves X. PATCH carries a change description, and change descriptions do not necessarily converge: "increment the counter" applied twice increments twice; "append to the list" applied twice appends twice. A PATCH can be idempotent if the operation is written that way (e.g., JSON Merge Patch setting a field to a fixed value is idempotent), but the method makes no promise, so intermediaries and clients must not assume it.

Q3: Walk me through what happens — at the protocol level — when a browser requests https://example.com/logo.png for the first time.

A: DNS resolves the name (Module 11 territory). TCP three-way handshake: 1 RTT. TLS 1.3 handshake: ClientHello → ServerHello → encrypted flight (certificate, proof of private-key possession) → client Finished: 1 more RTT. Now the HTTP request goes inside the encrypted tunnel: GET /logo.png HTTP/1.1 with headers. The server responds 200 OK with Content-Type, Content-Length, caching headers, and the body. Total before the first app byte: ~2 RTTs plus DNS. A strong answer names the round trips and notes that connection reuse and TLS resumption exist precisely to avoid paying them again.

Q4: A GET /report endpoint takes 30 seconds to generate and clients time out and retry, spawning duplicate expensive jobs. Fix it at the HTTP layer.

A: Make the operation asynchronous and the protocol honest about it. POST /reports returns 202 Accepted with a Location header pointing at a status resource (/reports/abc123). The client polls it with GET (safe, idempotent, cacheable — Cache-Control: no-cache or short max-age). When done, the status resource returns 303 See Other to the finished artifact, or simply includes the result. Retried POSTs are deduplicated with an idempotency key. Timeouts stop spawning duplicate work because the expensive work is keyed to the accepted request, not to each attempt.

Q5: What does the TLS handshake authenticate, and what does it not authenticate?

A: It authenticates the server's identity (certificate chain to a trusted root, hostname match) and the integrity of the handshake itself (the Finished MACs cover the transcript). It does not authenticate the client (unless mutual TLS is configured), it does not vouch for the server's honesty or the content's truth, and in TLS 1.3 with SNI it does not hide which site you are visiting from a network observer. Encryption without the certificate check is worthless — that is why clients must fail closed on verification errors rather than offering "click through."

8. Try it

Each command is labeled. Run on your Mac — your output will differ (ports, dates, timing).

  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Watch a real HTTPS handshake's round trips: curl -w 'connect=%{time_connect} appconnect=%{time_appconnect} total=%{time_total}\n' -o /dev/null -s https://example.com time_appconnect minus time_connect is roughly the TLS handshake time. Compare a fresh connection against a resumed one with curl --tlsv1.3 (session resumption is negotiated automatically on the second run).
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. See the exact request your client sends: curl -v https://example.com 2>&1 | head -20 The lines starting with > are the request line and headers — compare them against the anatomy diagram.
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Inspect a real certificate chain: openssl s_client -connect example.com:443 -servername example.com 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -subject -issuer -dates Then add -showcerts to see the intermediate chain the server sends.
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Prove keep-alive reuse with timing: curl -w 'reused=%{num_connects}\n' -o /dev/null -s https://example.com https://example.com One connection serving two requests shows num_connects as 1.
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Run the module's localhost example (works offline): ~/.local/bin/python3.13 ~/workspace/build/networking-guide/code/m12/m12_http_localhost.py

Standards cited: RFC 9110 (HTTP Semantics), RFC 8446 (TLS 1.3). Both are real, current, and worth reading at least once — RFC 8446's design rationale section is one of the best-written protocol documents in existence.

Module 13 — CDN & Caching

Why the internet keeps copies of everything: what a CDN is for, how anycast steers you to the nearest copy, the cache headers that control it all, and the revalidation dance that keeps copies honest.

1. Why it matters

Physics sets a floor on latency. Light in fiber travels about 200 km per millisecond, and a request from São Paulo to a server in Virginia crosses ~7,700 km — that is ~40 ms each way before queues, TLS, or application work. No protocol tweak beats geography. The only fix is to move the bytes closer to the user.

That is what a CDN does, and it solves two problems at once. The first is latency: serve the response from a data center 10 ms away instead of 150 ms away. The second is offload: absorb the traffic — especially the bursty, abusive, or attack traffic — before it reaches your origin. When a launch-day spike hits, the CDN serves the same bytes ten million times and your origin sees a trickle. When a DDoS hits, the CDN's edge is where it dies, not your rack.

As an architect, you will make CDN decisions constantly: what is cacheable, for how long, how you invalidate, and what happens on a miss. Get caching right and your origin fleet can be a tenth the size. Get it wrong and users see stale prices, or worse, each other's data.

What this module covers: what a CDN is for (latency + offload), anycast in plain language, and the edge-PoP request path. Then the cache headers that control it all — Cache-Control, ETag/If-None-Match, Expires, Last-Modified — plus conditional requests and pull vs. push distribution. The caching standard is RFC 9111 (HTTP Caching): real, current, and the authority behind every header here.

2. The mental model

Think of a CDN as a globally distributed cache you rent instead of build. You keep running your origin — the one true source of your content. The CDN places copies of your responses in hundreds of points of presence (PoPs) around the world, and user requests are steered to the nearest PoP. A request is either served from the copy (a hit) or, if no fresh copy exists, fetched from your origin, stored, and then served (a miss that populates the cache).

Two ideas govern everything:

Freshness is a contract, not a guess. The origin declares, in headers, how long a response may be reused: Cache-Control: max-age=3600 means "this is good for one hour." While the entry is fresh, the cache serves it without contacting the origin at all — not even to ask. When it goes stale, the cache must revalidate before serving: "has this changed?" The origin answers with the full body (200) or a tiny "nope" (304 Not Modified).

A cache key decides what "the same request" means. By default the key is the URL (plus the method — only GET/HEAD are cacheable). The Vary header adds dimensions: Vary: Accept-Encoding means "keep separate copies for gzip vs. br vs. identity." Get the key wrong and you serve the wrong copy — the desktop page to the phone, or one user's private data to another. Caching private data under a shared key is one of the classic production incidents, and it is always a key-design failure.

3. How it works

The request path: user → edge → origin

Anycast in plain language

Here is the trick that makes "the nearest PoP" work without any client configuration. A CDN announces the same IP address from every PoP simultaneously via BGP — the internet's inter-domain routing protocol. BGP routers, doing their normal shortest-path job, forward packets for that IP to the closest announcing location. Your request to 203.0.113.7 from Mumbai reaches the Mumbai PoP; the same destination IP from Berlin reaches the Berlin PoP. One address, many places, and the routing layer sorts it out.

This is different from DNS-based steering (the older approach, where DNS answers vary by resolver location). Anycast is faster to fail over — if a PoP dies, BGP withdraws the announcement and traffic reconverges in seconds — and it needs no per-client state. The trade-off: it only works well for stateless-ish protocols. TCP over anycast is fine as long as a given connection keeps landing at the same PoP, which holds in practice because BGP paths are stable on the timescale of a connection. QUIC/UDP services use anycast the same way.

Edge PoPs and what lives in them

A PoP is a small data center — sometimes a full building, sometimes a few racks inside an ISP — holding cache servers, TLS terminators, and WAF/DDoS scrubbing. The request path through one:

  1. TLS terminates at the edge. The CDN holds your certificate (or you upload it). This is why CDN setup asks for your cert: the edge must decrypt to read the HTTP layer and make caching decisions.
  2. The cache is consulted. Key = method + URL + Vary dimensions. Hit and fresh → respond immediately, origin never involved.
  3. On a miss, the edge fetches from origin (or from a parent cache in a tiered setup — shield PoPs that sit between edge and origin to collapse duplicate misses), stores the response per its headers, and serves it.
  4. The control plane handles the exceptions. Purge APIs evict entries on demand ("we shipped the wrong price — drop it everywhere now"). Logs and metrics flow back for your dashboards.

Note the trust implication: the CDN sees your plaintext. That is the job — it cannot cache what it cannot read. For content that must stay opaque to the CDN, you encrypt at the application layer or bypass caching for those paths. Architects should be able to say this sentence out loud in a security review.

4. The exact structure: cache headers that matter

All caching behavior is driven by response headers from the origin. These are the ones that matter, per RFC 9111:

Cache-Control — the master switch. A comma-separated list of directives. The essential ones:

Directive Meaning
max-age=N Fresh for N seconds after receipt. The core freshness lifetime.
s-maxage=N Like max-age but only for shared caches (CDNs). Lets you say "CDN: cache 1 hour, browsers: cache 1 minute."
no-store Do not store this response anywhere. For sensitive data — bank balances, personal info.
no-cache Misleading name: you may store it, but must revalidate with the origin before every reuse.
public Any cache may store it (default for some statuses, but be explicit).
private Only the end user's private cache (browser) may store it — never a shared CDN cache. Use for personalized content.
immutable This URL's content will never change. The client should not even revalidate for a year. Pair with fingerprinted asset URLs (app.a3f9c2.js) — the standard trick that makes aggressive caching safe: when the content changes, the URL changes.
must-revalidate / proxy-revalidate Once stale, do not serve the stale copy — revalidate first. (Without this, caches are allowed to serve stale in some edge cases.)
stale-while-revalidate=N Serve the stale copy for N seconds while fetching a fresh one in the background. The latency trick behind "always fast, eventually fresh."
stale-if-error=N On origin failure, serve stale for N seconds rather than erroring. Your outage story.

ETag / If-None-Match — the strong validator. The origin attaches an opaque version tag: ETag: "logo-v3". On revalidation, the client sends If-None-Match: "logo-v3". If the tag still matches, the server answers 304 Not Modified with no body. Strong because it is byte-exact: same tag means same bytes.

Last-Modified / If-Modified-Since — the weak validator. A timestamp instead of a tag. Weaker because content can change twice within one second, or change and change back, without the timestamp moving. Usable, but ETag is preferred whenever you can generate one cheaply (a content hash is ideal).

Expires — the legacy header. An absolute date ("good until Thursday"). It still works, but Cache-Control: max-age overrides it, and clock skew makes absolute dates fragile. New code should emit Cache-Control; Expires survives for HTTP/1.0-era clients.

Vary — cache-key dimensions. Vary: Accept-Encoding keeps separate entries per encoding. Vary: Accept-Language per language. Every Vary dimension multiplies your cache entries, so keep the list short. And never vary on something with unbounded values (like User-Agent raw strings) unless you enjoy a cache full of single-use entries.

The revalidation dance

When a cached entry goes stale, the cache does not have to refetch the body. It sends a conditional request — the same GET, plus validators — and the origin decides:

If the ETag had changed, the origin would answer 200 with the new body and tag — a normal fetch, and the cache replaces its entry. The elegance: the protocol never needs a separate "is it fresh?" API. The validator rides on the ordinary request.

Pull vs. push

Pull (origin pull) is the default you have seen: the CDN fetches from the origin on first miss. Nothing is pre-positioned. It is simple and self-maintaining — content that nobody requests never consumes edge storage. The cost: the first user in each region pays the miss latency, and a cold CDN after a purge hammers the origin. That stampede has a name, the thundering herd, and tiered "shield" caches exist largely to absorb it.

Push (content push / pre-positioning) uploads content to the edge before anyone asks — via API, or scheduled replication. Streaming platforms push popular titles overnight; software vendors push release binaries ahead of launch day. You trade operational complexity (and paying for storage of possibly-unrequested bytes) for guaranteed hot caches at peak. Most teams start with pull and add push only for the handful of assets where a cold miss is commercially painful.

Shielding: caches in front of caches

One refinement deserves its own paragraph because it answers the thundering-herd problem from the pull section. In a tiered setup, edge PoPs do not fetch from the origin directly. They fetch from a shield PoP — a larger regional cache sitting between the edges and the origin. A cold object is fetched from the origin once by the shield, then served to every edge that asks. The origin sees one request instead of three hundred. Shielding also gives you a single choke point for origin connection pooling and for collapsing concurrent misses (request coalescing: ten edges ask for the same cold object, one upstream fetch serves all ten). The cost is one more hop of latency on a miss and another cache layer whose invalidation you must reason about. For high-traffic sites it is close to mandatory; for small ones it is complexity without payoff.

5. A realistic example

Two localhost examples, both offline-safe. The first replays the revalidation diagram against a real server: a cold 200 with an ETag, then a conditional request that earns a 304 with zero body bytes.

Run it: ~/.local/bin/python3.13 code/m13/m13_conditional_cache.py

Real output:

first fetch : 200 OK, bytes=72, etag="logo-v3", cache-control='public, max-age=3600'
revalidate  : 304 Not Modified, bytes=0, etag="logo-v3"

Seventy-two bytes the first time, zero the second. That is the entire economic argument for validators: the revalidation costs a round trip of headers and nothing else.

The second example is pure logic — how a cache decides fresh vs. stale from Cache-Control directives, per RFC 9111's rules:

Run it: ~/.local/bin/python3.13 code/m13/m13_cache_control.py

Real output:

Cache-Control decisions (cache entry ages in seconds):
  public, max-age=3600                       age=   120s -> fresh
  public, max-age=3600                       age=  3700s -> stale (age > max-age)
  no-store                                   age=     5s -> must revalidate (no-store / no-cache)
  public, max-age=31536000, immutable         age=400000s -> fresh (immutable — never revalidate)
  private, max-age=60                        age=    10s -> fresh

Read the immutable line carefully: 400,000 seconds old — over four days — and still fresh. That is the fingerprinted-asset pattern: the URL contains a content hash, so the bytes at that URL cannot change. The cache never needs to ask. When you ship new code, the filename changes and the HTML referencing it is the only short-lived entry. This single pattern eliminates an entire class of "users see the old UI" incidents.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"The CDN makes my site faster by magic." It makes cacheable bytes faster. Uncacheable, personalized API responses still travel to the origin; the CDN only shortens the network path (which helps, but modestly). The speedup comes from your cache policy, not from the vendor's logo. Teams that point a CDN at an API with Cache-Control: no-store on everything are paying for a very expensive TLS terminator.

"Purge is instant." Purge is fast — typically seconds globally — but it is not atomic and not free. During propagation, different PoPs serve different versions. Purging by wildcard or tag is operationally nicer than purging by URL, and versioned URLs (fingerprints) are nicer still because they need no purge at all.

"no-cache means don't cache." It means "cache, but revalidate every time." The directive you want for "never store this" is no-store. This naming has caused real incidents — sensitive responses cached because someone wrote the wrong directive. Say the two out loud until the distinction is automatic.

"A 304 means the content didn't change, so I can skip parsing." Almost. It means the validator matched. With weak validators (Last-Modified), byte-identical content is not guaranteed — only "not modified since." If your logic needs byte equality (a download resume, a checksum), require a strong ETag.

"Caching Set-Cookie responses is fine." A shared cache storing a response with Set-Cookie can hand one user's session cookie to another user. RFC 9111 forbids shared caching of responses to requests with Authorization unless explicitly allowed, and any competent CDN refuses to cache Set-Cookie by default — but misconfigured Vary/key rules have caused exactly this leak. Personalized content gets private or no-store, no exceptions.

"Higher hit ratio is always better." Hit ratio measures how often the cache avoided the origin. A 99% hit ratio on a site serving yesterday's prices is a bug, not a win. The metric that matters is correct freshness at acceptable origin load — tune max-age per content type (immutable assets: a year; product pages: minutes; personalized: don't cache) instead of chasing the ratio.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Users report seeing stale prices after a deploy. Your CDN shows a 98% hit ratio. Walk me through the diagnosis.

A: The hit ratio is the clue, not the comfort: the edge is faithfully serving what it was told to serve. I check the origin's Cache-Control on the price API — likely a long max-age or a missing no-store/private on dynamic content. Then the purge story: did the deploy purge the affected URLs, and did it use exact URLs, wildcards, or surrogate keys? The structural fix is to separate cache lifetimes by content type. Fingerprinted static assets get immutable plus year-long max-age. Price and product data get short max-age (minutes) plus stale-while-revalidate, so revalidation never blocks a user. Never "fix" it by purging everything on every deploy — that converts each deploy into a thundering herd on the origin.

Q2: Explain the difference between ETag/If-None-Match and Last-Modified/If-Modified-Since. When would you use each?

A: Both are validators for conditional requests, but ETag is a strong validator (an opaque version tag — ideally a content hash) while Last-Modified is weak (a one-second-resolution timestamp). Strong means byte-identical; weak means "probably unchanged." Use ETag whenever you can compute one cheaply — it enables exact revalidation and range-request resume. Last-Modified is the fallback for origins that cannot cheaply hash content (large files served by a simple file server, where mtime is free). A subtle point: a strong validator is required for If-Match optimistic locking (Module 12's 412); timestamps are not safe there because of the one-second granularity.

Q3: What is anycast, and why do CDNs use it instead of just DNS-based routing?

A: Anycast announces the same IP address from many locations via BGP, and the internet's routers deliver each packet to the topologically nearest announcer. DNS-based steering instead returns different IPs per client region. Anycast wins on failover speed (a dead PoP's announcement is withdrawn and BGP reconverges — no DNS TTL to wait out) and simplicity (no client or resolver cooperation needed). The constraint is that it suits connection-oriented traffic only while a connection's packets keep landing at the same PoP, which BGP stability gives us in practice. Most large CDNs use both: anycast for the edge IPs, DNS steering for higher-level traffic management.

Q4: Design the caching strategy for an e-commerce product page.

A: Decompose the page by change rate. Static assets (images, JS, CSS) get content-hashed filenames + Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable — cached at edge and browser for a year, zero invalidation needed. The product data API gets public, max-age=60, stale-while-revalidate=300 — prices at most a minute stale, and revalidation never blocks rendering. Personalized fragments (cart count, recommendations) are private, no-store or fetched client-side after page load so the shell stays cacheable. Invalidation: deploys change asset hashes (no purge needed); price changes rely on the 60-second max-age; emergency corrections use surrogate-key purge ("purge everything tagged product-123"). The CDN never sees Set-Cookie on cacheable responses.

8. Try it

Each command is labeled. Run on your Mac — your output will differ (headers, timing, your CDN vendor).

  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Read a real site's cache policy: curl -sI https://example.com | grep -iE 'cache-control|etag|expires|last-modified|age' Then try a large asset (a JS bundle or image) and compare its max-age — static assets should be far longer-lived than HTML.
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Do a manual conditional request: ETAG=$(curl -sI https://example.com/logo.png 2>/dev/null | grep -i etag | tr -d '\r' | awk '{print $2}'); curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} %{size_download}\n' -H "If-None-Match: $ETAG" https://example.com/logo.png A 304 with 0 bytes downloaded is the validator working. (Pick a URL from the first command that actually returned an ETag.)
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Check whether a response came from cache: curl -sI https://example.com | grep -iE '^(x-cache|cf-cache-status|age|x-served-by)' Vendors expose hit/miss differently (X-Cache: HIT, cf-cache-status, Age header) — this is vendor-specific, not standard.
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Time a cold vs. warm fetch through a CDN: curl -o /dev/null -s -w 'cold: %{time_total}s\n' "https://example.com/asset.png?bust=$RANDOM"; curl -o /dev/null -s -w 'warm: %{time_total}s\n' "https://example.com/asset.png?bust=$RANDOM" (The random query busts the cache for the "cold" run. Real CDNs may ignore query strings per configuration — another vendor-specific behavior worth verifying.)
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Run the module's offline examples: ~/.local/bin/python3.13 ~/workspace/build/networking-guide/code/m13/m13_conditional_cache.py ~/.local/bin/python3.13 ~/workspace/build/networking-guide/code/m13/m13_cache_control.py

Standards cited: RFC 9111 (HTTP Caching). Anycast behavior described here follows standard BGP anycast practice (RFC 4786 covers the underlying multicast/anycast addressing concepts; CDN anycast itself is operational practice, not a single RFC — worth knowing the difference between "the standard says" and "the industry does").

Module 14 — Wireless Networking

The last hop is a shared, invisible, half-duplex medium: how 802.11 organizes the air, why channel choice is a trade-off, what Wi-Fi security actually proves, and how your laptop moves between access points without dropping the call.

1. Why it matters

For most users, "the network" is Wi-Fi. And Wi-Fi is the most hostile link in your architecture: a shared broadcast medium, half-duplex (a radio cannot listen while it talks), subject to interference from microwaves and neighbors. There is no physical perimeter — anyone in range receives your frames. Every design decision in 802.11 is a response to that hostility.

As a backend architect you will not configure access points daily, but you will debug through them. "The API is slow" turns out to be 2.4 GHz congestion in a dense office. "Sessions drop" turns out to be a roaming failure between APs. "Is this traffic safe?" turns on what WPA3 actually guarantees. This module gives you the mental models to reason about the wireless hop instead of treating it as magic — and to ask the right questions when it misbehaves.

What this module covers: 802.11 basics (BSS, ESS, association and authentication in plain language), bands and channels (2.4/5/6 GHz and the width-vs-interference trade-off), WPA2 vs. WPA3 (what the handshake proves, what WPA3 fixed — conceptual, no crypto), and roaming. The standard is IEEE 802.11 (with its amendments — 802.11n/ac/ax/be, 802.11i/r/k/v/w). No live Wi-Fi commands can run in this sandbox; those are marked try-on-Mac.

2. The mental model

Ethernet solved sharing with a wire: one cable, switches, full-duplex, collisions mostly engineered away. Wi-Fi has no wire. Every station in range shares one invisible medium, and the medium is half-duplex — like a walkie-talkie channel, not a phone call. Only one station can transmit at a time; everyone else must wait their turn. The entire 802.11 MAC layer is machinery for taking turns politely on a channel where you cannot hear yourself collide.

That gives you three governing ideas:

Listen before you talk, and back off when it goes wrong. A station with a frame to send first listens. If the channel is busy, it waits. If it is idle, it still waits a short random backoff — because two stations that both found the channel idle would otherwise transmit simultaneously. If frames collide anyway (no acknowledgment arrives), each station doubles its backoff window and tries again. This is CSMA/CA with binary exponential backoff: self-clocking, decentralized, and fair-ish. The worked example in §5 shows it in action.

Cells, not cables. Coverage is organized into cells. One access point plus its associated stations is a Basic Service Set (BSS) — the fundamental unit, identified by the AP's BSSID (basically its radio MAC address). Multiple BSSs sharing one network name (SSID) form an Extended Service Set (ESS) — your "Office-WiFi" that follows you across floors. Your station joins a BSS; the ESS is what makes it feel like one network.

The AP is a bridge. An access point is not a router and not magic. It bridges wireless frames onto the wired distribution system (usually Ethernet) and back. When your laptop "joins Wi-Fi," it is joining a Layer-2 network through a wireless bridge. Everything above — DHCP, IP, your HTTP request — works exactly as if the laptop were plugged in. This is why Wi-Fi problems so often present as Layer-2 problems: the IP stack never sees the air.

3. How it works

BSS, ESS, and joining a network

Association and authentication, in plain language

Joining a network has three steps, and the names are more grand than the reality:

  1. Scan. The station finds candidate BSSs — passively (listening for AP beacons, broadcast ~10×/second) or actively (sending probe requests, APs answer with probe responses). Your phone's Wi-Fi list is built from these.
  2. 802.11 authentication. Despite the name, with modern security this is a formality — "open system" authentication is two frames that say "may I?" / "sure." It is a leftover from the WEP era. The real authentication happens later, in the WPA handshake or via 802.1X.
  3. Association. The station sends an association request ("I want to join this BSS with these capabilities"); the AP replies with an association response containing an Association ID. Only now is the station a member of the BSS. Then the security handshake runs (next section), and only then does data flow.

For enterprise networks (WPA2/WPA3-Enterprise), an 802.1X/EAP exchange with a RADIUS server happens between association and the 4-way handshake — that is where your corporate username/password or certificates are verified. The AP is just the middleman passing EAP frames through.

Bands and channels: width vs. interference

Wi-Fi lives in unlicensed spectrum, in three bands:

Band Frequencies Notes
2.4 GHz 2.400–2.4835 GHz Longest range, best wall penetration, most crowded. 802.11b/g/n/ax. Only ~83 MHz of spectrum — the scarcest band.
5 GHz 5.170–5.835 GHz (region-dependent) Much more spectrum, less crowded, shorter range. 802.11a/n/ac/ax. Some channels require DFS (radar detection) — the AP must vacate if it hears radar.
6 GHz 5.925–7.125 GHz New with Wi-Fi 6E/7. ~1200 MHz of fresh spectrum, no legacy devices allowed — clean air, but the shortest range and worst wall penetration.

A channel is a slice of a band. In 2.4 GHz, channels are numbered 1–11 (US; 1–13 in EU, 14 exists in Japan for legacy 802.11b) with centers 5 MHz apart — but a standard 20 MHz-wide signal needs four channel-numbers of clearance. The geometry forces overlap:

The trade-off is now visible. Wider channels move more bits per second — roughly linearly with width, all else equal. But every hertz is shared with your neighbors. In 2.4 GHz there are only three non-overlapping 20 MHz channels, so a 40 MHz deployment leaves one usable channel for everyone nearby. In an apartment building or office, that is a tragedy of the commons. In 5 GHz there is room: 25 twenty-megahertz channels, so 40 or 80 MHz widths are practical. In 6 GHz, with ~1200 MHz of spectrum, even 160 and 320 MHz channels fit.

Two more physical realities worth knowing. Higher frequencies attenuate faster and penetrate walls worse — 6 GHz is a "same room" band. And wider channels also widen your interference aperture: you now hear (and defer to) every transmitter across the whole width. Throughput per hertz is roughly constant; width buys speed at the cost of airtime contention and neighbor relations. The interview-ready summary: channel width is a throughput-vs-coexistence knob, and the correct setting depends on band, density, and what the neighbors are doing.

4. The exact structure: what the security handshake proves

WPA2: the 4-way handshake

WPA2-Personal starts from a pre-shared key (PSK) — derived from your Wi-Fi password via PBKDF2 with the SSID as salt. Both sides know it; neither ever sends it. The 4-way handshake (defined in 802.11i) turns that shared secret into fresh per-session encryption keys and proves both sides hold it:

What the handshake proves, message by message:

  • Message 1 — the AP sends ANonce, a fresh random value. This binds the coming keys to this session; replaying an old handshake will not reproduce them.
  • Message 2 — the station replies with its own SNonce and a MIC (message integrity code) computed with keys derived from the PMK. The MIC is the proof: only someone holding the PMK could produce it, and the PMK itself never travels.
  • Message 3 — the AP, having verified the MIC, installs the PTK and delivers the GTK (group temporal key, shared by all stations for broadcast/multicast), encrypted. The PTK is the pairwise transient key: the actual session encryption key, derived from PMK + both nonces + both MAC addresses.
  • Message 4 — the station acknowledges. From here, unicast data is encrypted with the PTK (AES-CCMP in WPA2), broadcast with the GTK.

The conceptual payload: mutual proof of PMK possession without disclosure, plus fresh session keys bound to fresh nonces. An attacker who records the handshake learns nothing they can replay, and nothing that decrypts the session — provided the password is strong.

WPA3: what it fixed

WPA2-Personal's weakness was never the 4-way handshake. It was the PSK. The PMK is derived deterministically from the password, so anyone who captures the 4-way handshake can take it home and guess passwords offline, at billions of guesses per second, until the MICs verify. A weak password falls in minutes. Worse, once the password is known, past captured traffic decrypts too — no forward secrecy.

WPA3-Personal replaces the PSK derivation with SAE (Simultaneous Authentication of Equals), a password-authenticated key exchange based on the Dragonfly handshake. Conceptually:

  1. No more offline dictionary attacks. SAE is interactive — each password guess requires a live exchange with the AP, which rate-limits and can lock out attackers. Capturing frames no longer gives you a verifiable guess oracle.
  2. Forward secrecy, per session. Every SAE run uses fresh ephemeral keys, so the PMK is different every session. Learning the password later does not decrypt traffic captured earlier.
  3. No more weak-handshake downgrade games. WPA3 mandates Protected Management Frames (PMF, 802.11w): deauthentication and disassociation frames are now authenticated. The classic "deauth attack" — forging a deauth frame to kick a station off and capture its re-handshake — stops working.

WPA3 also brought a stronger enterprise mode (192-bit security suite) and Easy Connect (DPP) for headless IoT devices. The honest caveat: WPA3-Personal runs in transition mode on most networks (accepting WPA2 clients too), and transition mode re-exposes some downgrade surface. "WPA3" on the box is not the same as "WPA3-only" on the air.

Roaming, in one paragraph

When STA-2 walks down the hall, its station must leave BSS-2 and join BSS-1 without dropping the call. The naive path — full re-association plus full security handshake — takes hundreds of milliseconds, enough to glitch voice or video. The 802.11 amendments that fix this form a small alphabet soup with one job: make the handoff fast. 802.11r (Fast BSS Transition) pre-derives the keys so re-association completes in a few frames. 802.11k (radio resource measurement) lets the AP hand the station a neighbor report — "here are the APs around you and their channels" — so it scans smart instead of probing every channel. 802.11v (BSS transition management) lets the infrastructure suggest roams ("this AP is loaded, try that one"). Together, with a well-planned ESS (overlapping cells, same SSID, shared key hierarchy via a controller or standard key distribution), roams complete in tens of milliseconds. When roaming breaks in practice, it is usually one of three things. Cells that do not overlap enough, so the station clings to a dying signal — the "sticky client" problem. Mismatched 802.11r configuration across AP vendors. Or a client that simply ignores the network's suggestions. Clients, not APs, decide when to roam — the network can only persuade.

5. A realistic example

No radio is available in this sandbox, so the example models the two pieces of 802.11 you can compute exactly: channel geometry and the CSMA/CA backoff that arbitrates the shared air.

Run it: ~/.local/bin/python3.13 code/m14/m14_channels_backoff.py

Real output:

== 2.4 GHz channel geometry (US domain, channels 1-11) ==
channel centers: 1=2412 MHz ... 11=2462 MHz (5 MHz spacing)
non-overlapping at 20 MHz: [1, 5, 9]
  ^-- greedy maximal set; industry plans use 1/6/11 (extra 5 MHz guard
  against adjacent-channel interference from spectral leakage)
non-overlapping at 40 MHz: [1, 9]  <- why 40 MHz is a bad idea here

== 5 GHz (25 channels listed here) ==
non-overlapping at 20 MHz: 25 channels
non-overlapping at 80 MHz: [36, 52, 100, 116, 132, 165]
non-overlapping at 160 MHz: [36, 100, 132, 165]

== CSMA/CA: two stations collide, then back off ==
attempt 1: CW=0..  15  A waits  14 slots, B waits  12 slots  -> COLLISION again
attempt 2: CW=0..  31  A waits  28 slots, B waits  12 slots  -> one wins, channel clear
(contention window doubled 2x after the collision, halving the odds of colliding again)
winner resets to CWmin; loser keeps the doubled window. Fair-ish, self-clocking, no coordination needed.

Read the backoff trace as the protocol's core insight. Two stations picked the same slot and collided — neither could detect it while transmitting (half-duplex), so the missing acknowledgment is the signal. Both double their contention window (15 → 31 slots), which roughly halves the collision probability, and the next attempt separates them. No coordinator, no schedule, no central clock. Just "wait a random time, and wait longer when it fails." It is the same exponential-backoff shape you already know from Ethernet and from retry logic in distributed systems — because it is the same problem: independent actors sharing a scarce resource without coordination.

And note the honest detail in the channel output: the greedy algorithm finds [1, 5, 9], which is mathematically non-overlapping — but the industry plans 1/6/11. The extra 5 MHz is guard margin against spectral leakage (real radios are not brick-wall filters; energy spills into adjacent channels). Standards give you the math; practice adds the margin. That gap between the two is worth remembering as a general principle.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"Hiding the SSID is security." The SSID is broadcast in beacons and, worse, your devices probe for hidden networks by name wherever they go — leaking the names of your home and office networks to every passive sniffer in the café. Hidden SSIDs are obscurity, not security, and they make roaming and discovery worse.

"MAC filtering keeps intruders out." MAC addresses are sent in the clear in every frame header and are trivially spoofed. Filtering is an allowlist with no authentication — administrative overhead for zero real security.

"WPA2 with a strong password is basically as good as WPA3." Closer than the marketing suggests, but not equal. WPA3's SAE gives forward secrecy and kills offline dictionary attacks — properties a strong WPA2 password does not provide. If the password ever leaks, WPA2's past traffic decrypts; WPA3's does not. That said, a strong unique WPA2 password beats a weak WPA3 one every time. The password is still the weakest link.

"5 GHz is always better than 2.4 GHz." 5 GHz has more spectrum and less interference, but shorter range and worse wall penetration. The correct design uses both: 5 GHz (and 6 GHz) for capacity where coverage reaches, 2.4 GHz as the coverage blanket. Band-steering features exist to push capable clients up — and misconfigured band steering is a classic source of "Wi-Fi worked yesterday" tickets.

"More APs = better Wi-Fi." APs on overlapping channels interfere with each other — two APs on channel 6 in the same hallway share one collision domain. Density helps only with proper channel planning (1/6/11 reuse pattern), power tuning (turn down transmit power so cells stay small), and enough wired backhaul. An over-dense, over-powered deployment performs worse than a sparse, planned one.

"The deauth attack means Wi-Fi is broken." It meant unprotected management frames were broken — fixed by PMF (802.11w), mandatory in WPA3. If you are assessing a WPA2 network's exposure, "is PMF enabled?" is the precise question, not vibes about hackers.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Why can't Wi-Fi use collision detection like Ethernet (CSMA/CD)?

A: A radio cannot transmit and receive on the same frequency at the same time — its own transmission drowns out everything else, orders of magnitude louder than any distant signal. So a station cannot hear a collision while it is colliding. 802.11 therefore uses collision avoidance: listen first, wait random backoffs, and treat a missing acknowledgment as the collision signal. The deeper point: every design difference between 802.11 and 802.3 traces back to half-duplex wireless physics. If you remember "walkie-talkie, not telephone," you can re-derive most of the MAC layer.

Q2: Walk me through what happens when my laptop joins a WPA3-Personal network, and say what each step proves.

A: Scan (beacons/probes) finds the BSS. 802.11 authentication and association join the station to the BSS — formalities at this point. Then SAE (Dragonfly): a password-authenticated key exchange where both sides prove knowledge of the password without sending it or anything verifiable offline, deriving a fresh per-session PMK. Then the 4-way handshake: nonces are exchanged, MICs prove both sides hold the PMK, and fresh PTK/GTK session keys are derived and installed. What is proven: mutual possession of the password-derived secret, freshness of the session keys (nonces), and — thanks to SAE's ephemeral keys — that past sessions stay secret even if the password later leaks. Data then flows under AES encryption with per-packet keys.

Q3: An office complains that Wi-Fi is unusable at lunch in the cafeteria but fine at desks. Diagnose it.

A: This smells like density and interference, not coverage. At lunch, dozens of clients associate to the nearest APs — many on 2.4 GHz, which penetrates from the whole floor — and they all share a handful of collision domains. Diagnosis: check channel utilization (anything sustained above ~60-70% is saturated), co-channel interference from neighboring APs on the same channel, and the client mix (one 802.11b-era client drags the whole cell down via protection mechanisms). Fixes: more APs on non-overlapping channels with reduced transmit power (smaller cells, more airtime to go around), steer clients to 5 GHz, ensure 2.4 GHz stays 20 MHz wide, and check that legacy data rates are disabled so slow clients do not hog airtime. The general principle: Wi-Fi capacity is airtime, and airtime is shared — the fix is almost always "more, smaller cells on clean channels," not "louder APs."

Q4: What does WPA3 fix over WPA2, and what does it not fix?

A: It fixes three things: offline dictionary attacks (SAE requires live interaction per guess — captured handshakes are no longer crackable at home), forward secrecy (fresh ephemeral keys per session — a later password compromise does not decrypt past traffic), and management-frame spoofing (mandatory PMF kills deauth attacks). It does not fix: weak passwords (SAE still falls to an online guessing campaign against a weak password, just far slower), evil-twin APs (nothing in WPA-Personal authenticates the network to the user — that needs Enterprise with server certificates), or anything above Layer 2 (once associated, your traffic still needs TLS; the Wi-Fi encryption only covers the air hop).

Q5: Your video call stutters every time you walk between conference rooms, but never at your desk. What is likely happening?

A: A roaming problem. The client is either clinging to the distant AP too long (sticky client — clients decide when to roam, and their thresholds are often conservative), or the roam itself is slow (full re-authentication without 802.11r fast transition, so the handoff takes hundreds of milliseconds and the jitter buffer empties). Check: do the APs support 802.11r/k/v and is the client using them? Do the cells overlap adequately at the boundary (a coverage gap forces a hard break)? Is transmit power too high, making the distant AP look artificially attractive? The fix set: enable 802.11r FT, publish 802.11k neighbor reports, tune power down for cleaner cell edges, and verify with a client that actually roams aggressively — some clients are just stubborn, which is a client problem, not a network problem.

8. Try it

No radio work is possible in this sandbox — every command below is labeled. Run on your Mac — your output will differ (your hardware, your networks, your channels).

  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. See your current Wi-Fi state (macOS): /System/Library/PrivateFrameworks/Apple80211.framework/Versions/Current/Resources/airport -I Look at agrCtlRSSI (signal), agrCtlNoise (noise floor — the difference is your SNR), channel, and auth (the AKM in use). Low SNR plus a crowded channel explains most "slow Wi-Fi" complaints.
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Scan the neighborhood and count channel overlap: /System/Library/PrivateFrameworks/Apple80211.framework/Versions/Current/Resources/airport -s Count how many SSIDs share your channel in 2.4 GHz. More than a couple of strong ones on the same channel means you are sharing airtime with the neighbors.
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Check what your AP and client negotiated: Hold Option and click the Wi-Fi menu-bar icon — macOS shows PHY mode, channel, width, MCS index, and security type directly. Compare the channel width against the trade-off discussion in §3.
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Run the module's offline example (works anywhere): ~/.local/bin/python3.13 ~/workspace/build/networking-guide/code/m14/m14_channels_backoff.py
  • Run on your Mac — your output will differ. Watch roaming in real time (laptop required, walk around): Run the airport -I command in a loop while walking between rooms and watch the BSSID field — each change is a roam. Time how long traffic stalls during it with a concurrent ping.

Standards cited: IEEE 802.11-2020 (the base standard; WPA2 = 802.11i amendment, WPA3 = Wi-Fi Alliance certification building on SAE/802.11s-derived mechanisms and 802.11w PMF, fast roaming = 802.11r, neighbor reports = 802.11k, BSS transition = 802.11v). Note the distinction the module respects throughout: IEEE defines the mechanisms; the Wi-Fi Alliance's WPA certifications define which mechanisms products must implement — "WPA3" is a certification, not an IEEE amendment.

Module 15 — NAT & Firewalls

How one public address serves a whole office, and how a firewall decides what gets through.

1. Why it matters

Every device on your home Wi-Fi has an IP address. Your laptop, your phone, the TV. They all reach the internet through a single public address — the one your ISP gave your router. That sleight of hand is Network Address Translation, and it is the reason IPv4 survived its own funeral.

IPv4 has about 4.3 billion addresses. That sounded generous in 1981. It was not. Phones, laptops, servers, smart bulbs, and cars ate through the pool, and the last blocks were handed out in the early 2010s. NAT bought the world time. It let millions of devices hide behind one address. IPv6 is the real fix, and it is coming, but the internet you will troubleshoot in interviews and on the job still runs on NAT.

Firewalls are the other half of this module. NAT decides which address a packet carries. A firewall decides whether the packet is allowed at all. People constantly mix the two up. By the end of this module you will not.

If you interview for backend or distributed-systems roles, NAT shows up in the questions nobody warns you about: why a service behind a home router cannot accept inbound connections, why two containers on the same host can collide on ports, why a SIP phone call has one-way audio. Firewalls show up too, usually as a wrong assumption: "the security group blocks it" is the answer to a surprising number of "it works locally but not in staging" mysteries.

2. The mental model

Think of a large office building with one street address and a mailroom. Hundreds of employees work inside. None of them has their own street address. When someone inside mails a letter, the mailroom stamps the building's address on it and notes, in a logbook, which employee sent it. When a reply arrives at the building's address, the mailroom checks the logbook and delivers it to the right desk. A letter that arrives with no logbook entry — nobody inside asked for it — gets thrown away.

That is NAT. The building is your private network. The street address is the public IP. The mailroom is the NAT device, usually your router. The logbook is the translation table. And the rule "no entry, no delivery" is why inbound connections do not work through NAT unless you set up an exception.

Now extend the metaphor. The mailroom does not just forward mail. It also enforces policy: no packages from that sender, no letters to that country, nothing after 6 PM. That second job is the firewall. Same building, different function. NAT rewrites addresses. The firewall makes allow/deny decisions.

Keep one more distinction ready. Your laptop's private address (like 192.168.1.10) is only meaningful inside the building. The moment it steps outside, it must wear the building's address. The ranges set aside for this purpose — addresses that are never routed on the public internet — are defined in RFC 1918:

Range CIDR Rough size
10.0.0.0 – 10.255.255.255 10.0.0.0/8 ~16.7 million
172.16.0.0 – 172.31.255.255 172.16.0.0/12 ~1 million
192.168.0.0 – 192.168.255.255 192.168.0.0/16 ~65 thousand

Routers on the public internet drop packets addressed to these ranges. They are not routable, by convention and by configuration. Your ISP will not forward them, and neither will anyone else's. That is what makes them safe to reuse in every home and office on earth.

One more range worth knowing: 100.64.0.0/10. That is shared address space from RFC 6598, used by ISPs for carrier-grade NAT — NAT inside NAT, which we will get to. If you ever see a 100.64.x.x address on a WAN interface, your ISP is NATing you before you even reach the public internet.

3. How it works

The three flavors of NAT

Terminology first, because vendors muddy it. RFC 3022, the document that standardized NAT vocabulary, defines these:

Static NAT maps one private address to one public address, permanently, one-to-one. 192.168.1.10 always appears on the internet as 203.0.113.10. No ports are rewritten. This is used when an internal server needs a stable public identity — a mail server, for example. Vendors often call this 1:1 NAT. Same thing.

Dynamic NAT maps private addresses to public addresses from a pool, one-to-one, but only for as long as the session lasts. When the session ends, the public address goes back in the pool. It conserves addresses, but it still burns one public address per active host. It was a stepping stone. You will rarely meet it in the wild today.

PAT — Port Address Translation — maps many private addresses to one public address by rewriting the source port as well as the source address. This is the one that matters. RFC 3022 calls it NAPT (Network Address and Port Translation). Cisco calls it NAT overload. Linux calls it masquerading. Your home router just calls it NAT. All the same mechanism: the port number becomes the disambiguator that lets one address serve thousands of hosts.

The port matters because the full identity of a connection is not the IP address. It is the 5-tuple: source IP, source port, destination IP, destination port, protocol. Change any one element and you have a different conversation. PAT exploits that. Two laptops can both talk to the same web server on port 443 from the same private source port, and the router gives each conversation a different public source port. The server sees two distinct clients. The router's table remembers which is which.

The translation table, up close

Here is the whole trick, drawn out. Two laptops, one public address, one web server:

Notice the detail that makes PAT work. Laptop A and Laptop B both chose source port 51234. That is not a problem. The router assigned public port 50000 to A's conversation and 50001 to B's. From the server's side, the two connections are (203.0.113.7, 50000) and (203.0.113.7, 50001) — different 5-tuples, no ambiguity.

The entry is created when the first outbound packet leaves. Most NATs are triggered by outbound traffic. The table entry also records which protocol it is (TCP and UDP have separate port spaces — TCP port 50000 and UDP port 50000 can map to different hosts). Entries expire. A UDP mapping might time out after a couple of minutes of silence; a TCP mapping after hours. The exact timers are implementation choices; RFC 4787 and RFC 5382 set behavioral expectations for UDP and TCP NATs respectively, including how quickly mappings should time out. When an entry expires, its public port goes back in the pool.

And the inbound rule: a packet arriving from the internet is checked against the table. Match, and it gets un-translated and delivered. No match, and it is dropped. That "no match, no delivery" behavior is what makes people call NAT a firewall. It is not one. It is a side effect of having no mapping. The moment you add a port-forwarding rule — a permanent table entry — that side effect disappears for that port.

Hairpin NAT: talking to yourself through the front door

Here is a scenario that confuses everyone once. You port-forward 203.0.113.7:443 to your home server at 192.168.1.50. From a café, it works. From your laptop on the same home Wi-Fi, you open https://203.0.113.7 and it fails.

What happened: your laptop sends a packet to the public address. The router DNATs the destination to 192.168.1.50 — but the source is still your laptop's private address, 192.168.1.20. The server replies directly to 192.168.1.20, bypassing the router. Your laptop receives a reply from 192.168.1.50 when it was talking to 203.0.113.7. The 5-tuples do not match. The TCP stack drops the reply as nonsense.

The fix is hairpin NAT (also called NAT loopback): the router must SNAT the source as well, rewriting it to its own LAN address so the reply is forced back through the router, which then un-translates both sides. Many consumer routers do this automatically. Some do not, and the symptom — "works from outside, broken from inside" — sends people chasing DNS and TLS ghosts for hours. When you see that exact symptom shape, check the NAT, not the app.

The helpers that lie: ALGs

Some protocols carry IP addresses inside their payloads, where the NAT's header rewrite cannot reach. Classic FTP in active mode is the textbook case: the client tells the server "connect back to me at 192.168.1.10, port 5001" — inside the FTP control message. The server tries, and fails, because that address means nothing on the internet.

The historical fix is the ALG — Application Layer Gateway. It is a protocol-aware helper inside the NAT that opens the payload, rewrites the embedded address, and creates the expected inbound mapping. FTP and SIP ALGs ship in most routers. They are also a running source of bugs: they misparse, they break encrypted control channels (they cannot rewrite what TLS hides, so FTPS and SIP/TLS silently defeat them), and security folks routinely recommend disabling the SIP ALG because it mangles headers. The modern answer is to avoid protocols that embed addresses — or to tunnel them — rather than to trust the ALG.

Carrier-grade NAT: NAT all the way down

Your ISP may NAT you before your router NATs you.
