With carrier-grade NAT (CGN, also called large-scale NAT), the ISP puts thousands of subscribers behind a handful of public addresses, using the shared space 100.64.0.0/10 (RFC 6598) between you and their NAT. Your router's "public" address is a 100.64.x.x address that is itself private.

This breaks things in ways home NAT does not. Port forwarding on your router no longer reaches the internet — the ISP's NAT has no mapping for you, and you cannot configure it. Inbound connections, self-hosted services, some VPN setups, and older games all fail. Latency rises slightly. Troubleshooting gets a layer harder: when a customer says "my port forward doesn't work," the first question is now "is your WAN address in 100.64.0.0/10?" If yes, no amount of router configuration will fix it. The remedies are asking the ISP for a real public address (sometimes a paid add-on), using IPv6 if offered, or relaying through an external server.

CGN is also why peer-to-peer applications grew an entire sub-discipline of NAT traversal: STUN (a client asking "what address does the internet see me as?"), TURN (a relay that forwards when direct connection is impossible), and ICE (the framework that tries direct, then STUN-assisted, then TURN-relayed paths in order). You do not need the protocol details for most interviews. You need the one-line story: two hosts behind NAT cannot open connections to each other without help, so real-time apps probe, punch holes, and fall back to relays.

Port forwarding: the deliberate exception

Port forwarding (vendors also say virtual server, port mapping; the underlying mechanism is a static PAT entry, or a DNAT rule in Linux terms) is a permanent, manually configured translation-table entry. It says: anything arriving at public_ip:8443 goes to 192.168.1.50:443, always.

That is how you run a server behind NAT: a game server, a home lab, a security camera. It punches a hole. Understand what that means. The port is now open to the entire internet, not just to the friend you had in mind. Port forwarding does not authenticate. It forwards.

UPnP and NAT-PMP are protocols that let applications on your LAN create these mappings themselves, without asking you. A game console uses this to open the ports it needs. Malware can use it the same way. Many security guides tell you to turn UPnP off on your router. Now you know why: it lets any device inside your network reprogram the mailroom's logbook.

Stateful vs stateless packet filtering

A firewall's job is allow/deny per packet. There are two philosophies.

Stateless filtering judges every packet in isolation, against a fixed rule list. It looks at the header — the 5-tuple plus flags — and decides. It remembers nothing. This is simple and fast, which is why core routers and cloud network ACLs still use it. The cost: return traffic must be explicitly permitted. If you allow outbound TCP to port 443, you must also allow the inbound replies, usually with a broad rule like "allow established TCP from 443" — except a truly stateless filter cannot verify "established". It can only check that the ACK flag is set and trust it.

Stateful filtering keeps a connection table — notice the family resemblance to NAT's translation table. When it sees the start of a legitimate connection (a TCP SYN going out, or the first UDP packet of an exchange), it records the 5-tuple and the state. Return packets that match an entry are allowed automatically. Anything else falls through to the rule list. This is what "stateful inspection" means — a term Check Point coined in the 1990s, now generic. Consumer routers call it SPI. iptables implements it with the conntrack module and the -m state / -m conntrack match.

Two observations worth keeping. First, most real NAT devices are stateful firewalls wearing a trench coat. Your home router does PAT and stateful filtering in one box: the translation table and the connection table are often the same data structure. Second, statelessness is not stupidity. It is a performance choice. A stateless filter holds no per-connection state, so it cannot be exhausted by a flood of fake connections — but that is also why cloud providers pair stateless network ACLs with stateful security groups. Each covers the other's weakness.

4. The exact structure

The firewall rule: 5-tuple anatomy

Every packet-filter rule, on every platform, is built from the same ingredients. The 5-tuple is the packet's identity; the rule adds direction, action, and usually an interface or zone:

action      protocol   src IP          src port   dst IP          dst port
──────────  ─────────  ──────────────  ─────────  ──────────────  ─────────
ALLOW       tcp        192.168.1.0/24  any        10.0.0.5        443
DENY        tcp        any             any        10.0.0.5        23
ALLOW       udp        any             any        8.8.8.8         53
DENY        all        any             any        any             any

Rules are evaluated top to bottom. The first match wins. That ordering is the source of half of all firewall misconfigurations: a broad ALLOW above a narrow DENY silently swallows it. The final DENY all — the implicit or explicit default-deny — is what makes a firewall a firewall rather than a sieve.

The same rule wears different uniforms per vendor. This is worth seeing once, because interviews and jobs will hand you all of them:

# Linux iptables — the classic. "-m conntrack --ctstate" is the stateful part.
iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 443 -m conntrack --ctstate NEW -j ACCEPT

# nftables — the modern Linux successor. One language, cleaner syntax.
# (table inet filter; chain input; shown as a single rule line)
tcp dport 443 ct state new accept

# Cisco IOS access list — stateless, numbered, top-to-bottom.
access-list 101 permit tcp 192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255 any eq 443
access-list 101 deny   ip any any

# AWS security group — stateful, but only ALLOW rules exist; default is deny.
# (expressed as console fields, not CLI)
Type: HTTPS | Protocol: TCP | Port: 443 | Source: 192.168.1.0/24

Note the AWS difference. Security groups have no DENY and no ordering — everything not explicitly allowed is dropped, and return traffic is auto-allowed because the group is stateful. AWS network ACLs, by contrast, are stateless and numbered like Cisco's. Two constructs, two philosophies, one cloud. When something "works locally but not in staging," the security group is suspect number one.

The translation-table entry

For completeness, here is what a NAT table row actually contains — more than the diagram showed:

inside local (private)      inside global (public)     outside (destination)
───────────────────────     ──────────────────────     ─────────────────────
192.168.1.10:51234  (TCP)   203.0.113.7:50000  (TCP)    93.184.216.34:443

Each entry binds the inside socket, the outside socket, the destination, the protocol, and a timer. Some NATs also record the interface. That is the entire data structure. Everything NAT does — outbound rewrite, inbound reverse, aging, port-forwarding exceptions — is an operation on this table.

The four states of a tracked connection

Linux's connection tracker — the engine inside iptables, nftables, and most consumer routers — classifies every packet into one of four states. You will see these names in firewall rules, so learn them exactly:

  • NEW — the first packet of a connection the tracker has not seen before. For TCP, this means a SYN. For UDP, it means the first datagram of a new 5-tuple. Rules usually say "allow NEW to port 443" — that is the front door.
  • ESTABLISHED — a packet belonging to a connection the tracker already knows, in either direction. The overwhelmingly common rule is "allow everything ESTABLISHED" — that is how replies get back without a second rule.
  • RELATED — a packet that starts a new connection but belongs to an existing one. The classic case is FTP active mode: the control connection is ESTABLISHED, and the server's inbound data connection is RELATED to it. ICMP errors (like "destination unreachable") quoting the original packet are RELATED too. This state exists so helper modules can bless traffic the base rules would otherwise drop.
  • INVALID — a packet the tracker cannot associate with any connection and cannot classify as NEW: a stray ACK with no SYN seen, a packet with impossible flag combinations, an out-of-window sequence number. The safe default is to drop INVALID early. Attackers and broken stacks both produce these.

A minimal, sane Linux firewall is three rules long: allow loopback, allow ESTABLISHED and RELATED, allow NEW to the ports you serve, drop the rest. Every enterprise firewall you will ever meet is a fancier version of that sentence.

5. A realistic example

A 200-person company. One ISP link, one public address: 203.0.113.7. Inside, 10.0.0.0/8 for everything — laptops, printers, the CI runners.

Morning: 200 laptops open browsers, Slack, and email. Each opens dozens of connections. PAT hands out public ports from its pool — 64512 of them in the standard dynamic range (49152–65535 per RFC 6335). Two hundred people times fifty connections is 10,000 mappings. Comfortable.

Afternoon: the data team kicks off a load test from a single CI runner — 60,000 parallel connections to a staging API. The NAT pool has ~64k ports total, and each destination got its own mapping. The pool drains. New connections from the whole office start failing with timeouts that look exactly like the internet is down. The fix is not "the internet is down". The fix is a second public IP, or source-port reuse across destinations (some NATs key mappings per destination, squeezing more out of the pool), or running the load test outside the NAT.

Meanwhile the firewall: the office policy says engineers can SSH to production, nobody else can. One rule: ALLOW tcp from 10.0.4.0/24 (engineering VLAN) to prod-bastion:22, then default deny. A new hire on the guest Wi-Fi (10.0.99.0/24) tries to SSH to prod and fails. The ticket says "SSH is broken". SSH is fine. The rule is doing its job. Half of firewall troubleshooting is distinguishing "the control works" from "the control is misconfigured" — and the only way to tell is to read the rules, in order, against the actual 5-tuple of the failing traffic.

And the mailroom metaphor's last lesson: the company wants to host a small status page from a server inside the office. One static port-forward — 203.0.113.7:443 to 10.0.2.20:443 — and it is reachable worldwide. It is also now scanned by bots within minutes. Port forwarding is exposure. Treat it like publishing, because that is what it is.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"NAT is a firewall." No. NAT translates addresses. The inbound-drop behavior is incidental — there is no mapping, so there is nowhere to deliver. A stateful firewall enforces policy deliberately, including on traffic NAT would happily translate. Your home router does both, which is why the confusion survives. In an interview, say the sentence: NAT provides no security policy; the drop is a side effect of a missing mapping.

"NAT is needed for IPv6." No. IPv6 restores end-to-end addressing — every device can have a public address again. NAT66 exists as a product feature, but the standards community discourages it. The firewall still matters in IPv6; the NAT does not.

"Private addresses are encrypted / hidden / safe." They are merely unroutable. Anyone on the path — your ISP, a compromised router, the VPN concentrator — sees them plainly in the header. Privacy comes from encryption (TLS, IPsec), not from RFC 1918.

"Port forwarding only opens the port for the person I told." It opens the port for the entire internet. There is no allowlist in a port-forward rule. If you need restricted access, that is a firewall rule or a VPN — not a forward.

"More NAT layers = more security." Carrier-grade NAT (your ISP NATing you, then your router NATing again) adds latency, breaks protocols, and complicates troubleshooting. It adds zero security policy. Double NAT is a deployment accident, not a design.

Ordering firewall rules wrong. The classic: a broad ALLOW above a specific DENY, or forgetting the implicit deny at the bottom. Always read rules top-down against the failing 5-tuple. On Cisco-style ACLs, remember the invisible deny ip any any at the end — traffic you did not explicitly permit is dropped, and the router will not warn you.

Forgetting that UDP has no "connection". A stateful filter fakes UDP state with timers: first packet out creates an entry, replies within the timeout window are allowed. Set the timeout too short and DNS or QUIC breaks intermittently. Set it too long and the table fills with dead entries. UDP timeouts are the quiet cause of "works for a minute, then stops" bugs.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Two hosts behind the same PAT router both open a connection from source port 51234 to the same server port 443. How does the router keep the replies straight?

It does not rely on the private source port at all. On the way out, it assigns each conversation a different public source port — say 50000 and 50001 — and records both mappings in the translation table. The server sees two distinct 5-tuples: (203.0.113.7, 50000, server, 443, TCP) and (203.0.113.7, 50001, server, 443, TCP). When a reply arrives at public port 50001, the router looks up that port in the table and reverses the rewrite to the correct private host and port. The disambiguator is the public port, not the private one.

Q2: Is NAT a security mechanism? Defend your answer.

No — not by itself. NAT rewrites addresses; it enforces no policy. The reason unsolicited inbound packets get dropped is incidental: there is no translation-table entry, so the router has nowhere to send them. The moment you add a port-forwarding rule, that protection vanishes for the forwarded port, which proves it was never a policy. A stateful firewall is the actual security control: it holds explicit allow/deny rules and a connection table built from observed, policy-checked traffic. In practice the same box does both, which is why people conflate them. Name the distinction and you stand out.

Q3: What breaks when you put NAT in the path?

Three categories. First, inbound initiation: nobody outside can open a connection to a host behind NAT without a port-forward or a relay — this is why peer-to-peer apps need STUN/TURN hole-punching. Second, protocols that embed addresses in their payloads: classic FTP active mode and SIP put the private IP inside the application data, where the NAT cannot (or should not) rewrite it; helpers called ALGs exist for some of these, and they are famously buggy. Third, protocols that protect the header: IPsec AH authenticates the IP header, so NAT's rewrite invalidates the signature — AH does not survive NAT; ESP in tunnel mode with NAT-Traversal (UDP encapsulation on port 4500, RFC 3948) is the standard workaround.

Q4: A stateful firewall and a stateless firewall both sit in front of a web server. What does each one need to allow HTTPS to work?

The stateful firewall needs one rule: allow inbound TCP to port 443 (or allow outbound from the clients, depending on direction). When the SYN arrives, it creates a connection-table entry, and the SYN-ACK and everything after match the entry automatically. The stateless firewall needs at least two rules: allow inbound TCP to 443, and allow the return traffic — typically "allow TCP with the ACK flag set from source port 443". It cannot verify the return packet belongs to a real connection; it can only check the flags. That is the fundamental trade: stateful is easier to get right, stateless holds no per-connection state and cannot be exhausted by state-table floods.

Q5: Your office shares one public IP. A load test from a single machine opens 70,000 concurrent connections and the whole office loses internet. What happened, and what are the fixes?

Port exhaustion. The PAT pool is roughly 64k ports per public IP (fewer in the standard dynamic range), and each mapping consumes one. The load test ate the pool, so no new mappings could be created for anyone — every new connection in the office failed. Fixes: add more public IPs to the NAT pool, run the load test from outside the NAT (or from a host with its own address), reduce connection concurrency, or use a NAT that keys mappings per destination so ports can be reused across different destinations. Note the diagnostic trap: it looks like the internet is down, but the ISP link is fine — the NAT table is the bottleneck.

8. Try it

These run on your Mac. Every machine's addresses differ, so compare structure, not values.

# Your private address — the "inside local" from the mailroom metaphor.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
ipconfig getifaddr en0

# Your public address — what the internet sees after PAT rewrites you.
# Compare it with the private address above. They will differ.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ (needs internet)
curl -s https://api.ipify.org; echo

# Your default gateway — the NAT router itself, almost always.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
netstat -rn | grep default

# macOS's packet filter state: NAT rules and active filter rules.
# Empty output is normal on a Mac — the point is seeing where they'd live.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
sudo pfctl -s nat
sudo pfctl -s rules

# macOS application firewall state (the popup that asks "allow incoming connections?").
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
sudo /usr/libexec/ApplicationFirewall/socketfilterfw --getglobalstate

# Which ports your Mac is listening on — these are what a port-forward
# would expose to the whole internet. Read it before forwarding anything.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
lsof -iTCP -sTCP:LISTEN -P -n | head -20

And the simulator from this module — it runs anywhere Python 3.13 does:

# run on your Mac — your output will differ only in nothing; it's deterministic
python3 ~/workspace/build/networking-guide/code/m15/nat_simulator.py

Real output (from the verified run — the key lines):

Two laptops browse the same web server from behind one public IP:
  [new]     192.168.1.10:51234 -> 93.184.216.34:443 mapped to 203.0.113.7:50000
  [new]     192.168.1.11:51234 -> 93.184.216.34:443 mapped to 203.0.113.7:50001
  [new]     192.168.1.10:51235 -> 93.184.216.34:443 mapped to 203.0.113.7:50002

Replies come back and get un-translated:
  [reverse] 203.0.113.7:50001 -> 192.168.1.11:51234
  [reverse] 203.0.113.7:50000 -> 192.168.1.10:51234

A stranger knocks on a random port — nothing in the table:
  [DROP]    no table entry for 203.0.113.7:50099 — unsolicited inbound, dropped

Stale TCP mappings time out and their ports are recycled:
  [timeout] 192.168.1.10:51234 released 203.0.113.7:50000
  [timeout] 192.168.1.11:51234 released 203.0.113.7:50001
  [timeout] 192.168.1.10:51235 released 203.0.113.7:50002

Two things to notice in the output. Both laptops chose source port 51234, and the router still told them apart — the public port is the disambiguator. And the unsolicited knock on 50099 was dropped with no rule written anywhere: no table entry, no delivery. That is the incidental "security" people mistake for a firewall.

Key terms: NAT, PAT/NAPT, static NAT, dynamic NAT, port forwarding, translation table, inside local/global, stateful inspection, stateless filtering, 5-tuple, default deny, RFC 1918, RFC 3022, RFC 4787, RFC 5382, RFC 6598.

Module 16 — VPNs & Tunnels

Packets inside packets, and how to build a private network on top of a public one.

1. Why it matters

Your laptop on a café Wi-Fi is on a hostile network. The office file server is behind the company firewall. The two need to talk as if the café did not exist — privately, and as if your laptop were plugged into the office LAN. That is what a VPN does. It builds a private network on top of the public internet.

Interviewers love this topic because it sits at the intersection of three things they care about: encapsulation (Module 2 again), cryptography used for a concrete purpose, and real operational trade-offs. "When would you pick WireGuard over IPsec?" is a senior-level question with no single right answer. This module gives you the mental models to answer it like someone who has run both.

A note on scope: this module is concepts only. No configuration files, no step-by-step server setup. The goal is that you can reason about tunnels, compare the approaches, and know which one fits a given situation. The configs change with every vendor. The concepts do not.

2. The mental model

A tunnel is encapsulation with a purpose. Go back to Module 2: encapsulation means wrapping one protocol's packet inside another's. A tunnel wraps an entire IP packet — header and all — inside a new packet, then usually encrypts the inner one.

The mailroom metaphor, one last time. You want to send a confidential letter through a postal system you do not trust. You put the letter in an envelope addressed to your colleague (the inner packet). Then you put that envelope inside a second envelope addressed to a trusted courier's office (the outer packet), and you seal the inner envelope with wax (encryption). Every post office along the way sees only the outer envelope: from you, to the courier. The courier breaks the wax, reads the inner address, and delivers the letter. Nobody in the middle learned who the real recipient was or what the letter said.

That is a tunnel. The outer packet gets the data across the untrusted network. The inner packet carries the real conversation. Encryption makes the inner packet opaque to everyone except the tunnel endpoints.

Two consequences fall out immediately. First, the endpoints do the work: encryption, decryption, wrapping, unwrapping. The network in the middle just forwards outer packets like any others. Second, a tunnel can carry traffic the middle could never route directly — private RFC 1918 addresses, for instance. Your laptop gets an office-internal address like 10.8.0.5 on its virtual tunnel interface, and to every application on the laptop it looks directly connected to the office LAN. The café's router never sees a 10.x packet. It sees only encrypted outer packets addressed to the VPN server.

3. How it works

The anatomy of a tunneled packet

Here is what actually goes on the wire when your laptop talks to the office server through a tunnel:

ESP is the Encapsulating Security Payload (RFC 4303). It is the "wax seal": it encrypts the inner packet and adds an authentication tag so tampering is detected. Every serious VPN uses ESP or something equivalent for the data path. The approaches differ mostly in the handshake and in how much machinery surrounds it.

IPsec: transport mode vs tunnel mode

IPsec (RFC 4301) is the standards-based suite — not one protocol but a framework: ESP for the data path, IKEv2 (RFC 7296) for the handshake. It has two modes, and the difference is exactly what gets encapsulated.

Transport mode protects the payload of an IP packet but leaves the original IP header in place. The packet keeps its real source and destination addresses; ESP is inserted between the IP header and the TCP/UDP header. Use it when two endpoints talk directly to each other — host to host — and there is no NAT or private addressing in the way. Two servers in the same data center encrypting their replication traffic: transport mode.

Tunnel mode encapsulates the entire original IP packet — header included — inside a new outer IP packet, as the diagram above shows. The inner addresses are hidden. Use it when the endpoints are gateways rather than the final hosts: your laptop to the office VPN concentrator, or office router to office router (site-to-site). The inner packet can carry private addresses the public internet could never route.

A word on that last line, because it connects two modules. ESP is its own IP protocol (protocol number 50) — not TCP, not UDP. NAT's translation table keys on ports, and ESP has no ports. So a NAT device cannot multiplex ESP flows; at best it can pass one through. NAT-Traversal fixes this by wrapping ESP packets in UDP datagrams on port 4500. Now the NAT sees ordinary UDP with ports, and everything works. IKEv2 detects the NAT automatically during the handshake and switches to NAT-T on its own. When someone asks "why is my IPsec VPN slow to establish behind a hotel router," NAT-T negotiation is the machinery involved.

The return trip: routing is half the VPN

Getting your packet into the tunnel is only half the job. The office server must be able to send the reply back. That sounds automatic, but it is the most common site-to-site VPN bug in existence.

Your laptop, tunneled in as 10.8.0.5, sends a packet to the file server at 10.1.2.20. The VPN concentrator decrypts it and drops it on the office LAN with source 10.8.0.5. The file server replies to 10.8.0.5 — and its routing table has no idea where that is. Its default gateway is the office router, which sends it toward the internet, where it dies. The tunnel shows "connected." Packets flow one way. Nothing works.

The fix is a route: the office LAN must know that 10.8.0.0/24 (the VPN client pool) is reachable via the VPN concentrator. In small setups that is a static route on the LAN's default router. In larger ones the concentrator advertises the pool into the office's routing protocol. Either way, the principle is symmetric: a VPN is not up until traffic flows both ways, and the return path is a routing problem, not a VPN problem. When a tunnel is "connected but nothing loads," check the route back before touching the VPN config.

WireGuard: the modern take

WireGuard is not an RFC. It is a protocol described in a 2017 research paper (by Jason Donenfeld, presented at NDSS), with a reference implementation that is now part of the Linux kernel. Its pitch is not a new cipher. Its pitch is radical simplicity: the entire protocol is a few thousand lines of code, versus the hundreds of thousands in a typical IPsec stack. Fewer lines, fewer bugs, easier audits.

The handshake is the famous part: 1-RTT. One round trip — one message each way — and both sides have authenticated each other and agreed on session keys. It uses the Noise protocol framework's IK pattern: each side has a static long-term keypair, and the handshake mixes in ephemeral keys for forward secrecy. Compare that with IKEv2's multi-round exchange, and you see the appeal for mobile devices bouncing between cell towers: reconnects are nearly instant.

WireGuard's other big idea is cryptokey routing. There is no negotiation of "interesting traffic" like IPsec's security policies. The configuration is a table: this public key is allowed to use these IP addresses inside the tunnel. A packet arriving decrypted with key K gets routed according to K's allowed IPs; a packet leaving for an inner address gets encrypted with the key that owns that address. Identity and routing are the same table. It is elegant, and it is why WireGuard configs are ten lines long.

What did simplicity cost? WireGuard is UDP-only, so it cannot disguise itself as HTTPS on port 443 — on networks that allow only TCP 443 out, it does not connect. It has no built-in concept of users, passwords, or certificates; identity is a static public key, and key distribution is your problem (fine for ten engineers, a project at ten thousand). And it does not hide the fact that you are using WireGuard: the packet shape is recognizable. None of these are flaws. They are the trade-offs the design chose.

TLS VPNs: when the network only speaks web

A TLS VPN tunnels packets inside a TLS connection — the same TLS your browser uses for HTTPS (TLS 1.3 is RFC 8446). OpenVPN is the well-known implementation: open source, around since 2001, but not itself a standard. Its superpower is camouflage. It can run over TCP port 443, which makes its traffic look like ordinary HTTPS to every firewall between you and the server. Networks that block IPsec's UDP 500/4500 and WireGuard's UDP outright will still let a TLS VPN through, because blocking 443 means blocking the web.

The cost is the TCP-over-TCP problem: tunneling TCP inside a TCP connection means two congestion-control loops fighting each other, which degrades badly on lossy networks. That is why OpenVPN prefers UDP mode when it can get away with it, and why modern TLS VPNs are careful about it. The handshake is also heavier than WireGuard's — a full TLS handshake with certificates — though session resumption softens that.

When each fits, in one breath: IPsec when you need the standard — site-to-site between vendors, compliance checklists, built-in OS clients. WireGuard when you control both ends and want speed and simplicity — engineer laptops, server meshes. TLS VPN when the network is hostile to everything else — contractors on locked-down corporate guest Wi-Fi, countries with aggressive filtering, any place where only port 443 escapes.

The handshake, concretely

"Handshake" is vague until you see what is actually exchanged. The three protocols do the same job with very different message counts.

IKEv2 (RFC 7296) negotiates in two phases. First, IKE_SA_INIT: the two sides exchange Diffie-Hellman public values and nonces — two messages — and from those they derive the keys that protect the rest of the handshake itself. Then IKE_AUTH: each side proves its identity (certificate, pre-shared key, or EAP exchange for a username/password) and negotiates the first child security association — the actual ESP parameters. Four messages minimum, more with EAP or certificate chains. It is a negotiation in the full sense: the initiator proposes cipher suites, the responder picks. That flexibility is why IPsec interoperates across vendors — and why its configuration is famously fiddly.

WireGuard does it in one round trip. The initiator sends a handshake initiation containing its ephemeral public key, encrypted to the responder's static public key. The responder replies with its own ephemeral key, encrypted to the initiator's. Both sides now compute the same session keys. There is no negotiation at all: the cipher suite is fixed (ChaCha20-Poly1305, Curve25519, BLAKE2s), so there is nothing to agree on. If the responder does not recognize the initiator's static key, it stays silent — it does not even send a rejection, which makes scanning for WireGuard servers unrewarding.

TLS VPNs run a standard TLS handshake first — ClientHello, ServerHello, certificate verification, key exchange — then authenticate the user inside the encrypted channel (username/password, client certificate, or both), then negotiate the tunnel parameters over a control channel. It is the heaviest of the three, but every step is familiar web technology, which is why it is easy to operate and easy to get through firewalls.

Notice the pattern: flexibility and negotiation cost round trips. Fixed choices cost agility. Every VPN handshake lives somewhere on that line.

4. The exact structure

What the handshake establishes

Every VPN handshake, regardless of protocol, produces the same three things. Learn these and you can read any VPN's documentation:

  1. Authentication — proof of identity on both sides. IKEv2 uses certificates or pre-shared keys (often EAP for users). WireGuard uses static public keys. TLS VPNs use X.509 certificates like the web.
  2. Session keys — fresh symmetric keys for this session, with forward secrecy: even if the long-term key leaks later, past sessions stay unreadable. This is the property the ephemeral Diffie-Hellman exchange buys.
  3. The security association — agreement on what is protected: which ciphers, which traffic selectors (for IPsec: which subnets flow through the tunnel), lifetimes and rekey intervals.

The data path then does the same job everywhere: encrypt each packet, authenticate it (so tampering is detected), add a sequence number (so replays are detected), wrap it in the outer header, send it. ESP's trailer carries the authentication tag and padding. WireGuard's data messages carry a nonce and an authentication tag under ChaCha20-Poly1305. Different ciphers, same skeleton.

Rekeying: why sessions do not last forever

Session keys have lifetimes. IPsec negotiates them — typically hours for the IKE session, shorter for the ESP child sessions — and rekeys before they expire, without dropping traffic. WireGuard rotates data-path keys every couple of minutes, continuously, with no handshake involved: each side just starts using the next key derived from the ongoing chain.

The reason is exposure math. A key used to encrypt terabytes over many hours gives an attacker more material and more time than a key that lives for minutes. Rekeying bounds the damage of any single compromised key. It also means "the VPN dropped at 3 AM" is sometimes just a failed rekey — expired credentials, a changed certificate, clock skew breaking authentication. When a long-lived tunnel dies on a schedule, suspect the rekey before the network.

Split tunneling: the deliberate leak

By default, a VPN can route all your traffic through the tunnel (full tunnel) or only the office-bound traffic (split tunnel). Split tunneling sends 10.0.0.0/8 through the VPN and everything else — YouTube, personal email — straight out the local connection. It saves the company bandwidth and keeps Netflix fast. It also means the local network sees your non-office traffic, and a compromised laptop is simultaneously on the office LAN and the café Wi-Fi — a bridge the security team did not intend. Enterprises argue about this endlessly. The conservative posture is full tunnel with DNS through the VPN too; the pragmatic one is split tunnel with the risks written down. Know that the choice exists, because "why is my internet slow on the VPN" is almost always full-tunnel routing, and "the VPN doesn't protect my browsing" is almost always split tunnel.

5. A realistic example

A 300-person company. Two offices — New York and London — plus engineers working from home and contractors on client sites.

Office to office runs IPsec in tunnel mode between the two routers. The New York LAN is 10.1.0.0/16, London is 10.2.0.0/16. The tunnel's traffic selectors say exactly that: packets for 10.2.0.0/16 get encapsulated toward London's gateway, and vice versa. Nobody configures anything per host. To every machine in both offices, the other office looks like a local subnet. The routers rekey every few hours without anyone noticing. This is IPsec's home turf: standards-based, every enterprise firewall speaks it, and the auditors recognize the names.

Engineers at home get WireGuard. Each engineer generates a keypair, sends the public key to the ops team, and gets a ten-line config assigning them 10.8.0.x. Connections establish in a round trip. When a laptop roams from home Wi-Fi to a phone hotspot, the tunnel survives — WireGuard has no fragile session to break, just keys and counters. Ops loves it because there is almost nothing to debug.

Contractors on locked-down client networks get the TLS VPN. The client's guest Wi-Fi allows only TCP 443 outbound. IPsec's UDP 500 never leaves the building; WireGuard's UDP is dropped silently. The TLS VPN on TCP 443 sails through — to the client's firewall it is indistinguishable from web browsing. It is slower under packet loss (TCP-over-TCP), but it connects, which beats fast and blocked.

One company, three VPN technologies, each chosen for the network it has to cross. That is the real skill: not configuring any one of them, but knowing which one the situation calls for.

Tunnels inside tunnels

It happens more than you would think. An engineer on the office network — already behind the site-to-site IPsec tunnel — opens a WireGuard tunnel to a personal server. A contractor's TLS VPN runs inside a corporate network that itself tunnels to a data center. Each layer adds its own headers and its own encryption, and each layer costs MTU and CPU.

Two rules for nested tunnels. First, the MTUs compound: every layer eats 40–80 bytes, so a 1500-byte packet can end up fragmented twice before it reaches the wire. If nested tunnels behave strangely, lower the inner MTU deliberately rather than letting fragmentation sort it out. Second, keep the layering meaningful: each tunnel should cross a trust boundary. A VPN inside a VPN on the same trust domain is usually an accident of history, not a design. When you inherit one, ask which boundary each layer defends. The answer is sometimes "none" — and then you know what to simplify.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"A VPN makes me anonymous / private." A VPN moves trust; it does not remove it. Without a VPN, your ISP sees your traffic's destinations. With one, the VPN provider sees them instead — plus the decrypted inner packet, at least its headers. A commercial "privacy" VPN just changes who you trust. For the office VPN, the trust lands on your employer, which is the point: the company can inspect, log, and filter. Never confuse "encrypted on the café Wi-Fi" with "invisible to everyone."

"The VPN encrypts everything on my device." Only traffic routed through the tunnel. Split tunneling, IPv6 leaks (the VPN handles IPv4 but the OS happily sends IPv6 around it), and DNS going to the local resolver are the classic leaks. "My IP still shows my city on the VPN" is usually one of these three.

"IPsec is slow and WireGuard is fast, so WireGuard always wins." The speed difference people feel is usually the handshake and the codebase weight, not the cipher — AES-GCM on modern CPUs is fast everywhere. IPsec wins where you need multi-vendor interop, FIPS-certified modules, or the built-in clients in every OS. WireGuard wins where you control both ends. Benchmarks aside, the deciding factor is almost always operational, not cryptographic.

"TCP port 443 means it looks exactly like HTTPS." It looks like TLS on 443, which is enough for most firewalls. But deep packet inspection can still distinguish VPN handshakes from browser handshakes by timing and packet sizes. "Looks like web traffic" defeats port-based blocking, not determined analysis. In adversarial networks, that distinction matters.

"Transport mode is a lighter VPN for remote access." Transport mode does not hide the original IP header, so it cannot carry private addresses and cannot cross NAT usefully. It is for host-to-host protection (two servers, no gateways). Remote access and site-to-site are tunnel mode, nearly always.

Forgetting the MTU. Tunnels add headers — ESP, the outer IP header — which shrinks the payload that fits in one packet. If nothing adjusts, large packets get fragmented or dropped, and you get the classic symptom: small pages load, large downloads stall. The fix is MSS clamping (rewriting the TCP maximum segment size during the handshake) or proper path MTU discovery. "VPN is connected but some sites hang" — check the MTU before anything else.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Explain IPsec transport mode vs tunnel mode. When would you use each?

Transport mode inserts ESP between the original IP header and the payload — the header stays visible, only the payload is encrypted. Use it host-to-host, where the two endpoints are the actual sender and receiver and no NAT or private addressing sits between them. Tunnel mode encapsulates the entire original packet inside a new outer IP packet, hiding the inner addresses. Use it whenever a gateway is involved: remote-access VPN (client to concentrator) or site-to-site (router to router), and whenever inner addresses are private. The one-line version: transport protects a conversation; tunnel builds a network.

Q2: Why does IPsec need NAT-Traversal, and what does it do?

ESP is IP protocol 50 — neither TCP nor UDP, so it has no ports. NAT multiplexes flows by rewriting ports, which means it cannot track or multiplex bare ESP: at most one ESP flow passes, and many NATs drop it outright. NAT-Traversal (RFC 3948) wraps ESP packets in UDP datagrams on port 4500. The NAT then sees ordinary UDP with source and destination ports, creates a normal mapping, and everything works. IKEv2 detects a NAT in the path during the handshake and enables NAT-T automatically.

Q3: WireGuard's handshake is 1-RTT. Why is that a big deal, and what did the design give up to get there?

One round trip means connections — and reconnections — complete in a single exchange: critical for mobile clients hopping networks, where IKEv2's multi-round handshake visibly stalls. WireGuard gets there with the Noise IK pattern: long-term static keys identify both sides immediately, and ephemeral keys mixed into the same exchange provide forward secrecy. What it gave up: agility. There is no cipher negotiation, no user/password or certificate identity (keys are the identity, and distributing them is your problem), no UDP/TCP choice (UDP only, so TCP-443-only networks block it), and no traffic camouflage. Simplicity is the feature and the limitation.

Q4: A contractor is on a client network that only allows TCP 443 outbound. Which VPN do you give them, and why not the others?

A TLS VPN (like OpenVPN in TCP mode). IPsec needs UDP 500 for IKE and UDP 4500 for NAT-T — both blocked. WireGuard is UDP-only — blocked. A TLS VPN on TCP 443 is indistinguishable from HTTPS at the port level, so it passes. The trade-off to name: TCP-over-TCP meltdown on lossy networks makes it slower than the alternatives, but connectivity beats speed. This is the standard answer and the standard deployment.

Q5: Does a corporate VPN protect an employee's browsing from the employer?

No — the opposite. The VPN moves the trust boundary: traffic the ISP used to see is now decrypted at the VPN concentrator, inside the company's network, where it can be logged, filtered, and inspected. Full-tunnel VPNs route even personal browsing through the company. Split-tunnel VPNs only expose office-bound traffic. Either way, "the VPN" is not a privacy tool against the VPN's owner. The honest framing: a VPN protects traffic from the local network by trusting the remote network instead.

8. Try it

macOS speaks IKEv2 natively and shows tunnel interfaces as utun. These are read-only — safe to run.

# VPN connections macOS knows about (built-in IKEv2/IPsec profiles).
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
scutil --nc list

# Tunnel interfaces. Active VPNs appear as utun0, utun1, ...
# No utun interfaces means no VPN is currently up.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
ifconfig | grep -A3 '^utun'

# Routes pointing through the tunnel — this is what "full tunnel"
# vs "split tunnel" looks like in the routing table.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
netstat -rn | grep utun

# Which DNS resolvers you're using — on a proper VPN setup these
# should be the office resolvers, not the café's.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
scutil --dns | grep -A2 'resolver #1'

If you have the WireGuard app installed (App Store, free), one more:

# WireGuard's own status — handshake age tells you the 1-RTT story:
# a fresh "handshake ... seconds ago" after roaming networks is the
# fast-reconnect behavior from section 3, visible in one line.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
/Applications/WireGuard.app/Contents/MacOS/wireguard-go --version

VPN comparison

IPsec (IKEv2) WireGuard TLS VPN (e.g. OpenVPN)
Standard Yes — RFC 4301/4303/7296 No — research paper, open implementations TLS is standard (RFC 8446); the VPN framing is not
Layer Network (IP protocol 50 / UDP 500, 4500) Network (UDP, single port) Transport (TCP or UDP, often 443)
Handshake Multi-round (IKEv2) 1-RTT (Noise IK) Full TLS handshake
Identity Certificates, pre-shared keys, EAP users Static public keys X.509 certificates
NAT traversal NAT-T on UDP 4500 (RFC 3948), automatic Native — it is already UDP Native on UDP; TCP mode needs nothing
Firewall escape Often blocked (UDP 500/4500) Often blocked (UDP) Best — TCP 443 looks like HTTPS
Configuration Heavy — policies, selectors, lifetimes ~10 lines — keys and allowed IPs Moderate — certs plus server config
Best fit Site-to-site, compliance, built-in OS clients Controlled endpoints, roaming clients, meshes Hostile networks, contractors, last resort that always works

Key terms: tunnel, encapsulation, ESP, IKEv2, transport mode, tunnel mode, NAT-T, WireGuard, Noise, cryptokey routing, 1-RTT, forward secrecy, TLS VPN, split tunneling, security association, RFC 4301, RFC 4303, RFC 7296, RFC 3948, RFC 8446.

Module 17 — Network Security Deep Dive

Assume breach. Compartmentalize everything. Verify every request.

1. Why it matters

Read any major breach postmortem and the shape is the same. The attacker got in through one door — a phishing email, an unpatched VPN, a leaked credential. Then they walked the hallways. Flat network, no internal barriers, servers that trusted anything on the LAN. The initial compromise took minutes. The lateral movement took months. The damage was total because the inside of the network was soft.

This module is about making the inside hard. Not with one product, but with an architecture: segmentation that limits how far any compromise can spread, zero-trust principles that stop trusting the network location, and DDoS defenses matched to the layer being attacked. If Modules 15 and 16 were about individual mechanisms, this one is about how they compose into a defensible whole.

For interviews, this is where you show you think like an architect rather than a tool operator. "We put a firewall at the edge" is a junior answer. "We segmented the payment workloads into their own zone with default-deny between zones, and every service authenticates with workload identity regardless of network location" is a senior one.

2. The mental model

Forget the castle. The castle model — thick walls, trusted interior — is exactly what failed in every breach postmortem above. Think instead of a submarine. A submarine is divided into watertight compartments. A breach in one compartment floods one compartment. The crew seals the hatches, and the vessel survives.

Your network is the submarine. Segmentation is the watertight doors. Zero trust is the rule that every sailor shows ID at every hatch, even the ones they just came through. DDoS defense is the hull plating — it does not stop a torpedo, but it decides how big a wave the ship can ride out.

Two ideas carry the whole module. First, breach is assumed: design as if the attacker is already inside, because eventually they will be. Second, every layer compensates for the last: no single control is complete, so each one covers the gaps of the one outside it. The firewall misses something; segmentation contains it. Segmentation is bypassed; host controls catch it. That is defense in depth, and it is the opposite of buying one expensive box and relaxing.

3. How it works

Layered defenses

Here is the stack, outside to in. Each layer assumes the one outside it has failed:

Read it as a story of failure. The perimeter stops the volumetric DDoS and the port scans. Something targeted gets through — a phished credential, a zero-day. Segmentation keeps it in one zone. The IDS notices the odd lateral connection the firewall rules technically allowed. The host's EDR kills the payload. The application's auth refuses the stolen session because the device posture changed. And if all of that fails, the stolen database is encrypted and the backups are offline.

No layer is optional because no layer is sufficient. That sentence is the whole philosophy.

Detection: IDS vs IPS, signatures vs anomalies

Layer 3 in the diagram says "IDS/IPS" without explaining it. The distinction matters.

An IDS (Intrusion Detection System) watches a copy of the traffic and raises alerts. It sits beside the wire — usually on a span port or tap — so it cannot block anything. It sees everything and stops nothing. An IPS (Intrusion Prevention System) sits in the wire: traffic flows through it, and it can drop packets. Same engine, different placement, very different risk: a false positive on an IDS is a noisy alert; a false positive on an IPS is an outage. That is why IPS deployments start in detect-only mode and earn blocking one signature at a time.

Both detect two ways. Signature-based detection matches traffic against known-bad patterns — a Snort rule for a specific exploit, a known malware callback domain. Precise, low false positives, blind to anything new. Anomaly-based detection learns what normal looks like and flags deviations: a workstation suddenly talking to fifty hosts, a service account logging in at 3 AM from a new country. Noisy, but it catches the novel. Mature shops run both: signatures for the known, anomalies for the unknown, and a human deciding which alerts matter. The human is not optional. Detection without triage is just a louder way to be breached.

Segmentation in three steps

Segmentation means dividing the network so traffic between parts passes through a control point. Three generations of the idea, each finer than the last:

VLANs are the coarsest cut. A VLAN (IEEE 802.1Q) splits one physical switch into multiple logical Layer-2 networks. Engineering is VLAN 10, guests are VLAN 99, and their broadcasts never meet. But — and this is the part people get wrong — a VLAN by itself is not a security boundary. It is a separation of broadcast domains. Traffic between VLANs routes through a Layer-3 device, and unless that device enforces policy (ACLs, a firewall), the VLANs can still talk freely. A VLAN without inter-VLAN filtering is just tidier, not safer.

Firewall zones add the policy. You group interfaces into zones — inside, outside, DMZ — and write rules about zone-to-zone traffic: inside can initiate to outside, outside can initiate to DMZ web servers on 443 only, DMZ can initiate to inside on nothing. (Vendors name this differently: Palo Alto calls them zones with security policies, Cisco ASA calls them security levels, Linux does it with interface-based rules. Same idea.) The DMZ — demilitarized zone — is the classic: public-facing servers live there, isolated from both the internet and the internal LAN, so a compromised web server cannot reach the database directly.

Microsegmentation takes the zone idea down to the workload. Instead of "the database VLAN can talk to the app VLAN," the policy is "this specific app instance can talk to that specific database instance on port 5432, and nothing else can." Enforcement moves into the hypervisor, the container host, or a host agent — the network fabric itself carries identity. VMware NSX popularized it; Kubernetes NetworkPolicies are the container-native version. The granularity is the point: when every workload has its own perimeter, lateral movement has nowhere to go.

Zero trust: stop trusting the address

Zero trust starts from an observation: the network location tells you nothing about trustworthiness. The laptop on the office LAN might be compromised. The phone on the café Wi-Fi might be the CEO's, fully patched, with hardware-backed keys. "Inside = trusted" was never true; it was convenient.

So zero trust inverts it. Never trust, always verify. Every request — user to app, service to service — is authenticated, authorized, and encrypted based on identity, not on where the packet came from. The identity can be a user (with MFA), a device (with a health certificate: patched, encrypted disk, EDR running), or a workload (a SPIFFE-style identity a service proves cryptographically). The policy engine asks: this identity, on this device, in this posture, asking for this resource — allowed? Then it re-asks continuously, because posture changes.

In plain language: identity-aware access means the firewall rule is no longer "allow 10.0.4.0/24 to the database." It is "allow the payments service, running on a compliant host, presenting a valid workload certificate, to the database." The IP address becomes a routing detail, not a credential. NIST SP 800-207 is the canonical reference — the standard's name for this is Zero Trust Architecture, and it is worth knowing the number because interviewers cite it.

Be honest about what zero trust is not. It is not a product you buy — vendors sell "zero trust solutions," but zero trust is an architecture of many controls: strong identity, device posture, microsegmentation, continuous verification, least privilege. It is not the absence of a perimeter either; you still filter the edge. It is the refusal to let the perimeter's verdict be the final one.

NIST SP 800-207 gives the machinery three names worth knowing. The Policy Engine makes the decision: given this identity, device, and context, is access granted? The Policy Administrator carries it out: it configures the enforcement points, opens the connection, shuts it down when the session ends or posture changes. The Policy Enforcement Point sits in front of the resource and gates every request — the bouncer that asks the engine before letting anyone through. Every "zero trust" product you will ever evaluate is some arrangement of these three roles. When a vendor demo gets hand-wavy, ask: where is your policy engine, where are the enforcement points, and what signals feed the decision? The good ones answer immediately.

DDoS by layer

Denial of service is the one attack that does not need to get in. It just needs to make the service unreachable. The defenses depend entirely on which layer is being flooded, because each layer exhausts a different resource.

L3/L4 floods: exhausting the machinery. A SYN flood (RFC 4987 describes the attack and mitigations) sends a storm of TCP SYNs with spoofed sources. The server allocates state for each half-open connection until its backlog fills, and legitimate SYNs get dropped. The honest mitigations: SYN cookies — the server encodes the connection state in the sequence number it sends back instead of storing it, so there is nothing to exhaust; SYN proxies at the edge that complete the handshake before the server ever sees it; and anycast, which spreads the flood across many points of presence so no single machine takes it all.

A UDP amplification attack is sneakier. The attacker sends a small request with a spoofed source address — your server's address — to a public UDP service (open DNS resolver, NTP server, memcached). The service sends a large response to the spoofed address: your server. Amplification factors of 50x or more turn a trickle into a flood. Two truths here. The victim-side mitigations — upstream filtering, scrubbing centers, anycast — only absorb the attack. The fix is at the source networks: BCP 38 ingress filtering (RFC 2827, updated by RFC 3704) says ISPs must drop packets whose source address does not belong to the customer sending them. Spoofed sources cannot leave a BCP-38-compliant network, and without spoofing there is no reflection. Twenty-plus years later, deployment is still incomplete, which is why amplification attacks still work.

L7 floods: exhausting the application. An HTTP flood sends perfectly legitimate-looking requests — thousands of clients asking for the expensive search page, or slowloris-style connections that dribble headers to hold workers open. Nothing is spoofed. Nothing is malformed. A packet filter sees normal HTTPS and waves it through. This is why L7 attacks are harder: the defense has to understand the application.

The mitigations, honestly: a WAF with behavioral analysis that distinguishes bot patterns from human ones; aggressive rate limiting per client, per endpoint, per API key; challenge pages (JavaScript or CAPTCHA) that cheap bots cannot pass; caching at the edge so the flood hits the CDN's cache instead of your origin; and autoscaling, which buys time but is not a fix — it just raises the attacker's bill alongside yours. The unglamorous winner is usually the cache: a flood for a cacheable resource is the CDN's problem, and CDNs are built for exactly that.

One more honest note: you cannot out-bandwidth a serious DDoS on your own. A determined attacker rents more bots than your pipe. That is why scrubbing services and CDNs exist — they aggregate capacity across thousands of customers. "We handle DDoS ourselves" is a statement about small attacks.

4. The exact structure

A zone policy matrix

This is what firewall-zone policy looks like when written down. Rows are the source zone, columns the destination. "Initiate" means new connections; replies are handled by statefulness (Module 15).

              │ to: outside   │ to: DMZ            │ to: inside
──────────────┼───────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────
from: outside │ —             │ initiate → :443    │ DENY
              │               │ only               │
from: DMZ     │ initiate →    │ —                  │ DENY
              │ updates, DNS  │                    │ (even the DB:
              │ only          │                    │ use a proxy)
from: inside  │ initiate      │ initiate → :443    │ —
              │ (stateful)    │ (stateful)         │ (internal
              │               │                    │ segmentation
              │               │                    │ applies)

Read the DMZ row. The web servers can initiate outbound for patches and DNS — narrowly — but cannot initiate anything into the inside zone. If an attacker compromises the web server, the matrix says where they can go: back out to the internet, nowhere inside. That single row is the difference between "the web server got owned" and "the company got owned." Note the parenthetical: the web server's database lives in the DMZ or is reached through a tightly scoped proxy, never by direct initiation into the inside zone.

Mapping controls to layers

When someone says "we need DDoS protection," the professional follow-up is "at which layer?" This table is the cheat sheet:

Layer        Attack examples              What actually helps
───────────  ───────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────
L3/L4        SYN flood, UDP flood,        SYN cookies/proxy, anycast,
             amplification, ping flood    upstream filtering, scrubbing,
                                          BCP 38 at source networks
L7           HTTP floods, slowloris,      WAF + behavioral analysis, rate
             credential stuffing          limiting, challenges, edge
                                          caching, autoscale (buys time)

If the mitigation and the layer do not match, it will not work. A WAF does nothing against a SYN flood — the packets never complete a handshake for the WAF to inspect. SYN cookies do nothing against an HTTP flood — every request completes a legitimate handshake. Match the tool to the layer. Say that sentence in an interview.

What a detection signature looks like

Signatures are the exact structure of layer 3. Here is a simplified Snort-style rule — the real syntax is close to this:

alert tcp any any -> 10.0.0.0/8 445 (
    msg:"SMB exploit attempt";
    content:"|ff|SMB"; depth:4;
    content:"|00 00|"; distance:30;
    threshold:type limit, track by_src, count 5, seconds 60;
    sid:1000001; )

Read it left to right: raise an alert (not a drop — this is IDS posture) on TCP traffic from anywhere to the internal network on port 445 (SMB), when the payload contains the SMB marker followed by a suspicious pattern, throttled to one alert per source per minute. The sid is the signature's ID — every signature has one, and tuning an IDS is largely the job of enabling, disabling, and thresholding SIDs. An IPS version of the same rule would say drop instead of alert, and you would test it for a month before letting it.

5. A realistic example

A mid-size SaaS company. One office, one AWS footprint, a payments service with PCI scope.

The office network is segmented before anything clever: engineering on one VLAN, finance on another, guests on a third with client isolation. Inter-VLAN traffic passes the firewall. Finance cannot initiate to engineering's CI runners. Guests can only reach the internet. This is thirty minutes of switch and firewall config, and it already defeats the "plugged into the conference room jack" attack.

The data center side uses zones. Public web servers and the WAF sit in the DMZ. Application servers are in an app zone. Databases in a data zone. The zone matrix is written down and reviewed quarterly — the review matters more than the initial config, because rules accrete. Every quarter, someone asks of each rule "do we still need this," and dead rules get deleted. Firewall rulebases rot like anything else.

The payments service gets microsegmentation. Its containers carry workload identity; the policy says the payments API can reach the payments database on 5432 and the token vault on 8200, and nothing else — not the replica, not the analytics warehouse, not the internet. When a vulnerability hits the payments API, the blast radius is the two flows it legitimately needs. The Kubernetes NetworkPolicies expressing this are under twenty lines of YAML. Small file, enormous containment.

Then the DDoS story. A competitor — or a bored teenager — points 100,000 bots at the marketing site. L7 flood: legitimate-looking GETs for the homepage. The CDN absorbs it: the homepage is cached at the edge, so the origin sees a ripple, not a wave. Rate limiting kicks in per IP, and the WAF's bot management challenges the worst offenders. The site stays up. Total origin impact: a slightly exciting afternoon for the on-call.

Six months later the attack is different: a UDP amplification flood, 300 Gbps, aimed at the company's authoritative DNS. This one is not absorbable at the origin. The DNS is anycast across a managed provider; the flood spreads across PoPs; upstream scrubbing filters the spoofed traffic. The fix that would have prevented it — BCP 38 on the attackers' networks — is not theirs to deploy. They survive it instead. That asymmetry is the honest truth about volumetric DDoS: you do not win, you endure, and you architect so that enduring is cheap.

And one quieter story, the kind that never makes the news. A developer's laptop is compromised through a malicious browser extension. The attacker's first move is the standard one: scan the local network for neighbors. The laptop is on the engineering VLAN — it finds CI runners and workstations, but the firewall between VLANs denies everything except the documented flows, so the scan returns a wall of filtered ports. The attacker tries the credentials harvested from the laptop against the VPN. The identity check passes — the password is right — but the device posture check fails: the laptop's EDR reported the extension as malicious an hour ago, and the compromised device certificate was revoked. Access denied. The IDS, meanwhile, flagged the scan pattern and paged the on-call, who isolated the laptop.

Nothing in that story is exotic. VLANs, a firewall rule, device posture, EDR, an IDS alert, a human. Six ordinary controls, each covering the last one's miss. The attacker got a laptop and nothing else. That is what the submarine looks like from the inside: not dramatic, just sealed.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"VLANs are a security boundary." They separate broadcast domains. Without inter-VLAN filtering at Layer 3, hosts on different VLANs can still route to each other freely. Worse, VLAN hopping attacks (double-tagging) can jump VLANs on misconfigured trunk ports. Treat VLANs as organization, and put the enforcement in the firewall between them.

"We have a next-gen firewall, so we're covered." A firewall enforces the policy you wrote. It does not write it, review it, or notice when it rotted. The most common firewall finding in audits is not a missing feature — it is a five-year-old ANY-ANY rule nobody remembers approving. Default deny, quarterly reviews, and change tickets beat any feature list.

"Zero trust means no perimeter / no VPN." Zero trust means the perimeter is not the trust decision. You still filter at the edge — there is no reason to let the whole internet port-scan your office. And the VPN does not disappear; it becomes one verified path among others, with device posture checked at connect time. Anyone selling "zero trust" as "delete your firewalls" is selling something.

"DDoS protection = bigger pipes." Bandwidth absorbs volumetric floods up to a point, and then the bill arrives. Real protection is layered: absorb what you can (anycast, CDN), filter what you can (scrubbing, WAF), and fix the ecosystem causes you control (no open resolvers, BCP 38 on your own egress). "Just add bandwidth" is the most expensive possible strategy.

"Encryption solves data theft." Encryption at rest protects against stolen disks, not stolen credentials. If the attacker authenticates as a legitimate user — the usual case — the application happily decrypts for them. Data-layer defense means access logging, anomaly detection on access patterns, and backups that survive ransomware (offline, immutable, tested). The backup you have never restored is not a backup.

"Internal traffic is safe because it's inside." This is the castle model in one sentence, and it is the assumption behind every flat-network breach. Inside traffic deserves encryption (mTLS between services, not just at the edge), authentication (workload identity, not IP allowlists), and the same suspicion as external traffic. Assume breach is not paranoia. It is the postmortem talking.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: What is the difference between a VLAN and a firewall zone?

A VLAN is a Layer-2 construct: it splits a switch into separate broadcast domains (IEEE 802.1Q tagging). It organizes traffic but enforces nothing by itself — inter-VLAN traffic routes through a Layer-3 device, and without ACLs or firewall rules there, the VLANs communicate freely. A firewall zone is a policy construct: interfaces are grouped into zones (inside, outside, DMZ), and explicit rules govern which zones can initiate connections to which. The zone is where the security decision lives. The crisp version: VLANs separate the wire; zones enforce the policy. You need both, and the VLAN alone is not a boundary.

Q2: Explain zero trust to a skeptical senior engineer in one minute.

"The network location never proved anything — we've all seen compromised laptops on the office LAN. Zero trust stops using location as a trust signal. Every request is authenticated and authorized on identity: which user, which device, what health posture, which workload — and re-verified continuously, not once at login. Practically that means mTLS between services with workload identity instead of IP allowlists, device compliance checks before access, and microsegmentation so a compromised service can only reach what it needs. It's not a product and it doesn't delete the perimeter. It's the assumption that breach has already happened, designed in from the start." Then name NIST SP 800-207 as the reference architecture.

Q3: How do mitigations differ between a SYN flood and an HTTP flood? Why can't one tool handle both?

They exhaust different resources at different layers. A SYN flood exhausts the server's connection backlog with half-open handshakes — the fix is at L4: SYN cookies (encode state in the sequence number instead of storing it), SYN proxies at the edge, anycast to spread the load. An HTTP flood exhausts application workers with legitimate-looking requests — every handshake completes, so L4 tricks see nothing wrong. The fix is at L7: WAF behavioral analysis, rate limiting, bot challenges, edge caching. One tool cannot handle both because the SYN flood never becomes an HTTP request and the HTTP flood never looks anomalous at L4. Match the mitigation to the layer.

Q4: Why are UDP amplification attacks possible, and what actually fixes them?

Two enablers. First, UDP is stateless with no handshake, so a request can carry any source address — including a forged one. Second, some UDP services (open DNS resolvers, NTP, memcached) reply with responses much larger than the request, giving amplification. The attacker sends small spoofed requests; the victim drowns in large replies. Victim-side defenses (scrubbing, anycast, upstream filtering) only absorb attacks. The actual fix is BCP 38 ingress filtering: networks must drop outbound packets with source addresses that don't belong to them. Without spoofing, reflection is impossible. It has been the answer for over two decades; deployment is still incomplete, which is why the attacks persist. Know both halves: how to survive one, and what would end them.

Q5: Your app sits behind a WAF and still falls over during an attack. Walk me through your thinking.

First, identify the layer. If it's a volumetric L3/L4 flood, the WAF is irrelevant — it inspects HTTP, and the flood may never complete a handshake; the fix is upstream (scrubbing, anycast, CDN). If it's L7, the WAF sees the traffic, so ask why it didn't help: is the attack hitting an uncached dynamic endpoint (cache the edge or rate-limit that route)? Are the bots rotating IPs faster than the rate limiter's window? Is the WAF in monitor-only mode — tuned but not blocking? Is the bottleneck actually the database behind the app, where no WAF rule can help? And check the boring one: did autoscaling actually trigger, or did a quota cap it? The meta-lesson: "behind a WAF" is not a layer analysis. Name the layer, name the resource being exhausted, then pick the control.

8. Try it

Security posture of your own Mac — read-only commands.

# The macOS application firewall: which apps have inbound exceptions.
# This is your laptop's own allowlist — read it like an auditor.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
sudo /usr/libexec/ApplicationFirewall/socketfilterfw --listapps

# Global state: is the firewall even on?
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
sudo /usr/libexec/ApplicationFirewall/socketfilterfw --getglobalstate

# Listening services — your local attack surface. Every line is a
# port someone on your network could knock on.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
lsof -iTCP -sTCP:LISTEN -P -n

# Who's on your local segment right now (ARP table) — the population
# of your broadcast domain, the thing VLANs would subdivide.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
arp -an | head -20

And a thought exercise, no command needed: pick your Mac as the "compromised host." List everything it can initiate a connection to — your LAN, the internet, your office VPN. Now ask which of those are necessary. That gap, between reachable and necessary, is the blast radius you would close with segmentation. It is the same exercise, scaled up, that enterprise architects run on data centers. Do it once for your home network and the abstraction stops being abstract.

Key terms: defense in depth, assume breach, segmentation, VLAN (IEEE 802.1Q), firewall zone, DMZ, microsegmentation, zero trust (NIST SP 800-207), identity-aware access, workload identity, least privilege, IDS/IPS, WAF, EDR, SYN flood (RFC 4987), SYN cookies, UDP amplification, BCP 38, scrubbing, anycast, L7 flood, slowloris, RFC 4732.

Module 18 — Troubleshooting Like a Pro

The capstone: a methodology for finding the broken layer, and one fully worked outage.

1. Why it matters

Everything in this guide converges here. Addressing, ARP, DHCP, DNS, routing, NAT, firewalls, VPNs — troubleshooting is what all of it is for. In interviews, "walk me through how you'd debug X" is the closest thing to a practical exam. The interviewer is not testing whether you know the commands. They are testing whether you have a method — whether you narrow the problem systematically instead of guessing.

On the job, the difference is starker. A methodical troubleshooter resolves in twenty minutes what costs a guesser four hours and two wrong config changes. And every wrong change is a new variable. The first rule of troubleshooting is the Hippocratic one: change nothing until you understand something.

This module teaches two methodologies, when each wins, and then walks one complete scenario — "can't reach the website" — from symptom to root cause, with the exact command at every step.

2. The mental model

Think of the network stack as a chain of dependencies. The application depends on TCP. TCP depends on IP routing. Routing depends on ARP and the link. DNS depends on UDP reaching a resolver. Every layer assumes the ones below it work.

That gives you the fundamental move: a failure at layer N is usually caused at layer N or below, never above. If DNS fails, the problem could be DNS itself — or UDP, or routing, or the link. It cannot be caused by the web application. This one rule eliminates half the possibilities at every step.

The corollary: test from the bottom when you are lost, and test the middle when you are in a hurry. Those are the two methodologies. Both are just ways of exploiting the dependency chain.

3. How it works

Two methodologies

Bottom-up: start at Layer 1, work up. Check the link. Then the local address. Then ARP and the gateway. Then routing beyond. Then DNS. Then the port. Then the application. You test each layer in order and stop at the first one that fails — the break is there or directly beneath it.

Bottom-up wins when you have no hypothesis: an unfamiliar system, a vague symptom ("the network is slow"), or any hint of physical trouble. It is slow and thorough. It never skips the layer that turns out to be the problem, which is its virtue and its cost.

Split the stack: bisect. Pick a test in the middle of the stack and run it first. The classic: ping the gateway. If it works, everything at L1–L3 locally is fine — the problem is above (DNS, firewall, app). If it fails, the problem is at or below L3 — forget DNS and the app entirely. You just eliminated half the stack with one command. Then bisect the broken half: if the gateway pings but the internet doesn't, the problem is between your gateway and the destination — test DNS next, then the route, then the port.

Split-the-stack wins when you have a hypothesis or a clock: production is down, the symptom is specific ("this one site fails"), or you already suspect a layer. It is fast because every test eliminates half the remaining possibilities. Its risk is a wrong first test — bisecting at a layer whose test is itself broken (ping blocked by a firewall, for example) sends you down the wrong half.

A third move belongs in your pocket: follow the packet. When you know the path — client to load balancer to app to database — check each hop in order with traceroute (network path) or logs (application path). It is bottom-up applied to a specific conversation rather than the whole stack. Senior engineers do this instinctively: they pick the failing request and walk it.

And the meta-rule that governs all three: one variable at a time. Form a hypothesis, run the test that would disprove it, observe, update. Never change two things between tests. The number of outages extended by "well, I changed the DNS and rebooted the router and now it's different" is uncountable.

Time-boxing: know when to escalate

Methodology needs a deadline. Set one before you start: fifteen minutes for a hunch, an hour for a real dig. When the box expires, you escalate — with everything you've gathered, using the handoff format in section 8. Time-boxing is not giving up. It is how you avoid the two failure modes of smart troubleshooters: digging a rabbit hole past the point of usefulness, and sitting on an outage out of pride while users wait.

The rule of thumb: escalate when you've identified the layer and the owner but can't reach the fix — the break is in the ISP, the cloud provider, the vendor appliance. Your job then is the handoff, not the repair. And loop back afterward: read the vendor's postmortem, learn what their tools showed that yours couldn't. Every escalation you write well makes the next one shorter.

The dependency ladder

Here is the stack as a checklist. Each rung depends on every rung below it. Next to each rung is the test that proves it — the same tests the worked scenario will use:

4. The exact structure: the decision procedure

Before the scenario, the procedure in words. At each rung, run the test. If it passes, climb. If it fails, stop — the fault is at this rung or below, and the rung's own diagnostics tell you which:

  1. Scope it first. Is it just you or everyone? Just this site or all sites? Just Wi-Fi or also wired? Two answers here can skip the entire ladder — "everyone can't reach it" means the problem is not your laptop.
  2. L1: link and address. Interface active? Do you have an IP? No IP means DHCP failed or the link is down — nothing above this can work.
  3. L2/L3 local: the gateway. Ping the default gateway. This is the single most informative test: it proves your link, your ARP, your IP config, and the first hop all at once.
  4. L3 beyond: the internet. Ping a public IP by number (8.8.8.8). This tests routing and NAT without involving DNS. If the gateway pings but this doesn't, the problem is past your router.
  5. DNS. Resolve the name. If steps 1–4 pass and this fails, it is DNS — the famous answer, but now it's a diagnosis, not a guess.
  6. L4: the port. Can you open TCP to the destination port? This separates "host unreachable" from "port blocked" from "service down."
  7. L7: the application. Talk to the service and read its response. Status codes, TLS errors, and timeouts each point somewhere different.

Write down what you observe at each step. "Ping gateway: 3/3 replies, 2ms" beats "ping works" when you escalate — and you will escalate, because some breaks live past your control.

The failure-signature reference

Every failure has a signature — a specific way it looks that points at a specific cause. Memorize this table and half of all outages become pattern-matching:

Signature                          Meaning
─────────────────────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────────
ping: 100% loss to gateway         L1/L2 local — link, ARP, Wi-Fi, VLAN
ping gateway OK, 8.8.8.8 fails     past your router — ISP, upstream, WAN
dig: no answer / SERVFAIL          DNS — resolver down, port 53 filtered
dig: NXDOMAIN                      name doesn't exist — typo or DNS record
nc: connection refused (RST)       reached the host — port closed / down
nc: timeout (silence)              firewall dropping, or dead route
curl: hangs after "Connected"      app accepted TCP but won't answer
curl: TLS certificate error        expired/cert mismatch — or TLS interception
curl: HTTP 403                     app/WAF refusing you — policy, not network
curl: HTTP 5xx                     server-side failure — app owner's problem
traceroute dies at hop N            everything past hop N is someone else's
works on cellular, not on Wi-Fi    your LAN/ISP/DNS — the delta is the fault
fast on -4, hangs on -6            broken IPv6 path with fallback delay

Two rows deserve emphasis because they get confused constantly. Refused is an answer from a live host: the network did its job, the service didn't. Timeout is silence: something between you and the service swallowed the packets, or nothing is there at all. And NXDOMAIN is DNS telling you the truth — the name really doesn't exist — which is different from DNS being broken. Read the signature before theorizing.

Tools of the trade: which question each answers

Every tool below answers exactly one question. Reach for the tool whose question matches yours:

Tool                  Question it answers
────────────────────  ─────────────────────────────────────────────
ping                  can ICMP reach it? (L3 alive?)
traceroute / mtr      which hops does the path take, and where does
                      latency appear? (mtr combines ping + traceroute,
                      and it's the better tool — install via Homebrew)
dig / nslookup        what does DNS say? (use +short; test resolvers
                      directly with @server)
