arp -an               who is on my local segment? (L2 reality check)
netstat -rn           where does my machine send things? (routing table)
nc -vz host port      does TCP connect? (refused vs timeout)
curl -v               what does the app say? (TLS, headers, status)
tcpdump -i iface      what actually crossed the wire? (the arbiter)
lsof -i               what is MY machine listening on / connected to?
scutil --dns          which resolvers is macOS actually using?

Notice what's missing: no tool here tells you why. Tools report observations. The methodology turns observations into a location, and the location into an owner. Juniors collect tool output. Seniors collect answers.

5. A realistic example: "can't reach the website"

The scenario: your browser says it cannot reach https://example.com. (We use example.com deliberately — it's reserved for documentation in RFC 2606, so it harms nothing.) Work it like a split-the-stack troubleshooter with a hypothesis — the symptom is specific — but verify each rung.

Step 0 — Scope. Before any command: is it just this site? Try a second site. Is it just this device? Check your phone (on cellular, not Wi-Fi — otherwise you're testing the same network twice). Suppose: all sites fail, phone on cellular works. The problem is your network or your Mac, not the internet. Proceed.

Step 1 — L1: is the link up and addressed?

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
ifconfig en0 | grep -E 'status|inet '

Good looks like status: active and an inet 192.168.1.x line. If there's no inet line, you have no address — DHCP failed or the link is down. Check Wi-Fi is associated, or try ipconfig getpacket en0 to see the DHCP exchange. If the interface shows no status: active, stop: it's physical. Cable, Wi-Fi association, airplane mode. Nothing above this matters yet.

Step 2 — L2/L3: can you reach the gateway?

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
netstat -rn | grep default
ping -c 3 192.168.1.1   # use the gateway address from the line above

Three replies means your link, ARP, IP config, and the router's LAN side all work. This is the bisect point: everything below L3-local is proven. If it fails, look at arp -an | grep 192.168.1.1 — an (incomplete) entry means ARP isn't resolving: wrong VLAN, client isolation on the Wi-Fi, or the gateway's ARP is broken. That's a Layer-2 answer, and you found it without touching DNS.

Step 3 — L3 beyond: can you reach the internet by number?

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
ping -c 3 8.8.8.8

By number, deliberately — no DNS involved. Replies mean routing and NAT work. Failure here with Step 2 passing means the break is past your router: ISP outage, upstream routing, or your router's WAN side. traceroute 8.8.8.8 shows where packets die — the last responding hop is the edge of your visibility, and everything past it is your ISP's problem to hear about with evidence.

Step 4 — DNS: does the name resolve?

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
dig example.com +short

An IP address back means DNS works. No answer means: check which resolvers you're using (scutil --dns | grep nameserver), then test one directly: dig @8.8.8.8 example.com +short. If the direct query works but the default doesn't, your configured resolver is broken — the classic "it's DNS," now proven. If neither works but Step 3 passed, UDP port 53 might be filtered, or a captive portal is intercepting you (hotels and airports love this one).

Step 5 — L4: is the port reachable?

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
nc -vz example.com 443

Three possible outcomes, each a different diagnosis. Connected — TCP works, climb to the app. Connection refused — you reached the host but nothing listens on 443: wrong port, service down, or you resolved a different host than you think. Timeout — packets vanish: a firewall is dropping them (yours, theirs, or the hotel's), or routing is broken asymmetrically. Timeout vs refused is the most useful distinction in networking: refused is an answer, timeout is silence, and they mean different things.

Step 6 — L7: what does the application say?

# run on your Mac — your output will differ
curl -sv --max-time 15 https://example.com -o /dev/null

Read the response like a log. HTTP/1.1 200 — the site works; your browser is the problem (cache, extension, proxy setting). 403/500 — the app or its WAF is refusing you; that's an application problem, not a network one. A TLS error — certificate expired, hostname mismatch, or a middlebox intercepting TLS (corporate proxies do this deliberately). A hang after Connected — the server accepts TCP but never answers: overloaded app, or a firewall doing silent drop after the handshake.

The decision tree you just walked:

can't reach the site
├── everyone affected? → not your machine; check the service status
├── link up + addressed? (ifconfig) → no: physical / DHCP
├── gateway pings? (ping) → no: L2 — ARP, VLAN, Wi-Fi
├── 8.8.8.8 pings? (ping) → no: past your router — ISP/upstream
├── name resolves? (dig) → no: DNS — resolver, port 53, captive portal
├── port connects? (nc) → refused: service down · timeout: firewall/routing
└── app answers? (curl) → status code tells you whose problem it is

Six commands, six rungs, one root cause. That is the whole craft: each test has exactly two outcomes, and each outcome names the next test. When you can walk this tree without notes, you troubleshoot like a professional.

Saying it out loud: the interview version

In an interview you won't have a terminal. You'll have a whiteboard and sixty seconds. Practice this as a spoken answer, not a memorized list: "First I'd scope it — me or everyone, this site or all sites. Then I'd walk the stack: link and address, ping the gateway, ping a public IP by number to rule out DNS, then dig, then test the TCP port — refused versus timeout tells me service-down versus firewall — then curl and read the status code. Each test eliminates a layer." That's the whole scenario in five sentences. The interviewer will then pick a rung — "say the port times out, what next?" — and you're in a conversation, not a recitation. That's the point where interviews are won: not knowing the commands, but showing the method survives contact with a follow-up.

When the ladder lies: the tricky cases

The ladder is honest, but the network sometimes cheats. Four cases that fool even experienced engineers:

Broken IPv6 with working IPv4. The symptom: curl hangs for twenty seconds, then loads fine. What happens: the resolver returns both AAAA and A records; the client tries IPv6 first (as it should), the IPv6 path is broken (tunnel misconfigured, firewall drops it silently), and after a long timeout it falls back to IPv4. Everything "works," just slowly. Diagnose with curl -4 vs curl -6: if -4 is instant and -6 hangs, the IPv6 path is the problem. The fix is repairing IPv6 or, as a stopgap, preferring IPv4 — but name it as a stopgap, because the broken path is still broken.

The captive portal. Hotel and airport Wi-Fi hijack your traffic until you log in. The symptom is bizarre: DNS resolves (to the portal), ping works, but every site shows the login page — or TLS errors everywhere, because the portal's certificate doesn't match. Diagnose: curl -v http://example.com (plain HTTP, so you can see the redirect) shows a 302 to some login page. Fix: open the portal page and authenticate. The ladder didn't lie — DNS and L3 genuinely worked. The network was just answering with someone else's server.

Proxy environment variables. http_proxy/https_proxy set in your shell (or a corporate PAC file) reroute traffic invisibly. The symptom: curl fails but the browser works, or vice versa — because they read different proxy configs. Diagnose: env | grep -i proxy and compare with the browser's settings. This one wastes hours because nobody suspects the environment.

The VPN that half-works. Connected to the office VPN, some sites load and others hang. Usually split tunneling plus DNS: office hostnames resolve through the VPN's DNS, public names through the local one, and one of the two paths is broken — or the VPN client installed a default route that sends everything through a congested concentrator. Diagnose: netstat -rn | grep utun (Module 16's command) to see what actually routes through the tunnel, and scutil --dns to see which resolver answers which domain. The VPN didn't break the network. It rearranged it, and the map changed.

6. Misconceptions & common mistakes

"It's always DNS." It frequently is — which is why the joke survives — but "frequently" is not "always," and guessing DNS first skips the four rungs below it. The procedure above proves it's DNS at Step 4 by showing everything beneath works. Earn the joke.

"Ping works, so the network is fine." Ping is ICMP. Firewalls treat ICMP and TCP differently all the time — many block ping entirely while passing web traffic, and some pass ping while blocking TCP ports. "Ping works" proves L3 reachability for ICMP. It says nothing about TCP 443. Test the actual protocol with the actual port.

"Traceroute stars mean the path is broken." Stars mean a hop didn't answer your probe — usually because it rate-limits or drops ICMP. The path can be perfectly healthy past a silent hop. Traceroute maps routers, not link quality. Read the last hop and the latency trend, not the stars.

Changing two things at once. You flushed the DNS cache and rebooted the router and it works now. What fixed it? You don't know, and it will break again. One change, one test, one observation. This discipline feels slow. It is the fastest way.

Testing from one vantage point. Your laptop says the site is down; your phone on cellular says it's up. That's not a contradiction — it's data: the difference between the two paths (your Wi-Fi, your ISP, your DNS) contains the fault. Always get a second vantage point before concluding.

Forgetting the return path. You prove packets leave. Do they come back? Asymmetric routing, a firewall that allows outbound but not the replies, NAT without a mapping — all produce "requests go out, nothing returns." tcpdump on the interface is the final arbiter: if you see requests leave and no replies arrive, the problem is downstream or the return path. Packets don't lie; everything else is interpretation.

Blaming the wrong layer's owner. "The app is broken" when the firewall drops the port. "The network is broken" when the app returns 500. The ladder exists to assign blame correctly, because the fix lives with the owner. Step 5's refused-vs-timeout distinction and Step 6's status codes are blame-assignment tools. Use them before opening the ticket, and put the evidence in the ticket.

Rebooting as diagnosis. The reboot fixed it, so the reboot was the fix. No — the reboot cleared the state that contained the evidence. DHCP renewed, ARP flushed, conntrack tables emptied, the leaking socket closed. It will happen again, and you'll know less next time. Reboot last, after you've captured the state: the ARP table, the routing table, the firewall counters, the app logs. The evidence is perishable. The reboot destroys it.

"It worked yesterday" as a theory. Something changed. Configs, certs, routes, DNS TTLs expiring onto new values, a deploy, a certificate rotation at 3 AM. "Nothing changed" is never true in a running system — it just means the change wasn't announced. Ask what could have changed on a schedule: cron jobs, cert renewals, DHCP lease times, BGP announcements. Time-correlated failures have time-correlated causes.

7. Interview Q&A

Q1: Walk me through how you'd debug "I can't reach the website."

Scope first: just me or everyone, just this site or all sites — that decides whether it's my machine, my network, or the service. Then work the ladder: link and address (ifconfig, ipconfig getifaddr), gateway (ping — the bisect point), internet by number (ping 8.8.8.8 — no DNS involved), DNS (dig), the port (nc -vz host 443 — refused vs timeout tells me service-down vs firewall), then the app (curl -v — status codes and TLS errors). Each test has two outcomes and each outcome names the next test. I change nothing until a test identifies the layer, and I write down observations for the escalation.

Q2: Ping to the server works, but curl hangs. What's your next step?

Ping proves ICMP reachability — nothing about TCP. My next test is the port: nc -vz server 443 or curl -v with timing. If TCP connects but the request hangs, the candidates are: a firewall doing silent drop after the handshake, an overloaded application accepting connections it can't serve, or an MTU problem (large packets dropped, small ones fine — the classic "small pages load, downloads stall"). I'd check curl -v to see exactly where it stalls (after connect? during TLS? waiting for response?), and try a tiny request vs a large one to test the MTU hypothesis. Ping working was never evidence the app was reachable.

Q3: DNS resolves, but the connection times out. Layer analysis?

DNS resolving proves UDP to the resolver works and the name exists — DNS is innocent. A TCP timeout after successful resolution means SYN packets go out and nothing comes back: the port is filtered (firewall dropping silently), routing is broken asymmetrically, or the server is firewalled off. Contrast with connection refused, which means I reached the host and it actively rejected me — service down or wrong port. So: timeout → suspect the path and firewalls, verify with traceroute and by testing the port from a second vantage point. And check my own outbound firewall too — local pf rules and corporate proxies cause this exact symptom.

Q4: How do you distinguish "firewall is blocking me" from "the server is down"?

By the failure's signature. Connection refused (TCP RST) means my SYN reached a host that answered — the network path works, nothing listens on that port: server down, wrong port, or service not started. Timeout (no answer at all) means something swallowed the SYN: a firewall dropping silently, or a dead route. ICMP administratively-prohibited means a firewall rejected me explicitly — the most honest answer, and the rarest. The definitive test is tcpdump: if I see my SYN leave and a RST come back, the server is up and refusing. SYN leaves, nothing returns: firewall or routing. I trust the packet capture over every theory.

Q5: Traceroute shows stars in the middle but reaches the destination. Problem?

No — that's normal. Stars mean those hops didn't respond to the probe, usually because routers rate-limit or deprioritize ICMP TTL-expired messages. The path is proven by the final hop responding. What matters in traceroute is the last responding hop (the edge of your visibility when it stops early) and sudden latency jumps between consecutive hops (a real bottleneck). Beginners read stars as damage. Professionals read the endpoint and the deltas.

8. Try it

A guided lab. Break nothing — this is all read-only. Walk the ladder on your own Mac and write down what each rung says.

# Rung 1 — link and address.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
ifconfig en0 | grep -E 'status|inet '

# Rung 2 — the gateway (your bisect point).
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
netstat -rn | grep default

# Rung 3 — ping it. 0% loss is the only acceptable score here.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
ping -c 3 192.168.1.1   # replace with YOUR gateway from the line above

# Rung 4 — internet by number, no DNS involved.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
ping -c 3 8.8.8.8

# Rung 5 — DNS.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
dig example.com +short
scutil --dns | grep nameserver | head -5

# Rung 6 — the port. Watch for the refused-vs-timeout distinction.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
nc -vz example.com 443

# Rung 7 — the application. Read the first HTTP line like a diagnosis.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' --max-time 15 https://example.com

Now the exercise that builds the instinct. Pick a site you use daily and run the ladder against it, writing one line per rung: what you ran, what good looks like, what you actually saw. Then break it on purpose — turn on airplane mode and run rung 1; disconnect Wi-Fi and run rung 2; set a bogus DNS server (networksetup -setdnsservers Wi-Fi 192.0.2.1 — TEST-NET-1, guaranteed dead — then set it back with networksetup -setdnsservers Wi-Fi Empty) and run rung 5. Each break produces exactly the symptom its rung predicts. After three deliberate breaks, the ladder stops being a diagram and becomes reflex.

One caution on that DNS experiment: 192.0.2.1 is documentation-only address space (RFC 5737) — nothing will answer, which is the point — and Empty restores DHCP-provided DNS. If anything feels off, rebooting the Mac restores everything: these are all runtime settings.

Finally, the arbiter exercise. When two theories disagree — "the firewall blocks me" vs "the server is down" — stop theorizing and watch the wire:

# Watch your own SYN leave and see what (if anything) comes back.
# Ctrl-C to stop. Filter to one destination so the output stays readable.
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
sudo tcpdump -i en0 -n host example.com and port 443
# in another terminal:
# run on your Mac — your output will differ
curl --max-time 10 https://example.com -o /dev/null

Read the capture. SYN out, SYN-ACK back, then a hang: the app. SYN out, RST back: refused, service down. SYN out, nothing back: firewall or routing. Three runs of this and you'll never again argue about whose layer is broken — you'll show them the packets.

Escalation: handing over a break you can't fix

Some breaks live past your control: inside the ISP, inside the cloud provider, inside the vendor's appliance. Escalation is not surrender. It is a handoff, and a good handoff gets fixed faster than a vague one. Include exactly this:

  1. The symptom, scoped: "all hosts in 10.0.4.0/24 can't reach 203.0.113.9:443 since 14:20; hosts in 10.0.5.0/24 can." Scope tells them where to look.
  2. The rung where it breaks: "gateway pings, 8.8.8.8 pings, but traceroute to 203.0.113.9 dies at hop 6 (their edge router)." You've done their triage for them.
  3. The evidence: paste the actual command output — traceroute, tcpdump excerpt, timestamps. "Packets don't lie" applies to vendors too.
  4. What you ruled out: "DNS resolves, port is refused-not-timeout from the working subnet, no local firewall changes today." This stops them from asking you to reboot.
  5. Impact and urgency: how many users, what business function, any workaround in place.

The template fits in a ticket or a phone call. The team that receives "traceroute dies at your hop 6 since 14:20, here's the output" fixes it in one touch. The team that receives "the network is down, please advise" opens a two-day thread. Write the handoff you'd want to receive.

Key terms: bottom-up troubleshooting, split-the-stack (bisect), dependency chain, vantage point, refused vs timeout, ICMP vs TCP, traceroute stars, tcpdump as arbiter, one variable at a time, blame assignment, RFC 2606 (example.com), RFC 5737 (documentation addresses).

Module 19 — Back Matter

Reference material for the whole guide: the glossary, the subnetting cheat sheet, the ports table, and the troubleshooting flowchart.

Glossary

Every key term in the guide, in one place. Terms are defined as this guide uses them.

5-tuple — the five values that identify a connection: source IP, source port, destination IP, destination port, protocol.

AH (Authentication Header) — IPsec protocol that authenticates packets without encrypting them; does not survive NAT (RFC 4302).

UDP amplification — attack trick: a small request with a forged source address triggers a large response aimed at the victim.

anycast — one address announced from many locations; routing delivers to the nearest. Absorbs DDoS and speeds up DNS and CDNs.

APIPA — Automatic Private IP Addressing: the 169.254.0.0/16 address a host gives itself when DHCP fails (RFC 3927).

ARP — Address Resolution Protocol: resolves an IPv4 address to a MAC address on the local link (RFC 826).

AS (autonomous system) — a network under single administration, identified by an ASN; BGP routes between autonomous systems.

ASN — Autonomous System Number: the identifier of an AS in internet routing.

asymmetric routing — forward and return packets taking different paths; breaks stateful devices that see only one direction.

BCP 38 — best current practice: providers must filter packets with spoofed source addresses at their network edge (RFC 2827, RFC 3704).

BGP — Border Gateway Protocol: the routing protocol between autonomous systems; how the internet backbone works (RFC 4271).

bandwidth — the maximum data rate of a link. Distinct from latency (delay) and throughput (achieved rate).

broadcast — one-to-all delivery within a broadcast domain. Routers do not forward broadcasts.

broadcast domain — the set of hosts that receive each other's broadcasts. A VLAN is one broadcast domain.

CIDR — Classless Inter-Domain Routing: prefix notation like 10.0.0.0/8 (RFC 4632).

CGNAT / CGN — carrier-grade NAT: ISP-level address translation using the shared space 100.64.0.0/10 (RFC 6598).

checksum — a field used to detect corrupted data; carried by IP, TCP, and UDP headers.

congestion control — TCP's algorithms (slow start, congestion avoidance) that match send rate to what the network can carry.

connection tracking (conntrack) — the kernel's table of live connections; the engine behind stateful firewalls and NAT.

DDoS — distributed denial of service: many sources overwhelming a target with traffic.

default gateway — the router a host uses for destinations outside its own subnet.

default route — 0.0.0.0/0: matches every destination not matched more specifically.

DHCP — Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol: assigns IP address, mask, gateway, and DNS to hosts (RFC 2131).

DHCP snooping — a switch feature that trusts DHCP replies only from approved ports; defeats rogue DHCP servers.

DMZ — demilitarized zone: a segment holding public-facing servers, isolated from both the internet and the internal LAN.

DNS — Domain Name System: resolves names to addresses (RFC 1035).

DNSSEC — cryptographic signatures on DNS data, defending against cache poisoning (RFC 4033–4035).

DoH / DoT — DNS over HTTPS (RFC 8484) / DNS over TLS (RFC 7858): encrypts DNS queries.

DSCP — Differentiated Services Code Point: IP header bits marking a packet's priority for QoS (RFC 2474).

duplex — full duplex sends both directions at once; half duplex takes turns. A mismatch causes classic slowness.

ECN — Explicit Congestion Notification: routers mark packets instead of dropping them to signal congestion (RFC 3168).

egress / ingress — traffic leaving / entering a network. Firewall rules usually differ by direction.

encapsulation — wrapping one packet or protocol inside another. The basis of layering, tunnels, and VPNs.

ESP — Encapsulating Security Payload: IPsec's encryption and authentication for the data path (RFC 4303).

Ethernet — the wired LAN standard; frames addressed by MAC address.

firewall — a device or software enforcing allow/deny policy on traffic.

firewall zone — a policy grouping of interfaces (inside, outside, DMZ) with explicit rules between zones.

flow — packets sharing one 5-tuple over time; the unit stateful devices track.

forward secrecy — the property that session keys stay safe even if long-term keys are compromised later.

fragmentation — splitting an oversized packet into pieces; avoided with path MTU discovery.

full tunnel — a VPN configuration routing all traffic through the tunnel (opposite: split tunnel).

gateway — a router joining networks; also the default next hop for off-subnet traffic.

GRE — Generic Routing Encapsulation: simple IP-in-IP tunneling, no encryption (RFC 2784).

half-open connection — SYN received and SYN-ACK sent, final ACK never arrived. SYN floods exploit these.

handshake — the opening exchange of a protocol: TCP's 3-way (SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK); TLS, IKEv2, and WireGuard have their own.

HTTP/2, HTTP/3 — web protocols; HTTP/3 runs over QUIC on UDP (RFC 9113, RFC 9114; QUIC is RFC 9000).

hub — a Layer-1 repeater: every port hears everything. Replaced by switches.

ICMP — Internet Control Message Protocol: ping, error messages, and traceroute's mechanics (RFC 792).

IDS / IPS — intrusion detection (watches and alerts) vs intrusion prevention (sits inline and blocks).

IKEv2 — Internet Key Exchange v2: IPsec's handshake protocol (RFC 7296).

interface — a host's attachment point to a network; has a MAC address and usually IP addresses.

IP — Internet Protocol: addressing and forwarding. IPv4 is RFC 791; IPv6 is RFC 8200.

IPsec — the framework for IP-layer security: ESP, AH, and IKEv2 (RFC 4301).

IPv4 exhaustion — depletion of the 32-bit address pool; the reason NAT exists and IPv6 is needed.

jitter — variation in latency over time; destroys real-time voice and video quality.

keepalive — periodic packets that keep a connection or NAT mapping from timing out.

L2TP — Layer 2 Tunneling Protocol; commonly paired with IPsec for remote access (RFC 2661).

latency — the time for data to travel. Distinct from bandwidth (capacity).

link-local — addresses valid only on one link: IPv4 169.254.0.0/16, IPv6 fe80::/10.

load balancer — distributes traffic across backends; Layer-4 vs Layer-7 balancing.

loopback — 127.0.0.0/8: addresses a host uses to talk to itself.

MAC address — the 48-bit hardware address of an Ethernet or Wi-Fi interface.

MASQUERADE — the iptables term for PAT when the public address is dynamic.

microsegmentation — per-workload network policy; the policy follows the workload, not the wire.

MTU — maximum transmission unit: the largest packet a link carries (1500 on Ethernet). Tunnels shrink the effective MTU.

MSS — maximum segment size: the TCP option negotiated per connection; often clamped through tunnels.

multicast — one-to-many delivery to subscribed hosts (RFC 1112).

NAT — Network Address Translation: rewriting addresses between networks (RFC 3022).

NAT-T — NAT traversal for IPsec: ESP wrapped in UDP on port 4500 (RFC 3948).

NAPT / PAT — NAT with port translation: many private hosts sharing one public address.

netmask — the 32-bit mask splitting network bits from host bits; CIDR notation is the modern form.

next hop — the next router on the path toward a destination.

NTP — Network Time Protocol: clock synchronization (RFC 5905); abused in amplification attacks.

OSI model — the seven-layer reference model. TCP/IP is the model the internet actually runs.

OSPF — Open Shortest Path First: a link-state interior routing protocol (RFC 2328).

overlay network — a virtual network built on top of a physical one (VXLAN, tunnels).

packet — the unit of data at the network layer.

path MTU discovery — learning the smallest MTU along a path to avoid fragmentation (RFC 1191 for IPv4, RFC 8201 for IPv6).

payload — the data a packet carries, excluding headers.

port — the 16-bit transport-layer multiplexer, 0–65535 (RFC 6335).

port forwarding — a static NAT entry exposing an internal service to the internet.

prefix — the network portion of an address, written in CIDR notation.

promiscuous mode — a NIC capturing all traffic on the wire, not just its own; what sniffers use.

protocol — agreed rules for communication; also the IP header field naming the payload type.

proxy — an intermediary making requests on a client's behalf. Forward proxies serve clients; reverse proxies front servers.

QoS — quality of service: prioritizing latency-sensitive traffic over bulk traffic.

QUIC — a UDP-based transport with built-in TLS; underlies HTTP/3 (RFC 9000).

RADIUS — authentication protocol for network access (RFC 2865).

route — a forwarding-table entry mapping a destination prefix to a next hop or interface.

routing table — a router's map of where to send each destination.

RTT — round-trip time: the latency there and back again.

RST — TCP reset: "this connection is dead" or "nothing listens here."

segmentation (TCP) — splitting a byte stream into segments; also network segmentation: dividing a network into compartments.

SLAAC — IPv6 stateless address autoconfiguration (RFC 4862).

SNI — Server Name Indication: the TLS extension revealing the hostname; visible to observers on the path.

socket — an IP address plus port: one endpoint. A connection is two sockets.

split tunneling — a VPN configuration routing only selected traffic through the tunnel.

SSH — Secure Shell: encrypted remote login and tunneling (RFC 4251; port 22).

stateful inspection — a firewall judging packets using tracked connection state.

stateless filtering — a firewall judging each packet alone against fixed rules.

STP — Spanning Tree Protocol: prevents loops in switched networks (IEEE 802.1D).

subnet — a subdivided IP network; the unit of addressing plans.

switch — a Layer-2 device forwarding frames by MAC address table.

SYN flood — a storm of SYNs exhausting a server's connection backlog (mitigations: RFC 4987).

SYN cookie — encoding connection state in the SYN-ACK sequence number: stateless SYN-flood defense.

TCP — Transmission Control Protocol: the reliable, ordered byte stream (RFC 9293).

TCP Fast Open — sending data in the SYN to save a round trip (RFC 7413).

throughput — the data rate actually achieved; always ≤ bandwidth.

TLS — Transport Layer Security: encryption for the web, email, and TLS VPNs (TLS 1.3 is RFC 8446).

traceroute — path discovery by expiring TTLs hop by hop.

TTL — time to live: the hop count limiting a packet's lifetime.

tunnel — encapsulation carrying packets across an incompatible or untrusted network.

UDP — User Datagram Protocol: fast, unordered, connectionless (RFC 768).

unicast — one-to-one delivery; the normal case.

VLAN — virtual LAN: logical Layer-2 separation on shared switches (IEEE 802.1Q).

VPN — virtual private network: an encrypted tunnel making remote hosts look local.

VXLAN — a network virtualization overlay for data centers (RFC 7348).

WAN — wide area network: everything beyond the LAN.

WAF — web application firewall: inspects HTTP for attacks at Layer 7.

WireGuard — a minimal modern VPN protocol: 1-RTT handshake, UDP-only, static keys.

zero trust — the architecture of never trusting network location and always verifying identity (NIST SP 800-207).

Subnetting cheat sheet

CIDR /30 through /24 — the range Module 5 works in. Values verified with Python's ipaddress module (the same math as the Module 5 verifier).

CIDR Subnet mask Block size Usable hosts
/30 255.255.255.252 4 2
/29 255.255.255.248 8 6
/28 255.255.255.240 16 14
/27 255.255.255.224 32 30
/26 255.255.255.192 64 62
/25 255.255.255.128 128 126
/24 255.255.255.0 256 254

How to read it: usable hosts = block size − 2. The −2 is the network address (all host bits zero) and the broadcast address (all host bits one) — reserved in every subnet.

Two edge cases outside the table: /31 (RFC 3021) has 2 addresses and no network/broadcast reservation — used for point-to-point links between routers, where wasting two addresses per link is silly. /32 is a host route: one address, used in routing tables to pin a single destination.

The mental shortcut: each bit you take from the host portion halves the block. /24 → 256, /25 → 128, /26 → 64. Memorize the /24 row and halve downward; you'll never need the table again.

Common ports

The thirty a professional should know cold. Protocol column: the transport each service actually uses.

Port Protocol Service Why you care
20, 21 TCP FTP (data, control) Legacy file transfer; active mode breaks through NAT
22 TCP SSH Encrypted remote login; also tunnels and file transfer
23 TCP Telnet Unencrypted remote login — finding it open is a finding
25 TCP SMTP Mail server to mail server
53 TCP/UDP DNS Name resolution; UDP for queries, TCP for zone transfers
67, 68 UDP DHCP (server, client) Address assignment; 67 listens, 68 requests
80 TCP HTTP Unencrypted web
110 TCP POP3 Legacy mail retrieval
123 UDP NTP Clock sync; abused in amplification attacks
143 TCP IMAP Mail retrieval (modern)
161, 162 UDP SNMP (agent, trap) Network monitoring; 162 is traps from devices
389 TCP/UDP LDAP Directory services
443 TCP HTTPS Encrypted web; also where TLS VPNs hide
445 TCP SMB Windows file sharing; ransomware's favorite
514 UDP Syslog Log shipping (also runs over TCP/TLS now)
587 TCP SMTP submission Mail clients sending mail (authenticated)
636 TCP LDAPS LDAP over TLS
993 TCP IMAPS IMAP over TLS
1433 TCP Microsoft SQL Server Database; never expose to the internet
1521 TCP Oracle DB Database; never expose to the internet
3306 TCP MySQL/MariaDB Database; never expose to the internet
3389 TCP RDP Windows remote desktop; brute-forced constantly
5432 TCP PostgreSQL Database; never expose to the internet
5672 TCP AMQP (RabbitMQ) Message broker; the OrderFlow event bus
6379 TCP Redis Cache/store; ships with no auth by default — dangerous open
8080 TCP HTTP alternate Proxies, dev servers, app servers
8443 TCP HTTPS alternate Alt HTTPS; VPN portals and admin UIs live here

Three patterns to notice: databases (1433, 1521, 3306, 5432) are never internet-facing — every breach involving them starts with that rule broken. Mail has a port per era (25, 110/143, 587, 993) because each generation fixed the last one's security. And 443's row carries the VPN note: when everything else is blocked, tunnels hide in HTTPS.

Troubleshooting flowchart

Module 18's decision tree as a printable flowchart. Start at the top; each diamond's answer picks the next step.

Two reading notes. The DNS diamond is accented because it's the most skipped step — people test DNS before proving the network, then misread a network failure as a DNS failure. And the "port connects" diamond has three exits, not two: refused and timeout are different diagnoses, and the flowchart refuses to let you merge them.
